Taxation and public finance

Behavioural responses to income, wealth and audit policy, identified from reforms and experiments on full-population tax records.

Fertility and Family Labor Supply

Katrine Jakobsen, Thomas H. Jørgensen and Hamish Low · American Economic Review (forthcoming), 2026 · Individual

Uses the 2009 Danish tax reform to show that higher net-of-tax wages for women reduce fertility while higher wages for men raise it. A life-cycle model of couples choosing the timing and number of children, estimated on register data, implies that allowing fertility to adjust raises women's labour supply responsiveness substantially and that human capital depreciation around births drives much of the long-run gender wage gap.

Data
Monthly payroll records (BFL/eIndkomst), Annual income tax data (IND), Population and family register (BEF), family definitions and parent links, Education register (UDDA), Labour market experience from ATP payments, Parental leave spell register (OF)
Key variables
  • Outcome: fertility (births)
  • Treatment: 2009–2018 tax reform changes in marginal net-of-tax wage rate
  • Outcome: labour market participation, part-time and full-time work from pay-slip hours
  • Outcome: annual labour income
  • Control: education (high vs less skilled), labour market experience
Period
2004–2018 (tax sample 2004–2018; estimation sample 2010–2018)
Sample
10,851,444 individual-year observations (estimation sample); 2,531,181 (tax sample) Danish individuals aged 25–60 with an opposite-sex partner, not students, self-employed, retired or on disability pension; tax sample restricted to cohabiting or married women aged 25–40. source

Taxing the Wealth of the Poor: Evidence from the Danish Old-Age Support Asset Test

Niels Johannesen, Johan Sæverud and Emmanuel Saez · AER: Insights, 2026 · Individual

The asset test for Danish old-age support creates diffuse but large excess mass in liquid assets just below the threshold among the eligible, relative to comparable ineligible groups. Cohort analysis suggests the test reduces liquid assets by about 20 percent near the threshold, and high-frequency bank data show the responses are permanent rather than timed around the test date.

Data
Administrative registers on assets, income and demographics (Statistics Denmark): third-party reported and self-reported liquid wealth, Danske Bank customer data: monthly spending, cash withdrawals and account balances (2014–2016)
Key variables
  • Running variable: liquid wealth relative to the old-age check asset test threshold
  • Outcome: bunching / excess mass of liquid wealth below the threshold
  • Outcome: year-end spending and cash withdrawals (bank data)
  • Eligibility: age 65+ and low income
Period
2014–2018 (main cross-section); bank data 2014–2016
Sample
2,526,421 observations of singles aged 60+ (2,054,987 aged 65+; 1,283,721 low-income 65+), pooled 2014–2018; bank sample 461,471 observations, 2014–2016 Single individuals aged 60 and over in Denmark, with a linked Danske Bank subsample of about 20 percent of the elderly population. source

Micro versus Macro Labor Supply Elasticities: The Role of Dynamic Returns to Effort

Henrik Kleven, Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Kristian Larsen and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · American Economic Review, 2025 · Individual

Builds a model in which effort today raises earnings tomorrow, so responses to taxes are delayed and mediated by job switches. Danish administrative data confirm the model's life-cycle predictions for earnings and hours, and a quasi-experimental design based on job switchers finds a long-run earnings elasticity around 0.5, against a short-run elasticity of about 0.2.

Data
Linked administrative registers on earnings, occupation (DISCO), firm and demographics, full population, Employer-employee matched data (firm x occupation job cells), ATP pension contribution-based hours measure (1980–2018), Monthly uncapped hours measure (from 2008), Income tax records (2009 tax reform variation)
Key variables
  • Outcome: earnings (log earnings changes)
  • Treatment: 2009 tax reform change in marginal net-of-tax rate
  • Instrument: simulated net-of-tax rate change at baseline income
  • Mediator: job switches (firm x occupation cell), incl. mass-layoff switches
  • Outcome: hours worked
Period
1980–2018
Sample
N = 2,501,126 and 2,428,411 in the main elasticity regressions (Table 1) Full Danish population; tax-reform analysis uses taxpayers with baseline income between 250,000 and 1,000,000 DKK. source

Public Pensions and Private Savings

Esteban García-Miralles and Jonathan M. Leganza · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

A Danish reform raising the social security eligibility age, studied with a regression discontinuity in administrative data, increased contributions to personal and employer pension accounts when later eligibility kept people working longer. Inertia in previous savings behaviour and firms' default contribution rates are the key mechanisms.

Data
Population registers (date of birth, demographics), Labour-market and income/asset registers (tax-reported bank and stock balances, property values, pension contributions), Monthly earnings register (eIncome / eIndkomst), Voluntary early retirement pension (VERP) contribution and benefit records
Key variables
  • Running variable: birth date relative to 1 January 1954 cutoff
  • Treatment: higher pension eligibility ages from 2011 reform
  • Outcome: contributions to employer-sponsored and personal retirement accounts
  • Outcome: changes in bank and stock account balances and property values
  • Outcome: pre-tax earnings and monthly retirement age
Period
1985–2018 (annual); 2008–2017 (monthly)
Sample
40,042 individuals (analysis sample); 12,020 individuals (RD estimation sample) Non-self-employed Danes born within six months of 1 January 1954 who regularly contributed to the VERP early retirement scheme before the reform. source

New Evidence on Welfare's Disincentive for the Youth Using Administrative Panel Data

Olivier B. Bargain and Anders B. Jonassen · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

Maximum social assistance for childless unmarried Danes rose by 55 percent at age 25 between 2000 and 2006. Only the low-skilled respond: employment falls by 2 to 3 percent and benefit take-up rises by 10 to 14 percent, two thirds through moves from work to welfare, and crime shows no discontinuity.

Data
Danish administrative panel data on welfare benefit take-up, employment, earnings and crime (registers not named in abstract)
Key variables
  • Running variable: age relative to 25th birthday
  • Treatment: 55% increase in maximum social assistance at age 25 for childless unmarried individuals
  • Outcome: employment
  • Outcome: welfare benefit take-up
  • Outcome: criminal activity
Period
2000–2006
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Childless unmarried young people in Denmark around age 25, with effects concentrated among the low-skilled. source (abstract)

The Division of Parental Leave: Empirical Evidence and Policy Design

Thomas Høgholm Jørgensen and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses sharp incentives in the Danish parental leave benefit system for about 190,000 births to estimate parents' willingness to pay for leave. Parents bunch at kinks and are willing to pay for a traditional split with little leave for fathers, and simulations show that earmarked leave works much better when combined with high replacement rates.

Data
Parental leave benefit spell data (parents of children born 2008–2015), incl. benefits paid to parent or employer, Monthly earnings records for all employees (BFL/eIndkomst), from 2008, Danish administrative registers on demographics, family links and firm links (population and labour market registers)
Key variables
  • Outcome: weeks of parental leave taken by mother and father within three years of birth
  • Incentive: individual benefit replacement rates and benefit cap creating kinks in the household budget set
  • Incentive: employer-provided full wage compensation (from benefits paid to employer)
  • Policy: earmarked (father quota) leave and replacement rate counterfactuals in a structural model
Period
Births 2008–2015
Sample
189,974 births Births 2008–2015 to parents in stable employment and not on non-birth public benefits in the three months before birth, excluding births followed by another child within 15 months. source

Estimating Labor Supply Responses to Danish Tax Reforms

Hans Schytte Sigaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2023 · Individual

Applies taxable income elasticity methods to register data on hours actually worked. The intensive-margin labour supply elasticity is about 0.08, close to the labour income and taxable income elasticities, and it comes from changes in contract hours, paid absence and switches between main and secondary jobs.

Data
Wage Statistics Register (Lønstatistikken, LON) for performed hours of work, Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDAN) for workplace controls, Income registers (IND, INDK, INPI) for marginal tax rate simulation, Population register (BEF) and family income register (FAIN), Family register (FAMILIE), Education register (UDDA), Danish tax simulation programs (Kleven and Schultz 2014, extended by the Ministry of Finance)
Key variables
  • Outcome: three-year log change in annual performed hours of work
  • Treatment: three-year log change in the marginal net-of-tax rate on labour income
  • Instrument: mechanical (simulated) change in marginal tax rate from tax reforms, using lagged income
  • Secondary outcomes: labour income and taxable income
  • Controls: income splines, socio-economic and municipality controls
Period
2002–2016
Sample
10,755,733 observations Employed wage earners aged 25–65 covered by the Wage Statistics Register with positive hours in both years of each three-year difference and base-year labour income above DKK 250,000. source

The Disparate Effects of Information Provision: A Field Experiment on the Work Incentives of Social Welfare

Sofie Cairo and Robert Mahlstedt · Journal of Public Economics, 2023 · Individual

A field experiment in Denmark informed welfare recipients about a minimum work requirement, either with personalised running counts of their hours or with generic rules. Personalised information raised employment among those below the required hours, while generic notices reduced labour supply among those at low near-term risk of sanctions.

Data
Field experiment assignment and messaging data (Danish public employment service, jobnet.dk online tool), Population registers (socio-demographic background), Benefit payment records (DREAM), Monthly income and employment records (eIndkomst/BFL), Public employment service data on sanctions, exemptions, collected working hours, click data and registered job applications
Key variables
  • Treatment: random assignment to personalised online information tool about benefit-reduction risk under the work requirement (three arms)
  • Outcome: employment and earnings
  • Outcome: welfare benefit receipt and exit to alternative income support
  • Outcome: working hours collected toward the requirement and job applications
  • Heterogeneity: job search activity and sanction risk
Period
Intervention from 15 August 2018 with up to five monthly reminders
Sample
47,294 individuals All Danish welfare benefit recipients on 15 August 2018 subject to the 225-hour work requirement who had received the official notification letter. source

Constraints on Hours within the Firm

Claudio Labanca and Dario Pozzoli · Journal of Labor Economics, 2022 · Individual + firm

Uses Danish linked employer-employee data with hours and tax-rate variation from the 2010 Danish tax reform. Employees in firms with tight constraints on working hours do not adjust their hours when their taxes change, while employees in more flexible firms respond both directly and through spillovers from their coworkers.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Wage statistics with annual regular and overtime hours (Lønstatistikken, LON), Firm Statistics Register (Firmstat), Danish Foreign Trade Statistics Register, Firm-Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (FIDA), Danish Tax Register (income tax records), O*NET occupational data
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual hours worked (regular plus overtime)
  • Treatment: change in simulated marginal tax rate from the 2010 Danish tax reform (2008–2011 interval)
  • Moderator: firm-level hours constraints/coordination measured by within-firm dispersion of hours
  • Hourly wage: annual earnings over total hours
  • Spillovers: tax changes of coworkers
Period
2003–2011
Sample
more than 400,000 employees and approximately 8,300 firms (787,683 individual-year observations) Full-time employees aged 15–65 in private firms where hours are reported for at least 95% of the workforce, 2003–2011 (working-paper version 'Coordination of Hours within the Firm', IZA DP 12062). source

Identifying Behavioral Responses to Tax Reforms: New Insights and a New Approach

Katrine Jakobsen and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2022 · Individual

Shows that standard estimates of taxable income elasticities from tax reforms are biased by mean reversion and diverging income trends across the distribution, and proposes an approach that exploits reform timing to separate behavioural responses from these trends, applied to Danish tax reforms.

Data
Danish administrative registers for the full population since 1980 (labour market history, education, earnings, demographics), Third-party reported income tax records, Simulated marginal tax rates from an extended Danish tax simulator (Kleven and Schultz 2014 model)
Key variables
  • Outcome: changes in taxable income (broad, personal, labour income) over four-year periods
  • Treatment: predicted change in log net-of-tax rate from the 2004 and 2009–10 Danish tax reforms
  • Income-bin dummies capturing trend differentials across the income distribution
  • Validation: income trends in parts of the distribution unaffected by the reform
Period
Data since 1980; main analyses 1999–2012 (2004 reform: 1999–2003 pre-period, 2003–2007 reform period; 2009–10 reform: 2004–2008 pre-period, 2008–2012 reform period); 1987 and 1994 reforms in appendix
Sample
over 2,000,000 individuals per period (2,240,332 and 2,295,922 for the 2004 reform pre-reform and reform periods; 2,097,862 and 2,240,833 for the 2009–10 reform) Individuals present in the tax data with positive income over each four-year period and initial personal income above DKK 200,000 (2004 reform) or DKK 250,000 (2009–10 reform), about half the Danish adult population. source

Equilibrium Trade in Automobiles

Kenneth Gillingham, Fedor Iskhakov, Anders Munk-Nielsen, John Rust and Bertel Schjerning · Journal of Political Economy, 2022 · Individual

Develops and estimates a dynamic equilibrium model of the market for new and used cars with heterogeneous households on the universe of Danish car ownership records, which record every vehicle's owner, age and scrappage. The model is used to evaluate Denmark's very high registration taxes and fuel taxes, showing how tax changes propagate through used-car prices and the age distribution of the fleet.

Data
Car ownership register covering all privately owned cars (Statistics Denmark extract), Odometer readings from mandatory vehicle inspections (driving), Demographic and income registers for all Danish households, Tax deduction for commuting distance (work distance), Fuel prices from eof.dk
Key variables
  • Outcome: car ownership transitions (keep, trade, buy new, scrap)
  • Car types: four classes by weight and fuel efficiency, 25 age groups
  • Household types: couple/single, high/low income, high/low work distance
  • Outcome: kilometres driven
  • Policy variable: new car registration tax and fuel tax
Period
1996–2008 (estimation 1997–2008)
Sample
nearly 39 million observations on car ownership transitions All Danish households and all cars owned by private individuals, 1996–2008. source

Do People Respond to the Mortgage Interest Deduction? Quasi-Experimental Evidence from Denmark

Jonathan Gruber, Amalie Sofie Jensen and Henrik Kleven · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2021 · Individual

A major reform that scaled back the mortgage interest deduction for middle- and high-income households had a precisely estimated zero effect on home ownership. It did reduce the size and price of houses bought, lowered household indebtedness and pushed down equilibrium house prices.

Data
Income tax records (incomes, taxes, deductible interest expenses), Housing register (homeownership, property characteristics, home size), Tax authority property value appraisals (for imputed rent and wealth tax), Population registers with spouse/cohabitant and child links
Key variables
  • Policy: 1987 tax reform reducing the value of the mortgage interest deduction for top-rate taxpayers
  • Treatment: top-bracket taxpayers vs. below-top/middle/bottom groups (difference-in-differences)
  • Outcome: homeownership
  • Outcome: home size (square metres) and home value
  • Outcome: total deductible interest expenses (debt)
  • Elasticity with respect to the net-of-tax rate (1−τ)
Period
1980–2011 (main panel 1980–1996)
Sample
about 40 million individual-year observations; 34,440,869 observations in the main homeownership regression (below-top controls) Semi-balanced panel of Danish individuals aged 20–80 observed in every year 1980–1996, excluding children living with parents. source

Wealth Taxation and Wealth Accumulation: Theory and Evidence from Denmark

Katrine Jakobsen, Kristian Jakobsen, Henrik Kleven and Gabriel Zucman · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2020 · Individual

Exploits the 1989 Danish wealth tax reform, which cut rates sharply for the wealthy, to estimate long-run effects of wealth taxes on wealth accumulation. Responses are sizable for the very wealthy (top one percent) and small for the moderately wealthy, with effects that keep growing over a decade.

Data
Administrative wealth registry (third-party reported assets and debts, since 1980), Income registers and socio-economic registers (occupation, family composition)
Key variables
  • Outcome: log taxable wealth and third-party reported wealth
  • Treatment: 1989 wealth tax cut (unbound vs bound by tax ceiling: ceiling DD)
  • Treatment: doubling of couples' exemption threshold (couples DD)
  • Running variable for bunching: taxable wealth around exemption threshold
  • Controls: pre-reform portfolio shares and income deciles
Period
Wealth data since 1980; quasi-experimental panel 1985–1996
Sample
138,384 observations (ceiling DD) and 520,452 observations (couples DD, couples vs couples) Balanced panels of Danish households in the top 1% (ceiling DD) or married couples in the top 5% (couples DD) of the wealth distribution before the 1989 reform. source

The Welfare Magnet Hypothesis: Evidence from an Immigrant Welfare Scheme in Denmark

Ole Agersnap, Amalie Sofie Jensen and Henrik Kleven · AER: Insights, 2020 · Individual

The 2002 cut in benefits for non-EU immigrants, its repeal and its reintroduction provide repeated quasi-experiments on the welfare magnet hypothesis. The cut reduced net immigration by about 5,000 people a year and the repeal reversed it almost exactly, implying a migration elasticity with respect to benefits of 1.3.

Data
Danish administrative registers on the full population 1980–2017 (immigrant status, country of origin, demographics, family links), Residence permit data by permit type (asylum, family, work/study; available from 1997), Central Person Register (CPR) migration records, National statistics bureaus of other Nordic countries and OECD International Migration Database (1991–2017)
Key variables
  • Treatment: maximum monthly welfare benefit for non-EU immigrants (2002 introduction, 2012 repeal, 2015 reintroduction of the immigrant welfare scheme)
  • Outcome: net immigration flow by welfare benefit group, scaled by pre-scheme stock
  • Outcome: gross inflow by residence permit type
  • Comparison: EU vs non-EU immigrants; Denmark vs other Nordic countries
Period
1980–2017
Sample
444 group-year observations (12 welfare benefit groups) Immigrant flows to Denmark aged at least 30 (baseline), collapsed into 12 benefit-eligibility groups by EU/non-EU origin, marital status and number of children. source

Tax Evasion and Inequality

Annette Alstadsæter, Niels Johannesen and Gabriel Zucman · American Economic Review, 2019 · Individual

Matches leaked offshore account data (HSBC Switzerland, Panama Papers) and tax amnesty records to Scandinavian wealth registers. Tax evasion rises steeply with wealth: the top 0.01 percent evade about a quarter of their taxes, which materially raises measured top wealth shares.

Data
Leaked HSBC Switzerland client list (Swiss Leaks, 2007) matched to tax records in Denmark, Norway and Sweden, Panama Papers / Mossack Fonseca leak matched to Norwegian and Swedish wealth data (not Denmark), Tax amnesty voluntary disclosures in Norway and Sweden, Population-wide administrative wealth and income tax records for Norway, Sweden and Denmark, Danish stratified random tax audits by SKAT (tax years 2008, 2010, 2012), National accounts household wealth totals
Key variables
  • Outcome: taxes evaded (share of taxes owed) by wealth group
  • Ranking variable: household net wealth including imputed pension wealth
  • Indicator: owning an undeclared HSBC account / Panama Papers shell / amnesty participation
  • Outcome (event study): reported wealth and income after amnesty
  • Random-audit detected evasion (domestic evasion)
Period
HSBC leak 2006–2007; amnesties since 2006; Danish random audits for tax years 2008, 2010, 2012
Sample
520 HSBC households; 165 Panama Papers households; 1,422 amnesty taxpayers in Norway and 6,811 in Sweden; 18,985 randomly audited Danish taxpayers Scandinavian households appearing in offshore leaks or tax amnesties, ranked in the full population wealth distribution, combined with Danish random audits. source

Liquidity Constraint Tightness and Consumer Responses to Fiscal Stimulus Policy

Claus Thustrup Kreiner, David Dreyer Lassen and Søren Leth-Petersen · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2019 · Individual

The 2009 payout of Special Pension savings worked as a fiscal stimulus. Measuring constraint tightness by the marginal interest rate households pay, the spending response is highest for households facing high marginal rates and otherwise small, which supports targeting stimulus at the credit constrained.

Data
Telephone survey of SP account holders on spending of the 2009 SP payout (Epinion, January 2010), ATP register of Special Pension (SP) savings accounts (balances on 1 May 2009), Income tax register with incomes, assets and liabilities, 1998–2009, Account-level deposit and loan records with interest payments from the Danish Tax Agency (SKAT), 2007–2009, Demographic registers (Statistics Denmark)
Key variables
  • Outcome: self-reported share of the SP payout spent
  • Key regressor: household marginal interest rate in 2008 (from account-level loan and deposit data)
  • Policy: 2009 early release of Special Pension (SP) savings
  • Controls: payout size, income, liquid wealth, demographics
  • Validation: register-based measures of net savings and pension savings
Period
1998–2009 registers; account-level data 2007–2009; survey January 2010
Sample
5,037 completed interviews with respondents who took out the SP balance Random sample of Danish Special Pension account holders who withdrew their SP savings in 2009, linked to administrative registers. source

Labor Supply and Optimization Frictions: Evidence from the Danish Student Labor Market

Jakob Egholt Søgaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2019 · Individual

Danish students lose part of their study grant when annual earnings exceed a threshold. Register data show bunching below the threshold that grows as students gain experience with the rule, so optimisation frictions hide a structural elasticity well above the observed response.

Data
Danish administrative registers for the full population (education, earnings, benefits, demographics), Annual income tax returns, Monthly student benefit (SU) take-up records from the Student Benefit Administration, Monthly payroll records of earnings and hours (BFL/eIndkomst), from 2008
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual labour earnings relative to the student benefit income limit (bunching)
  • Incentive: kink/notch from phase-out (payback) of student grants above the individual income limit
  • Simulated individual marginal tax and phase-out rates
  • Monthly earnings and benefit take-up (timing of adjustment)
Period
2006–2011 (monthly earnings from 2008)
Sample
around 75,000 observations per year (core sample 75,500 individuals per year on average) Individuals aged 18–35 enrolled in tertiary education, fully eligible for student benefits and with positive labour earnings (above DKK 8,000 per year). source

Tax Reforms and Intertemporal Shifting of Wage Income: Evidence from Danish Monthly Payroll Records

Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Søren Leth-Petersen and Peer Ebbesen Skov · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2016 · Individual + firm

The 2010 cut in the top marginal tax rate was announced in advance, and monthly payroll data show top earners shifting wage income from December 2009 into January 2010. Shifting is concentrated among managers and employees of small firms, and it inflates short-run elasticity estimates.

Data
Monthly payroll records (eIndkomst / eIncome register) from the tax agency SKAT, Background registers on individuals and firms at Statistics Denmark (incl. firm size, liquid assets), Telephone survey on awareness of income-shifting opportunity (January 2011), merged to eIncome register
Key variables
  • Outcome: percentage change in monthly gross wage relative to 2008 average
  • Treatment: private-sector employees with 2008 monthly earnings above DKK 35,000 (top-tax incentive from 2010 reform)
  • Control group: monthly earnings DKK 30,000–35,000
  • Heterogeneity: firm size, liquid assets, earnings percentile
  • Elasticity of taxable income with and without shifting component
Period
January 2008–December 2011 (monthly)
Sample
328,679 individuals, of which 219,179 in the treatment group; survey sample of 878 taxpayers Private-sector employees with a wage record in every month of 2008 and positive income in all years, earning above DKK 30,000 per month in 2008. source

Do Employer Pension Contributions Reflect Employee Preferences? Evidence from a Retirement Savings Reform in Denmark

Itzik Fadlon, Jessica Laird and Torben Heien Nielsen · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2016 · Individual + firm

A reform that cut the subsidy to capital pension accounts for top-bracket workers led employers to reduce contributions to those accounts immediately, by more the larger the share of affected employees, and to shift the money to annuity accounts. Employer contributions track employees' savings preferences.

Data
Income tax register (incl. third-party reported pension contributions), Population register (BEF), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA)
Key variables
  • Outcome: employer contribution rate to capital and annuity pension accounts (median within workplace)
  • Treatment: share of workplace employees above the top income tax threshold
  • Policy variation: 1999 reform cutting the deduction for capital pension contributions for top-bracket taxpayers
  • Unit: workplace = 2-digit occupation × firm cell
  • Comparison: individual private (IRA-type) pension contributions
Period
1996–2001
Sample
2,020,705 worker-year observations; 84,764 workplace-year observations (26,775 unique workplaces) Private-sector white-collar wage earners aged 20–60 without self-employment income, in occupation-firm cells with at least five employees. source

Taxation and the Long Run Allocation of Labor: Theory and Danish Evidence

Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Jakob Roland Munch and Hans Jørgen Whitta-Jacobsen · Journal of Public Economics, 2015 · Individual + firm

Estimates a search model with non-linear taxation, in which workers keep looking for better-paid jobs, on employer-employee register data for all Danish workers and workplaces in 2004 to 2006. Danish taxes reduce total income by about 12 percent through the allocation of labour across jobs, and moving to low-tax OECD levels would recover four fifths of the loss.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Wage Statistics Register (Lønstatistikken) for hours-adjusted wages (robustness), Empirical effective marginal tax rate schedule for 2006 from Kleven and Kreiner (2006)
Key variables
  • Workplace average hourly wage rate (annual labour income / annual hours)
  • Workplace size, number of stayers and new hires (job-to-job flows)
  • New hires from non-employment
  • Effective marginal tax rates by income level (used to estimate structural search model and efficiency loss)
Period
2004–2006 (base year 2005)
Sample
126,400 workplaces employing 1.4 million workers Full population of private-sector workplaces in Denmark in 2005 and their workers aged 18–65, with wages between DKK 90 and DKK 714 per hour. source

Estimating Taxable Income Responses Using Danish Tax Reforms

Henrik Jacobsen Kleven and Esben Anton Schultz · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2014 · Individual

Uses a series of Danish tax reforms between 1984 and 2005 and full-population tax records to estimate the elasticity of taxable income. Elasticities are small for labour income (around 0.05) and larger for capital income and for top earners, and they are larger for big reforms than small ones.

Data
Income Tax Register (tax return information), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Authors' own tax simulator of the Danish tax system, 1984–2005
Key variables
  • Outcome: three-year changes in taxable labour income and capital income (positive and negative)
  • Treatment: change in simulated marginal net-of-tax rate
  • Instrument: mechanical (predicted) change in marginal tax rate from tax reforms
  • Controls: pre-reform (base year s−1) income levels
  • Key reforms: 1987 and 1994 Danish tax reforms
Period
Data since 1980; tax simulator and estimation 1984–2005 (three-year intervals 1984–1987 to 2002–2005)
Sample
about 37 million observations (stacked three-year differences; about 49 million before sample restrictions) Full Danish population aged 15–70 who are fully tax liable and whose base-year income is not primarily welfare benefits, 1984–2005. source

Migration and Wage Effects of Taxing Top Earners: Evidence from the Foreigners' Tax Scheme in Denmark

Henrik Jacobsen Kleven, Camille Landais, Emmanuel Saez and Esben Schultz · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2014 · Individual

The 1991 preferential tax scheme for high-earning foreigners roughly doubled the number of highly paid foreigners in Denmark. The implied elasticity of migration with respect to the net-of-tax rate is above one, far larger than domestic labour supply elasticities, and the scheme generated modest wage spillovers.

Data
Administrative tax and payroll records for all residents (income tax register), Civil registration (CPR) citizenship and migration records with dates of entry and exit, Foreigners' tax scheme beneficiary records (contract start and end dates), Employer links at firm and establishment level with industry
Key variables
  • Treatment: eligibility for the preferential foreigners' tax scheme (earnings above threshold)
  • Outcome: number of foreigners above vs below the earnings threshold (migration response)
  • Outcome: earnings of scheme beneficiaries (wage effects / bunching at the threshold)
  • Outcome: spillovers on native top earners' employment and earnings
  • Key variable: citizenship and years of presence in Denmark
Period
1980–2010 (tax data since 1980; scheme introduced 1992)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked All foreigners and natives resident in Denmark, focusing on foreigners with earnings around the scheme eligibility threshold (researchers excluded). source

Active vs. Passive Decisions and Crowd-Out in Retirement Savings Accounts: Evidence from Denmark

Raj Chetty, John N. Friedman, Søren Leth-Petersen, Torben Heien Nielsen and Tore Olsen · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2014 · Individual

Uses the universe of Danish savings records to show that tax subsidies for retirement accounts mostly shift saving between accounts: each krone of subsidy raises total saving by about one øre. Automatic employer contributions, by contrast, raise total saving almost one for one, because roughly 85 percent of individuals are passive savers who do not re-optimise.

Data
Income tax register with third-party reported earnings and pension contributions (IND), Third-party reported wealth: end-of-year assets and liabilities in taxable accounts, Population register (BEF), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA)
Key variables
  • Outcome: contributions to employer and individual retirement accounts
  • Outcome: taxable saving (change in taxable assets), gross and net
  • Treatment: employer pension contribution changes at job switches (automatic contributions)
  • Treatment: 1999 reduction in tax subsidy for capital pensions for top-bracket taxpayers (price subsidy)
  • Treatment: government mandatory savings plan (SP) contributions
Period
1995–2009
Sample
approximately 41 million observations for 4 million individuals Danish population aged 18–60, excluding observations with self-employment income (unbalanced panel). source

Tax Bunching, Income Shifting and Self-Employment

Daniel le Maire and Bertel Schjerning · Journal of Public Economics, 2013 · Individual

Self-employed Danes bunch sharply at the top tax kink, but much of the bunching reflects intertemporal shifting of income through retained earnings in the business rather than real changes in effort. Accounting for shifting implies a much smaller elasticity of real income.

Data
Tax records of Danish self-employed individuals (taxable income, retained earnings)
Key variables
  • Outcome: bunching of taxable income at kinks of the tax schedule
  • Mechanism: intertemporal income shifting via retained earnings in the business tax schemes
  • Estimate: taxable income elasticity (0.43–0.53 ignoring shifting; structural 0.14–0.20)
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish self-employed taxpayers. source (abstract)

Unwilling or Unable to Cheat? Evidence from a Tax Audit Experiment in Denmark

Henrik Jacobsen Kleven, Martin B. Knudsen, Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Søren Pedersen and Emmanuel Saez · Econometrica, 2011 · Individual

A randomised field experiment with the Danish tax authority on about 40,000 taxpayers. Evasion is close to zero for third-party reported income but substantial for self-reported income, and audit threat letters raise reported self-reported income. Information, not morality, is what makes modern tax systems work.

Data
Randomized tax audit experiment implemented by the Danish tax authority (SKAT), SKAT Business Object Database: third-party information reports, pre-populated returns, filed returns and after-audit returns, item by item, SKAT computer-generated audit flags, Basic socio-economic variables and employer industry/size from SKAT records
Key variables
  • Treatment: random assignment to 100% vs 0% unannounced audit group (2007 filing season)
  • Treatment: random threat-of-audit letters (100%, 50%, none) before the 2008 filing deadline
  • Outcome: detected tax evasion (audit adjustments to net income and tax)
  • Outcome: change in reported income in the following year
  • Heterogeneity: self-reported vs third-party reported income
Period
2006–2007 income years (2007 and 2008 filing seasons)
Sample
25,020 employees and 17,764 self-employed Stratified random sample of Danish taxpayers (employees, including transfer recipients, and self-employed) selected by SKAT for the audit experiment. source

Adjustment Costs, Firm Responses, and Micro vs. Macro Labor Supply Elasticities: Evidence from Danish Tax Records

Raj Chetty, John N. Friedman, Tore Olsen and Luigi Pistaferri · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2011 · Individual + firm

Uses bunching at kinks in the Danish tax schedule to show that observed earnings responses are shaped by search costs and by firms coordinating hours around tax thresholds. Once adjustment frictions are accounted for, the structural labour supply elasticity is around 0.3, far above the small elasticities from individual-level micro studies, which helps reconcile micro and macro estimates.

Data
Income tax register / Income Statistics Register (IND), Population register (BEF), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) with firm ID and occupation
Key variables
  • Outcome: bunching (excess mass) of taxable income at the top-tax kink
  • Treatment: marginal tax rate changes at bracket cutoffs (tax simulator)
  • Key variable: firm and occupation-level bunching (hours constraints / firm responses)
  • Key variable: size of tax change across kinks
  • Comparison: wage earners vs self-employed
Period
1994–2001
Sample
17.9 million observations of wage earners (6.8 million within DKr 50,000 of the top tax cutoff; 1.8 million self-employed observations) Danish wage earners aged 15–70, excluding the self-employed and pensioners, with self-employed studied separately. source

Public Pension Programmes and the Retirement of Married Couples in Denmark

Paul Bingley and Gauthier Lanot · Journal of Public Economics, 2007 · Individual

Estimates a dynamic discrete choice model of couples' joint retirement and saving on a 1 percent register sample of Danish couples, using the introduction and reforms of the early retirement programme (efterløn) for identification. Retirement responds asymmetrically for men and women to their own and their spouse's income flows.

Data
1% sample of Danish couples of potential retirement age from an administrative register
Key variables
  • Outcome: retirement decisions of husband and wife (joint retirement)
  • Identifying variation: introduction and subsequent reforms of the publicly financed early retirement programme (efterløn)
  • Explanatory: own and spouse's income flows
  • Model: dynamic discrete choice model with saving

Subjective Earnings Risk

Andrew Caplin, Victoria Gregory, Eungik Lee, Søren Leth-Petersen and Johan Sæverud · Review of Economic Studies, 2026 · Individual

Introduces a survey instrument for earnings risk that allows for quitting and being fired, fielded to Danish workers and linked to their register earnings histories. Job transitions drive subjective risk, which is many times smaller than traditional register-based estimates even after conditioning on demographics and job history, and helps explain why many hold few liquid assets.

Data
Copenhagen Life Panel (CLP) survey, January 2021 wave, linked on CPR number, Income-tax register earnings (IND), Monthly employer-employee match and earnings data (BFL/eIndkomst), Demographic, education, household composition and household wealth registers
Key variables
  • Survey: subjective probabilities of staying, quitting and being laid off
  • Survey: subjective earnings distributions conditional on each job transition
  • Benchmark: realized job transitions, time out of work and earnings growth in 2021 (administrative)
  • Weights: population weights from a survey-participation model on 2020 administrative data
Period
Survey January 2021; administrative data through 2022
Sample
10,942 employed respondents (gross sample 14,683 respondents aged 20–65) Copenhagen Life Panel respondents aged 20–65 who were employed at the time of the January 2021 survey. source

Beliefs About the Economy Are Excessively Sensitive to Household-Level Shocks: Evidence from Linked Survey and Administrative Data

Dmitry Taubinsky, Luigi Butera, Matteo Saccarola and Chen Lian · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2026 · Individual

Links Statistics Denmark's consumer expectations survey to the registers to show that people's inflation forecasts and backcasts move strongly, and negatively, with their own recent and expected income changes, far more than actual inflation does. The pattern departs from Bayesian rational expectations and is consistent with affect-cued recall, where bad personal events cue pessimistic recollections.

Data
Danish Consumer Expectations Survey (Statistics Denmark Omnibus), linked on CPR, Tax registers from SKAT: income, liquid assets, net wealth, taxes (IND, formue), Civil Registration System / Population register (CPR/BEF), Education register (UDDA), National Patient Register (LPR), emergency room visits 1994–2018
Key variables
  • Outcome: inflation forecasts and backcasts (qualitative and quantitative)
  • Explanatory: recent change in household log nominal income (t-1 vs t-2)
  • Explanatory: future household income change and expected household financial situation
  • Shock: family emergency room visit in the survey month
  • Benchmark: realized inflation
Period
2012–2019 (robustness from 2008; population registers from 1991)
Sample
35050 usable responses (from 55171 survey respondents aged 25–60) Danish Consumer Expectations Survey respondents aged 25–60 in 2012–2019 without substantial self-employment income and with complete answers, linked to administrative data. source

Survivors' Mental Health and the Protective Role of Income Stability

Itzik Fadlon, Astrid Sophie Fugleholm and Torben Heien Nielsen · AER: Insights, 2026 · Individual

Documents the immediate, large and lingering rise in mental health medication after a spouse's death across the universe of Danish households. A large share of the spike is explained by the income loss the death imposes, so safety-net liquidity protects survivors' mental health.

Data
Population Registry (BEF) with family linkages, Death Registry, National Patient Registry (LPR), Income Registry (earnings, transfers, pension payouts, capital income), Education Registry, Pharmaceutical Database / prescription register (LMDB), Health Insurance Registry (psychiatrist visits)
Key variables
  • Treatment: spouse's fatal heart attack or stroke (death within 12 months), treated vs later-event control group
  • Outcome: take-up of mental health medication (ATC N05, N06)
  • Mechanism: household income loss from spousal death (instrumented by deceased spouse's earnings share)
  • Heterogeneity: baseline income, net wealth and insurance (DI/OAP, sick pay)
Period
1995–2018
Sample
12,637 treatment and 15,212 control households Danish couples aged 45–80 in which one spouse had a heart attack or stroke between 1999 and 2018 and died within 12 months, excluding the self-employed, balanced four years before and after. source

Taxing the Wealth of the Poor: Evidence from the Danish Old-Age Support Asset Test

Niels Johannesen, Johan Sæverud and Emmanuel Saez · AER: Insights, 2026 · Individual

The asset test for Danish old-age support creates diffuse but large excess mass in liquid assets just below the threshold among the eligible, relative to comparable ineligible groups. Cohort analysis suggests the test reduces liquid assets by about 20 percent near the threshold, and high-frequency bank data show the responses are permanent rather than timed around the test date.

Data
Administrative registers on assets, income and demographics (Statistics Denmark): third-party reported and self-reported liquid wealth, Danske Bank customer data: monthly spending, cash withdrawals and account balances (2014–2016)
Key variables
  • Running variable: liquid wealth relative to the old-age check asset test threshold
  • Outcome: bunching / excess mass of liquid wealth below the threshold
  • Outcome: year-end spending and cash withdrawals (bank data)
  • Eligibility: age 65+ and low income
Period
2014–2018 (main cross-section); bank data 2014–2016
Sample
2,526,421 observations of singles aged 60+ (2,054,987 aged 65+; 1,283,721 low-income 65+), pooled 2014–2018; bank sample 461,471 observations, 2014–2016 Single individuals aged 60 and over in Denmark, with a linked Danske Bank subsample of about 20 percent of the elderly population. source

Breaking Bad: How Health Shocks Prompt Crime

Steffen Andersen, Elin Colmsjö, Gianpaolo Parise and Kim Peijnenburg · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2026 · Individual

Uses plausibly exogenous timing of cancer diagnoses in Danish registers to show that a health shock raises the probability of committing a crime, both first offences and re-offences, persistently. Patients replace lost legal income with illegal income and face a lower expected cost of punishment because of reduced survival, and welfare programmes that cushion the income loss dampen the effect.

Data
Danish administrative registers on cancer diagnoses (timing of diagnosis), Crime registers (first crimes and re-offenses), Income and welfare program registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: timing of a cancer diagnosis (health shock)
  • Outcome: probability of committing a crime (first crimes and re-offenses)
  • Mechanism: loss of legal income
  • Mechanism: survival probability (expected cost of punishment)
  • Heterogeneity: welfare program coverage
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish individuals diagnosed with cancer, using variation in the timing of diagnosis. source (abstract)

Dynamic Spending Responses to Wealth Shocks: Evidence from Quasi Lotteries on the Stock Market

Asger Lau Andersen, Niels Johannesen and Adam Sheridan · AER: Insights, 2024 · Individual

Uses lottery-like variation in stock market gains across investors with similar portfolios, observed in granular bank data, to trace spending responses. Responses are immediate and persistent, cumulating to a marginal propensity to consume of 4.4 percent over one year and 16 percent over three, consistent with the permanent income hypothesis with some inattention.

Data
Danske Bank customer records: transaction-level spending, securities portfolios and online logins (2009–2016), Population register (household identifiers, age), Tax register: income and banks' year-end account-level reports, Employment register (employer links), Education register
Key variables
  • Treatment: monthly stock market gains, with quasi-random variation across investors with similar portfolio characteristics
  • Outcome: monthly household consumer spending (card, bill, mobile payments, cash withdrawals)
  • Heterogeneity: liquidity
  • Heterogeneity: financial attention (frequency of account logins)
Period
2009–2016
Sample
around 13.5 million household-month observations comprising 390,000 distinct households Danske Bank households with stock portfolios of at least 100 DKK and at least five spending transactions per month, excluding employee-shareholders and holders of only Danske Bank shares. source

Public Pensions and Private Savings

Esteban García-Miralles and Jonathan M. Leganza · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

A Danish reform raising the social security eligibility age, studied with a regression discontinuity in administrative data, increased contributions to personal and employer pension accounts when later eligibility kept people working longer. Inertia in previous savings behaviour and firms' default contribution rates are the key mechanisms.

Data
Population registers (date of birth, demographics), Labour-market and income/asset registers (tax-reported bank and stock balances, property values, pension contributions), Monthly earnings register (eIncome / eIndkomst), Voluntary early retirement pension (VERP) contribution and benefit records
Key variables
  • Running variable: birth date relative to 1 January 1954 cutoff
  • Treatment: higher pension eligibility ages from 2011 reform
  • Outcome: contributions to employer-sponsored and personal retirement accounts
  • Outcome: changes in bank and stock account balances and property values
  • Outcome: pre-tax earnings and monthly retirement age
Period
1985–2018 (annual); 2008–2017 (monthly)
Sample
40,042 individuals (analysis sample); 12,020 individuals (RD estimation sample) Non-self-employed Danes born within six months of 1 January 1954 who regularly contributed to the VERP early retirement scheme before the reform. source

The Long-Run Effects of Individual Debt Relief

Gustaf Bruze, Alexander Kjær Hilsløv and Jonas Maibom · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

Links court records on the Danish debt relief programme to the registers and uses quasi-random assignment of applicants to court trustees with different admission rates. Debt relief raises earned income, employment, assets, home ownership and wealth for more than 25 years, with the move into employment accounting for two thirds of the income gain.

Data
Hand-collected debt relief announcements from the official gazette Statstidende (court records), 1984–2005, Central Person Register (CPR) for linkage, Income Tax Register: earned income, taxable assets and debt, real estate ownership, Labour market status registers (employment status in November), Hourly wage measure from Statistics Denmark
Key variables
  • Treatment: being granted court-ordered debt relief
  • Instrument: court trustee admission rate (judge/trustee leniency design)
  • Outcome: earned income and employment
  • Outcome: taxable assets, secured and unsecured debt, home ownership, wealth
Period
Applications 1984–2003; register outcomes 1980–2019
Sample
46,571 individuals (final sample); 32,794 observations in the IV sample with 515 trustee identifiers All applicants for debt relief in Danish City Courts from 1984 to 2003, linked to national registers. source

Spending Response to a Predictable Increase in Mortgage Repayments: Evidence from Expiring Interest-Only Loans

Henrik Yde Andersen, Stine Ludvig Bech and Alessia De Stefani · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

Matches the universe of Danish mortgages to borrower registers and studies the fully anticipated start of amortisation on interest-only loans. When instalments rise by 9 percent of income, consumption falls by 3 percent of income and stays down, with the failure to smooth concentrated among highly leveraged borrowers likely to be refused a new interest-only loan.

Data
Mortgage registry: loan-level data on the universe of Danish mortgages reported by mortgage institutions to Danmarks Nationalbank (from 2009), Income and wealth registers (IND/FORMUE): household income, bank deposits, stocks, bonds, pensions, debts, Population/household registers (BEF): family composition, residence, Imputed household consumption from income and wealth registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: expiry of the interest-only period (start of amortisation)
  • Outcome: imputed household consumption relative to income
  • Installment increase relative to income
  • Heterogeneity: loan-to-value ratio and liquid savings
Period
Mortgage registry 2009–2018; IO loans originated 2003–2007 expiring 2013–2017
Sample
46,025 household-year observations (baseline regressions); 8,813 households beginning amortization Danish households with an interest-only mortgage originated 2003–2007 whose IO period expires in 2013–2017 and who keep the original loan, excluding self-employed and house buyers/sellers. source

How Do Households Respond to Job Loss? Lessons from Multiple High-Frequency Datasets

Asger Lau Andersen, Amalie Sofie Jensen, Niels Johannesen, Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Søren Leth-Petersen and Adam Sheridan · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2023 · Individual

Combines bank transaction data with government registers to measure every margin of self-insurance after job loss. Over two years households cut spending by 30 percent of the income loss and cover about half of it by running down liquid balances; spousal labour supply, private transfers, home equity extraction and consumer credit contribute less.

Data
Danske Bank customer transaction and account records (spending, income inflows, balances, loans), Monthly payroll and income transfer records (BFL/eIndkomst) from the Danish Tax Agency, Population register (BEF) household structure and spouse links, End-of-year account-level bank loan and deposit records from the Danish Tax Agency, Loan-level mortgage data from mortgage banks (via Statistics Denmark), Mass layoff notifications to the Danish Agency for Labour Market and Recruitment, Annual income and wealth, education, home ownership and occupation registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: job loss event (main employer wage payments cease, followed by unemployment benefits)
  • Outcome: household spending (card, mobile, bills, ATM withdrawals)
  • Outcome: net saving in liquid assets, non-mortgage debt repayment, mortgage refinancing and equity extraction
  • Outcome: spousal labour income, private transfers, government transfers
  • Design: monthly event study from 18 months before to 24 months after job loss
  • Subsample: involuntary job loss from mass layoffs
Period
January 2009–December 2016 (job losses July 2010–December 2015)
Sample
10,002 individuals (baseline sample of active bank customers); gross sample of 66,844 job losers Danish individuals born 1950–1979 who lost their job between July 2010 and December 2015 after at least 18 months of stable earnings, in stable households that are active customers of Danske Bank. source

Consumption Heterogeneity: Micro Drivers and Macro Implications

Edmund Crawley and Andreas Kuchler · AEJ: Macroeconomics, 2023 · Individual

Estimates marginal propensities to consume across Danish households with a method that corrects biases in earlier register-based estimates. Liquid wealth is the dominant predictor of MPCs, and the estimated heterogeneity is used to quantify monetary policy transmission in Denmark and the United States.

Data
Tax-authority income records (household after-tax and transfer income), Tax-reported wealth data: bank deposits, loans, bond and stock holdings, mortgages (year-end balances), Private pension contribution records, Civil Registration System (CPR) family/household links, Mortgage reset data (amount resetting over the following year)
Key variables
  • Outcome: imputed household expenditure (income minus pension contributions minus change in non-housing net worth)
  • Shock: unexpected (residual) change in after-tax household income of the head
  • Heterogeneity: liquid wealth (bank deposits) quintiles
  • Heterogeneity: Unhedged Interest Rate Exposure (URE) and Net Nominal Position (NNP)
Period
2003–2015
Sample
7.7 million observations from 2004 to 2015 Danish households headed by an individual aged 30–55 in 2008, excluding business owners, households with housing transactions, and top/bottom 1% of income and income changes. source

Long-Run Saving Dynamics: Evidence from Unexpected Inheritances

Jeppe Druedahl and Alessandro Martinello · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2022 · Individual

Sudden parental deaths deliver unexpected inheritances. Heirs' wealth jumps and then declines over roughly a decade back toward its pre-inheritance path, which implies a moderate marginal propensity to consume out of wealth and informs the calibration of buffer-stock saving models.

Data
Wealth data from yearly third-party financial institution reports in the income/wealth registers (IND/FORMUE: BANKAKT, KURSANP, etc.), Individual tax returns / Income register (IND) for disposable income, Housing and land registers (property values, number of housing units), Birth registers / parent-child links (BEF), Mortality register / Register of Causes of Death (ICD-10 cause of death)
Key variables
  • Outcome: net worth normalized by permanent income
  • Outcome: liquid assets, housing equity, financial investments, uncollateralized debt
  • Treatment: timing of sudden parental death (ICD-10 I21–I22, V, X, Y, R96)
  • Potential inheritance: deceased parent's net worth split among children, net of inheritance tax
  • Permanent income: weighted average of past five years' disposable income
Period
1995–2012
Sample
6,286 heirs Heirs aged 25–50 at inheritance whose unmarried parent died suddenly between 1995 and 2012, with potential inheritance exceeding one year of permanent income, excluding the wealthiest 1 percent. source

Equilibrium Trade in Automobiles

Kenneth Gillingham, Fedor Iskhakov, Anders Munk-Nielsen, John Rust and Bertel Schjerning · Journal of Political Economy, 2022 · Individual

Develops and estimates a dynamic equilibrium model of the market for new and used cars with heterogeneous households on the universe of Danish car ownership records, which record every vehicle's owner, age and scrappage. The model is used to evaluate Denmark's very high registration taxes and fuel taxes, showing how tax changes propagate through used-car prices and the age distribution of the fleet.

  • Policy variable: new car registration tax and fuel tax

Sample

Reference Dependence in the Housing Market

Steffen Andersen, Cristian Badarinza, Lu Liu, Julie Marx and Tarun Ramadorai · American Economic Review, 2022 · Individual

Combines Danish housing listings and transactions with register data on owners to estimate a structural model of listing and sale decisions with loss aversion and financial constraints. Households derive utility from gains over the original purchase price and weigh losses about 2.5 times more than gains, which helps explain the co-movement of house prices and turnover.

Data
Property ownership and transaction register from SKAT and the Building and Housing Register (BBR), 1992–2016, Tax-assessed property values (SKAT), Property listings data from RealView, 2008–2016, Loan-level mortgage data from Danmarks Nationalbank, 2009–2016, Civil Registration System (CPR/BEF) for demographics and household links, Income and wealth records from SKAT (IND/formue)
Key variables
  • Outcome: listing decision and listing premium relative to hedonic value
  • Main regressor: potential gain or loss relative to original purchase price (reference point)
  • Potential home equity relative to down-payment constraint
  • Expected house value from hedonic pricing model
  • Structural parameter: loss aversion
Period
2009–2016 (transactions from 1992)
Sample
214,103 listings (191,507 unique households, 178,933 unique properties); housing stock of 5,538,052 observations of 807,345 properties Listings of Danish owner-occupied housing between 2009 and 2016, both sold and retracted, matched to mortgages and household finances. source

Jonathan Gruber, Amalie Sofie Jensen and Henrik Kleven · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2021 · Individual

A major reform that scaled back the mortgage interest deduction for middle- and high-income households had a precisely estimated zero effect on home ownership. It did reduce the size and price of houses bought, lowered household indebtedness and pushed down equilibrium house prices.

Sample
about 40 million individual-year observations; 34,440,869 observations in the main homeownership regression (below-top controls) Semi-balanced panel of Danish individuals aged 20–80 observed in every year 1980–1996, excluding children living with parents. source

Spot Price Biases in Non-Linear Health Insurance Contracts

Marianne Simonsen, Lars Skipper, Niels Skipper and Anne Illemann Christensen · Journal of Public Economics, 2021 · Individual

The Danish prescription drug reimbursement scheme has coverage that rises with annual spending and resets each year. Consumers look forward-looking around the reset but react too strongly to current prices within the year, and liquidity constraints rather than plan literacy or cognitive decline explain the spot-price bias.

  • Outcome: timing and amount of prescription drug purchases
  • Heterogeneity: liquidity constraints, plan literacy, cognitive decline

Sample

Loan-level records on all personal loans show strong intergenerational persistence in financial trouble: children of parents who default are far more likely to default themselves, and the correlation is present already in early adulthood and is not explained by income or education.

Katrine Jakobsen, Kristian Jakobsen, Henrik Kleven and Gabriel Zucman · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2020 · Individual

Exploits the 1989 Danish wealth tax reform, which cut rates sharply for the wealthy, to estimate long-run effects of wealth taxes on wealth accumulation. Responses are sizable for the very wealthy (top one percent) and small for the moderately wealthy, with effects that keep growing over a decade.

  • Controls: pre-reform portfolio shares and income deciles

Steffen Andersen, John Y. Campbell, Kasper Meisner Nielsen and Tarun Ramadorai · American Economic Review, 2020 · Individual

Many Danish households fail to refinance fixed-rate mortgages when it is clearly profitable. A model with inattention and psychological refinancing costs fits the data, and both frictions are stronger for households with less education, income and financial wealth.

Links incentivised time-discounting experiments to the wealth positions of a large sample of middle-aged Danes in the registers.   • Heterogeneity: credit-constrained vs unconstrained individuals

Sample
3,620 experiment participants (of 27,613 invited); 2,548 survey subjects in the 1952–1955 cohorts Danes born 1973–1983 who lived in Copenhagen municipality at age seven and completed the online experiment. source

Claus Thustrup Kreiner, David Dreyer Lassen and Søren Leth-Petersen · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2019 · Individual

The 2009 payout of Special Pension savings worked as a fiscal stimulus. Measuring constraint tightness by the marginal interest rate households pay, the spending response is highest for households facing high marginal rates and otherwise small, which supports targeting stimulus at the credit constrained.

  • Validation: register-based measures of net savings and pension savings
Period
