Consumption Network Effects

Giacomo De Giorgi, Anders Frederiksen and Luigi Pistaferri · Review of Economic Studies, 2019 · Individual + firm

Measures household consumption from Danish tax records on income and assets and defines peer groups by workplace using matched employer-employee data. Instrumenting peers' consumption with shocks to their own non-overlapping networks and firms, the paper finds sizeable consumption spillovers that can generate macro multipliers, operating through both intertemporal and intratemporal channels.

Data
Administrative income and wealth tax records (tax registry incl. wealth tax data), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) for plant IDs and coworker networks, Danish Expenditure Survey (DES) 1994–1996
Key variables
  • Outcome: household consumption imputed from income and change in assets
  • Endogenous regressor: average consumption of coworkers' households (peer consumption)
  • Instruments: firm-level shocks to distance-3 (non-overlapping) network members
  • Network definition: coworkers in the same plant
  • Mechanisms: budget shares of visible goods (DES)
Period
1980–1996
Sample
757,439 households Danish households with head aged 18–65 where both spouses are working employees, linked to spouses' coworkers. source

Born with a Silver Spoon? Danish Evidence on Wealth Inequality in Childhood

Simon Halphen Boserup, Wojciech Kopczuk and Claus Thustrup Kreiner · Economic Journal, 2018 · Individual

Third-party reported wealth records of children show that wealth inequality is as high in childhood as among adults, with top shares driven by transfers from parents and grandparents. Wealth at age 18 predicts wealth in adulthood, and intergenerational wealth correlations are present from early ages.

Data
Individual wealth data from tax return records (Danish Tax Agency SKAT, via Statistics Denmark), Population register with parent identifiers (BEF), Education and income registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: child wealth and wealth rank in adulthood (age 45)
  • Key regressor: childhood wealth (age 18) and parental wealth rank
  • Inferred inter vivos transfers and bequests (bunching at gift-tax allowance)
  • Portfolio components (deposits, securities, real estate, debt)
Period
1983–2011
Sample
Pooled cross-section: on average close to 650 thousand observations per one-year age group; panel regressions: 36,102 observations All individuals aged 1–40 in 2000–2011 with living parents (pooled cross-section), plus a panel of the 1965 birth cohort with both parents alive in 2011, followed 1983–2011. source

The Consumption Effects of the 2007–2008 Financial Crisis: Evidence from Households in Denmark

Thais Lærkholm Jensen and Niels Johannesen · American Economic Review, 2017 · Individual

Customers of banks that were hit hard by the crisis cut consumption more than customers of healthier banks. The channel is reduced credit availability: exposed households borrowed less and spent less, which shows how bank distress transmits to the real economy through households.

Data
Tax authority third-party reports of all individuals' loan and deposit account balances, linked to bank identity via branch numbers (Nets lists), Bank balance sheet data from the Danish Central Bank (MFI statistics), Administrative registers on demographics, education, labour market and income, Income and wealth registers (capital income, social transfers, stock portfolios, pension accounts), Motor vehicle register and real estate register
Key variables
  • Treatment: primary bank's 2007 loan-to-deposit ratio (above vs below median exposure)
  • Outcome: individual borrowing (loan balances by account)
  • Outcome: imputed spending = disposable income minus change in net wealth
  • Outcome: car ownership (motor vehicle register)
  • Separate analysis: customers of banks that failed 2008–2011
Period
2003–2011
Sample
around 440,000 individuals, almost 3.5 million individual-years and more than 5.7 million individual-account-years 25% random sample of non-self-employed Danish residents aged 20–50 in 2007 whose primary bank did not fail during 2008–2011. source

Do Employer Pension Contributions Reflect Employee Preferences? Evidence from a Retirement Savings Reform in Denmark

Itzik Fadlon, Jessica Laird and Torben Heien Nielsen · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2016 · Individual + firm

A reform that cut the subsidy to capital pension accounts for top-bracket workers led employers to reduce contributions to those accounts immediately, by more the larger the share of affected employees, and to shift the money to annuity accounts. Employer contributions track employees' savings preferences.

Data
Income tax register (incl. third-party reported pension contributions), Population register (BEF), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA)
Key variables
  • Outcome: employer contribution rate to capital and annuity pension accounts (median within workplace)
  • Treatment: share of workplace employees above the top income tax threshold
  • Policy variation: 1999 reform cutting the deduction for capital pension contributions for top-bracket taxpayers
  • Unit: workplace = 2-digit occupation × firm cell
  • Comparison: individual private (IRA-type) pension contributions
Period
1996–2001
Sample
2,020,705 worker-year observations; 84,764 workplace-year observations (26,775 unique workplaces) Private-sector white-collar wage earners aged 20–60 without self-employment income, in occupation-firm cells with at least five employees. source

Active vs. Passive Decisions and Crowd-Out in Retirement Savings Accounts: Evidence from Denmark

Raj Chetty, John N. Friedman, Søren Leth-Petersen, Torben Heien Nielsen and Tore Olsen · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2014 · Individual

Uses the universe of Danish savings records to show that tax subsidies for retirement accounts mostly shift saving between accounts: each krone of subsidy raises total saving by about one øre. Automatic employer contributions, by contrast, raise total saving almost one for one, because roughly 85 percent of individuals are passive savers who do not re-optimise.

Data
Income tax register with third-party reported earnings and pension contributions (IND), Third-party reported wealth: end-of-year assets and liabilities in taxable accounts, Population register (BEF), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA)
Key variables
  • Outcome: contributions to employer and individual retirement accounts
  • Outcome: taxable saving (change in taxable assets), gross and net
  • Treatment: employer pension contribution changes at job switches (automatic contributions)
  • Treatment: 1999 reduction in tax subsidy for capital pensions for top-bracket taxpayers (price subsidy)
  • Treatment: government mandatory savings plan (SP) contributions
Period
1995–2009
Sample
approximately 41 million observations for 4 million individuals Danish population aged 18–60, excluding observations with self-employment income (unbalanced panel). source

Housing Wealth and Consumption: A Micro Panel Study

Martin Browning, Mette Gørtz and Søren Leth-Petersen · Economic Journal, 2013 · Individual

Uses register-based imputed consumption and house price changes over the 1987 to 1996 boom and bust to test whether housing wealth drives spending. For most households consumption does not respond to unanticipated house price changes; the response is confined to young households, consistent with a collateral rather than a wealth effect.

Data
Administrative income and wealth (tax) registers for a 10% random sample of the Danish population aged 16+, House price / housing wealth information from registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: imputed household total expenditure (disposable income minus change in net wealth)
  • Key regressor: unexpected changes in house prices / housing wealth
  • Heterogeneity: age, owners vs renters, credit constraints (1992–93 mortgage reform allowing home-equity borrowing)
  • Disposable household income
Period
1987–1996
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Married or cohabiting stable couples from a 10% random sample of the Danish population aged 16+, who were either homeowners or renters throughout the period. source

Intertemporal Consumption and Credit Constraints: Does Total Expenditure Respond to an Exogenous Shock to Credit?

Søren Leth-Petersen · American Economic Review, 2010 · Individual

The 1992 reform that let Danish homeowners borrow against housing equity provides an exogenous credit shock. Total expenditure responded only modestly on average, with the response concentrated among young households with little liquid wealth, which indicates that credit constraints bind for a minority.

Data
Income tax register: taxable income, transfers, final taxes, Wealth tax register: third-party reported assets and liabilities (housing, shares, mortgage deeds, cash, bonds, debt), Administrative registers on family composition and dwelling characteristics, Danish Family Expenditure Survey (DES) 1994–1996, used to validate the expenditure imputation
Key variables
  • Outcome: total household expenditure imputed from income minus change in wealth
  • Treatment: 1992 credit market reform allowing home equity to be used as collateral for consumption loans
  • Treatment group: homeowners (renters as comparison/consistency check)
  • Credit-constraint proxy: low liquid (non-housing) assets in 1991 relative to monthly disposable income
Period
1987–1996
Sample
38,486 households, of which 25,275 live in owner-occupied housing (working-paper version) Households from a 10% random sample of the Danish population, aged 18–75, not self-employed, non-movers observed in all years 1987–1996. source

Imputing Consumption from Income and Wealth Information

Martin Browning and Søren Leth-Petersen · Economic Journal, 2003 · Individual

Tests whether consumption can be imputed from register data as income minus the change in wealth, by comparing the imputation with expenditure survey responses for the same households. The imputation works well for most households but is noisy for those with housing transactions or large stock holdings.

Data
Danish Expenditure Survey 1994–96, Administrative income and wealth (tax) records: disposable income and year-end asset holdings
Key variables
  • Outcome: survey-reported total household expenditure
  • Imputed consumption: disposable income minus change in wealth
  • Year-end holdings of different assets
Period
1994–1996 survey years, linked to register years around the survey year
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Households in the 1994–96 Danish Expenditure Survey linked to their administrative income and wealth records. source (abstract)

Labour markets, wages and sorting

Search and matching, wage dynamics and who works with whom, using matched employer-employee data.

Working Hours, Top Management Appointments, and Gender: Evidence from Linked Employer-Employee Data

Anders Frederiksen, Takao Kato and Nina Smith · Journal of Labor Economics, 2026 · Individual + firm

Links the Danish Labour Force Survey's detailed hours to register career histories. Long hours, hours relative to peers and nonstandard hours raise the odds of a top management appointment within the firm, and the gender gap in hours, driven mainly by supply-side factors, is a major contributor to the gender gap in reaching top management.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Danish Labour Force Survey (DLFS/AKU) linked on person identifier, Organizational hierarchy codes (occupation-based) from registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: appointment to a top management position between year t and t+1
  • Explanatory: weekly working hours from the Labour Force Survey in year t
  • Heterogeneity: gender (gender gap in hours and promotions)
  • Hierarchy level: top manager, manager, white-collar, blue/grey-collar
  • Controls: experience, tenure, education, children, marital status
Period
1994–2010
Sample
168,499 employees Danish employees randomly sampled in the Labour Force Survey 1994–2010, merged with IDA register data (IZA DP 11675 working-paper version). source

Reskilling and Resilience

Anders Humlum and Pernille Plato · AER: Insights (forthcoming), 2026 · Individual

Studies how retraining shapes workers' resilience to labour market shocks in Danish administrative data, extending the authors' work on human capital investment after injury to the question of which workers reskill and how reskilling protects earnings when jobs disappear.

Data
Work accident reports from Labour Market Insurance (AES), Education Register (UDDA), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), National Patient Registry (hospitalizations), Health Insurance Registry (primary care and specialist visits), Prescription Drug Database, Population register (BEF) for partner linkages
Key variables
  • Shock: work accident causing permanent loss of earning capacity
  • Treatment/moderator: access to reskilling (vocational degree granting direct entry to higher education)
  • Outcome: antidepressant use and other mental health measures
  • Outcome: partner labour market and health outcomes
  • Outcome: earnings and employment of injured workers
Period
1995–2017 (work accidents 1998–2017)
Sample
2,915 injury events (male craft workers); 2,103 stable partners Male craft workers with vocational degrees and full-time employment in the three years before a physical work accident causing permanent loss of earning capacity, and their partners. source

Subjective Earnings Risk

Andrew Caplin, Victoria Gregory, Eungik Lee, Søren Leth-Petersen and Johan Sæverud · Review of Economic Studies, 2026 · Individual

Introduces a survey instrument for earnings risk that allows for quitting and being fired, fielded to Danish workers and linked to their register earnings histories. Job transitions drive subjective risk, which is many times smaller than traditional register-based estimates even after conditioning on demographics and job history, and helps explain why many hold few liquid assets.

Data
Copenhagen Life Panel (CLP) survey, January 2021 wave, linked on CPR number, Income-tax register earnings (IND), Monthly employer-employee match and earnings data (BFL/eIndkomst), Demographic, education, household composition and household wealth registers
Key variables
  • Survey: subjective probabilities of staying, quitting and being laid off
  • Survey: subjective earnings distributions conditional on each job transition
  • Benchmark: realized job transitions, time out of work and earnings growth in 2021 (administrative)
  • Weights: population weights from a survey-participation model on 2020 administrative data
Period
Survey January 2021; administrative data through 2022
Sample
10,942 employed respondents (gross sample 14,683 respondents aged 20–65) Copenhagen Life Panel respondents aged 20–65 who were employed at the time of the January 2021 survey. source

Fertility and Family Labor Supply

Katrine Jakobsen, Thomas H. Jørgensen and Hamish Low · American Economic Review (forthcoming), 2026 · Individual

Uses the 2009 Danish tax reform to show that higher net-of-tax wages for women reduce fertility while higher wages for men raise it. A life-cycle model of couples choosing the timing and number of children, estimated on register data, implies that allowing fertility to adjust raises women's labour supply responsiveness substantially and that human capital depreciation around births drives much of the long-run gender wage gap.

Data
Monthly payroll records (BFL/eIndkomst), Annual income tax data (IND), Population and family register (BEF), family definitions and parent links, Education register (UDDA), Labour market experience from ATP payments, Parental leave spell register (OF)
Key variables
  • Outcome: fertility (births)
  • Treatment: 2009–2018 tax reform changes in marginal net-of-tax wage rate
  • Outcome: labour market participation, part-time and full-time work from pay-slip hours
  • Outcome: annual labour income
  • Control: education (high vs less skilled), labour market experience
Period
2004–2018 (tax sample 2004–2018; estimation sample 2010–2018)
Sample
10,851,444 individual-year observations (estimation sample); 2,531,181 (tax sample) Danish individuals aged 25–60 with an opposite-sex partner, not students, self-employed, retired or on disability pension; tax sample restricted to cohabiting or married women aged 25–40. source

Effects of Parental Death on Labor Market Outcomes

Mathias Fjællegaard Jensen and Ning Zhang · American Economic Review, 2026 · Individual

Uses sudden first parental deaths and matched controls in Danish administrative data. Earnings fall by about 2 percent for men and 3 percent for women, mental health worsens for both, and women with young children lose the most, around 4 percent, consistent with the loss of informal childcare from grandparents.

Data
Cause of death registers (DODSAARS/DODSAARG), Population register with parent-child links (BEF), Income and labour market registers (IND, AKM, IDAS/IDAN/IDAP), ATP-based hours proxy, Health Insurance Register for GP/psychologist/psychiatrist visits (SYSI/SSSY), National Patient Register (LPR_ADM/LPR_DIAG), Psychiatric Central Research Register (PSYK_ADM/PSYK_DIAG), Prescription register (LMDB), Childcare registers (DAGI/BOERNFB)
Key variables
  • Treatment: first sudden parental death (heart disease, cerebrovascular disease, acute respiratory infection, accidents)
  • Outcome: annual earnings (indexed to pre-death gender mean)
  • Outcome: employment and hours proxy
  • Outcome: mental health care use and prescriptions
  • Control group: matched not-yet-treated individuals
Period
1980–2019 (parental deaths 1985–2014)
Sample
Treated: 21919 daughters and 24354 sons whose mother died first; 74991 daughters and 85022 sons whose father died first (plus matched controls) Danish adults aged 25–50 with two known parents who experienced a sudden first parental death between 1985 and 2014, with matched controls. source

Universal Daycare and Mothers' Working Lifetime

Sarah Sander · Economic Journal, 2026 · Individual

Exploits the roll-out of daycare centres across Danish municipalities to follow mothers over their working lives. Daycare availability raises participation, hours and earnings sixteen years after the first birth, with effects driven by low-educated mothers and mediated partly through fertility and separation.

Data
1970 Census (FT70) with parish of residence, Historical daycare institution yearbooks digitised (1964–1975) and daycare institutions in registers from 1976, Population register with parent-child links and fertility histories (BEF), Earnings from tax records (income registers), 1980–2015, Supplementary Pension Fund register (ATP) contributions for hours and participation, Education registers (UDDA)
Key variables
  • Treatment: daycare availability in the mother's 1970 parish after first birth
  • Outcome: labour force participation by age of first child
  • Outcome: log annual earnings
  • Outcome: weekly hours and full-time employment from ATP contributions
  • Mediators: fertility and separation from child's father
Period
First births 1964–1975; earnings 1980–2015
Sample
370,602 mothers Non-immigrant women resident in Denmark in the 1970 census who had their first child between 1964 and 1975 and stayed in Denmark until the child turned 16. source

The Direct and Indirect Effects of Online Job Search Advice

Steffen Altmann, Anita M. Glenny, Robert Mahlstedt and Alexander Sebald · Review of Economic Studies, 2026 · Individual

A large two-stage randomised experiment on the Danish public employment service's online platform gave job seekers vacancy information and occupational recommendations, with treatment intensity varying by region. Advice raises employment and earnings when few job seekers receive it, but the gains vanish at high intensity because treated job seekers crowd into the same occupations.

Data
Field experiment on jobnet.dk (Danish public employment service platform): treatment assignment and dashboard exposure, Jobnet job search profiles, vacancy information and registered job applications (with occupation codes), Statistics Denmark register data on employment, hours and earnings (12 months after start), Population registers and benefit payment records (UI recipients), Register data on the universe of unemployment-to-job occupational transitions (to build recommendations)
Key variables
  • Treatment: occupational recommendations, vacancy information, or both (individual randomization)
  • Treatment intensity: municipality cluster share of treated job seekers (two-stage randomization)
  • Outcome: working hours and earnings in the year after intervention
  • Outcome: job applications by occupation (search strategy)
Period
Intervention from March 17, 2019; outcomes over the following 12 months (before March 2020)
Sample
92,098 individuals All individuals registered as unemployed and receiving UI benefits in Denmark on March 17, 2019. source

Managing Inequality: Manager-Specific Wage Premiums and Selection in the Managerial Labor Market

Alex Xi He and Daniel le Maire · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual + firm

Builds a manager-firm-worker matched dataset for the whole Danish population to estimate manager effects on wages. Manager-specific premiums are almost as important as firm premiums for between-firm wage inequality, are unrelated to productivity, and high-paying managers are more likely to be replaced after acquisitions.

Data
Matched employer-employee data covering all Danish workers and firms (IDA), Occupation codes used to identify managers, Firm balance sheet data for private-sector firms (FIRE), Mergers and acquisitions identified from firm and establishment identifiers
Key variables
  • Outcome: worker log wages
  • Key estimate: manager-specific wage premiums (manager fixed effects identified from worker and manager mobility)
  • Comparison: firm-specific wage premiums
  • Event: firm acquisition and manager replacement
  • Heterogeneity: industry concentration (HHI)
Period
1995–2011 (per 2019 working-paper slides)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Manager-firm-worker matched panel covering the entire Danish population; acquisitions sample of about 3,700 private-sector acquisitions in the 2019 working-paper version. source (abstract)

Changing Tracks: Human Capital Investment after Loss of Ability

Anders Humlum, Jakob R. Munch and Pernille Plato · American Economic Review, 2025 · Individual

Uses quasi-random work accidents in Danish administrative data to show that injured workers enrol in bachelor's programmes that build on their previous training. Exploiting differences in how stackable degrees are, higher education moves injured workers from disability benefits into full-time jobs paying 25 percent more than before injury, and reskilling subsidies pay for themselves four times over.

Data
Industrial Injury Register (Arbejdsskaderegisteret) / workers' compensation claims from AES, National Patient Register (LPR), Health Insurance Register (Sygesikringsregisteret), Prescription Drug Database (LMDB), Education Register (UDDA), Course Participant Register (VEUV), Danish Register for Evaluation of Marginalization (DREAM), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), linked to O*NET, Population register (BEF/POP)
Key variables
  • Treatment: work accident causing loss of earning capacity (vs. matched non-injured workers)
  • Outcome: enrollment in and completion of formal degrees and courses
  • Outcome: earnings, hours and occupation (task content)
  • Outcome: disability and other social transfers
  • Severity: assessed earning capacity loss and personal impairment
Period
1995–2017 (work injury claims 1998–2017)
Sample
14,481 injury events (14,369 distinct individuals); 4,568 events (4,528 individuals) for workers with vocational degrees with access to higher education Full-time employed Danish workers (excluding military) who suffered an accepted physical work accident with positive earning capacity loss, compared with matched non-injured workers. source

No Pain, No Gain: Work Demand, Work Effort, and Worker Health

David Hummels, Jakob R. Munch and Chong Xiang · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual + firm

Links Danish worker-firm data to hospital and prescription records to study how rising workload affects health. Increases in firm sales lead workers to log longer hours and raise the probability of stress, depression, heart disease and stroke, most for older and already strained workers; the welfare loss from sickness offsets nearly a quarter of the earnings gain.

Data
Firm Statistics Register (FIRM), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-worker link (FIDA), Accounts Statistics (Regnskabsstatistik), Prodcom firm sales by product (VARS), Health care registers: prescription drug purchases, doctor visits and hospitalizations (National Patient Register), Monthly payroll records with earnings and hours (eIndkomst/BFL), Monthly firm VAT sales and material purchases (FIKS)
Key variables
  • Treatment: log firm sales (Prodcom), with a Bartik/shift-share instrument in robustness checks
  • Outcome: monthly hours worked and earnings
  • Outcome: stress/depression indicators (anti-depressant use, psychiatrist visits, burnout hospitalizations)
  • Outcome: heart disease drugs, heart attack/stroke and alcoholism hospitalizations
  • Fixed effects: job-spell fixed effects
Period
1995–2012 (health data 1996–2012; monthly payroll data 2008–2012)
Sample
3,336,244 annual worker-year observations; 8,834,846 monthly observations Full-time workers aged 20–60 in private-sector Danish firms in the Prodcom sales data with at least 50 employees and positive imports and exports, excluding single-year employment relationships. source

Micro versus Macro Labor Supply Elasticities: The Role of Dynamic Returns to Effort

Henrik Kleven, Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Kristian Larsen and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · American Economic Review, 2025 · Individual

Builds a model in which effort today raises earnings tomorrow, so responses to taxes are delayed and mediated by job switches. Danish administrative data confirm the model's life-cycle predictions for earnings and hours, and a quasi-experimental design based on job switchers finds a long-run earnings elasticity around 0.5, against a short-run elasticity of about 0.2.

Data
Linked administrative registers on earnings, occupation (DISCO), firm and demographics, full population, Employer-employee matched data (firm x occupation job cells), ATP pension contribution-based hours measure (1980–2018), Monthly uncapped hours measure (from 2008), Income tax records (2009 tax reform variation)
Key variables
  • Outcome: earnings (log earnings changes)
  • Treatment: 2009 tax reform change in marginal net-of-tax rate
  • Instrument: simulated net-of-tax rate change at baseline income
  • Mediator: job switches (firm x occupation cell), incl. mass-layoff switches
  • Outcome: hours worked
Period
1980–2018
Sample
N = 2,501,126 and 2,428,411 in the main elasticity regressions (Table 1) Full Danish population; tax-reform analysis uses taxpayers with baseline income between 250,000 and 1,000,000 DKK. source

Permanent Residency and Refugee Immigrants' Skill Investment

Jacob Nielsen Arendt, Christian Dustmann and Hyejin Ku · Journal of Labor Economics, 2025 · Individual

A Danish reform tightened the employment and language requirements refugees must meet to obtain permanent residency. Rather than encouraging investment, it lowered overall employment and barely moved language proficiency, because refugees with weak pre-reform prospects reduced effort when the requirements looked out of reach.

Data
Population, income, education and immigration registers linked via the Central Person Registry, Danish language test results, Employment in annual full-time equivalents (register data)
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual employment in full-time equivalents
  • Outcome: passing the Danish language test at intermediate or higher level
  • Treatment: temporary residency in Jan–Oct 2004 (exposed to the 2007 permanent-residency reform) vs Jan–Oct 2003
  • Heterogeneity: pre-reform employment or language performance type
  • Time: years since temporary residency (YSR 1–7)
Period
Residency cohorts 2003–2004, followed seven years since temporary residency (to 2011)
Sample
767 unique individuals; 5,122 person-year observations Refugees aged 18–64 who applied for asylum after 28 February 2002 and received temporary residency in Jan–Oct 2003 (control) or Jan–Oct 2004 (treatment). source

Healthy at Work? Evidence from a Social Experimental Evaluation of a Firm-Based Wellness Program

Marianne Simonsen and Lars Skipper · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2025 · Individual + firm

A large social experiment across worksites, followed for up to twelve years in the registers, evaluates an employer-sponsored health programme. Employees at treated worksites see fewer GP visits and buy fewer prescription drugs for up to seven years, with no change in hospitalisations, absence or turnover and some spillovers within the family.

Data
Healthy-at-Work randomised experiment records (work-unit assignment, September 2008), Primary care physician consultation records (Statistics Denmark registers), Prescription drug purchase records, Hospital contact / hospitalisation records, Employment, sickness absence and turnover registers, Socio-economic background registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: work-unit randomised to the Healthy-at-Work wellness program
  • Outcome: primary care physician visits
  • Outcome: prescription drug purchases (any, months supply)
  • Outcome: hospitalisations (somatic, psychiatric)
  • Outcome: sickness absence and turnover
  • Spillover outcome: spouses' health care use
Period
Randomisation 2008; follow-up to 2020 (PCP visits and work outcomes), 2019 (prescriptions), 2018 (hospitalisations)
Sample
7,541 workers (3,735 treatment, 3,806 control) in 314 work-units Elderly-care health workers in 314 work-units of 110 employers across 11 Danish municipalities enrolled in the Healthy-at-Work experiment. source

Do Medical Treatments Work for Work? Evidence from Breast Cancer Patients

N. Meltem Daysal, William N. Evans, Mikkel Hasse Pedersen and Mircea Trandafir · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2025 · Individual

A Danish guideline change shifted which breast cancer patients received radiation therapy, giving quasi-experimental variation in treatment. Radiotherapy reproduces the survival gains found in trials and has economic returns: ten years after diagnosis it raises employment by 37 percent and earnings by 45 percent, undoing most of the post-diagnosis employment drop.

Data
Danish Breast Cancer Cooperative Group (DBCG) clinical Breast Cancer Database, Register of Causes of Death, Register-Based Labour Force Statistics (RAS), Income Statistics Register, DREAM weekly transfer register, Population Register (BEF), Education Register
Key variables
  • Treatment: receipt of radiotherapy
  • Instrument: January 1995 DBCG guideline change expanding radiotherapy eligibility (diagnosed after 1995 x eligible group)
  • Outcome: all-cause and breast cancer mortality
  • Outcome: employment, annual labour earnings, gross income
  • Outcome: sickness and disability benefit receipt
Period
1990–2008 (diagnoses 1990–1998)
Sample
2,823 observations (women) High-risk pre-menopausal women under 55 diagnosed with breast cancer in Denmark 1990–1998 and enrolled in the DBCG89 clinical trial. source

Grades and Employer Learning

Anne Toft Hansen, Ulrik Hvidman and Hans Henrik Sievertsen · Journal of Labor Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses a nationwide rescaling of Danish university grades to identify the return to GPA that is unrelated to ability. A reform-induced higher GPA raises earnings right after graduation, especially for graduates with fewer other signals, but the effect fades within a few years, consistent with employers learning quickly about productivity.

Data
University student records (Aarhus University and University of Copenhagen) with grades before and after the 2007 recoding, Administrative registers from Statistics Denmark (background, parental income and education, earnings)
Key variables
  • Outcome: log total gross earnings in each of the first five calendar years after graduation
  • Treatment: reform-induced change in GPA from the 1 August 2007 recoding to the new grading scale
  • Outcome: indicator for positive earnings
  • Controls: gender, age at graduation, high school GPA, parental income, employment and education
Period
Students enrolled on 1 August 2007 who graduated before 2011; earnings for the first five years after graduation
Sample
3,813 students Master's students at Aarhus University or University of Copenhagen enrolled on 1 August 2007 with at most 40 ECTS remaining who graduated before 2011. source

Public Pensions and Private Savings

Esteban García-Miralles and Jonathan M. Leganza · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

A Danish reform raising the social security eligibility age, studied with a regression discontinuity in administrative data, increased contributions to personal and employer pension accounts when later eligibility kept people working longer. Inertia in previous savings behaviour and firms' default contribution rates are the key mechanisms.

Data
Population registers (date of birth, demographics), Labour-market and income/asset registers (tax-reported bank and stock balances, property values, pension contributions), Monthly earnings register (eIncome / eIndkomst), Voluntary early retirement pension (VERP) contribution and benefit records
Key variables
  • Running variable: birth date relative to 1 January 1954 cutoff
  • Treatment: higher pension eligibility ages from 2011 reform
  • Outcome: contributions to employer-sponsored and personal retirement accounts
  • Outcome: changes in bank and stock account balances and property values
  • Outcome: pre-tax earnings and monthly retirement age
Period
1985–2018 (annual); 2008–2017 (monthly)
Sample
40,042 individuals (analysis sample); 12,020 individuals (RD estimation sample) Non-self-employed Danes born within six months of 1 January 1954 who regularly contributed to the VERP early retirement scheme before the reform. source

Joint Retirement of Couples: Evidence from Discontinuities in Denmark

Esteban García-Miralles and Jonathan M. Leganza · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses age discontinuities in eligibility for Danish early retirement and public pensions to show that when one spouse becomes eligible and retires, the other spouse is substantially more likely to retire as well, quantifying spillovers that matter for evaluating retirement reforms.

Data
Danish administrative registers for the full population 1986–2014 (labour market outcomes, pension benefits, socio-demographics, family/couple links), Annual taxable earnings registers, Monthly earnings records for all employees (eIndkomst/BFL) and monthly pension benefit records, from 2008, Voluntary early retirement pension (VERP) contribution records
Key variables
  • Outcome: spouse's retirement (ceasing to earn labour income)
  • Outcome: pension claiming (VERP or OAP) and annual labour earnings
  • Running variable (age-based design): reference individual's age relative to early pension eligibility age 60
  • Running variable (reform-based design): birth date relative to 1 January 1954 cutoff of the 2011 reform raising VERP eligibility age by 6 months
Period
1986–2014 (age-based design 1991–2013; reform-based design 2008–2014)
Sample
367,585 couples and 2,206,044 couple-year observations (age-based sample); 10,321 couples and 73,395 couple-year observations (reform-based sample) Dual-earner Danish couples (married, registered partners or cohabiting) up to 8 years apart in age, with the reference individual aged 57–60, excluding self-employed and disability recipients. source

The Long-Run Effects of Individual Debt Relief

Gustaf Bruze, Alexander Kjær Hilsløv and Jonas Maibom · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

Links court records on the Danish debt relief programme to the registers and uses quasi-random assignment of applicants to court trustees with different admission rates. Debt relief raises earned income, employment, assets, home ownership and wealth for more than 25 years, with the move into employment accounting for two thirds of the income gain.

Data
Hand-collected debt relief announcements from the official gazette Statstidende (court records), 1984–2005, Central Person Register (CPR) for linkage, Income Tax Register: earned income, taxable assets and debt, real estate ownership, Labour market status registers (employment status in November), Hourly wage measure from Statistics Denmark
Key variables
  • Treatment: being granted court-ordered debt relief
  • Instrument: court trustee admission rate (judge/trustee leniency design)
  • Outcome: earned income and employment
  • Outcome: taxable assets, secured and unsecured debt, home ownership, wealth
Period
Applications 1984–2003; register outcomes 1980–2019
Sample
46,571 individuals (final sample); 32,794 observations in the IV sample with 515 trustee identifiers All applicants for debt relief in Danish City Courts from 1984 to 2003, linked to national registers. source

Untying the Knot: How Child Support and Alimony Affect Couples' Dynamic Decisions and Welfare

Hanno Foerster · Review of Economic Studies, 2024 · Individual

Estimates a dynamic model of family labour supply, home production, saving and divorce on Danish register and survey data to study post-marital maintenance payments. Alimony carries larger labour supply disincentives and insures consumption less efficiently than child support, so welfare rises when child support is increased and alimony reduced.

Data
Danish register data on income, labour force status and hours worked, Marital histories and birth register (BEF/family links), Maintenance (child support and alimony) payment records and child residence after divorce, Danish Time Use Survey 2001 (DTUS)
Key variables
  • Outcome: labour supply (employer-recorded hours bins) and annual labour income
  • Outcome: divorce
  • Policy: child support and alimony (maintenance) payments
  • Survey: housework hours and relative consumption in couples (DTUS)
Period
1980–2013
Sample
322,732 couples (645,464 individuals) and 4,312,826 couple-year observations; DTUS 2,105 households Danish couples in their first marriage, both spouses aged 25–58, at least one working, excluding couples with children from previous relationships. source

The Gender Application Gap: Do Men and Women Apply for the Same Jobs?

Jonas Fluchtmann, Anita M. Glenny, Nikolaj A. Harmon and Jonas Maibom · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

Uses administrative records of the job applications made by every Danish unemployment insurance recipient. Men and women apply to very different job types even when observationally similar, and these differences in applications account for more than 70 percent of the residual gender wage gap.

Data
Joblog job application records from the public employment service platform (Jobnet), Unemployment insurance payment records / DREAM event-history data, Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Employment Statistics for Employees, monthly earnings and hours (BFL)
Key variables
  • Outcome: characteristics of applied-for jobs (occupation, industry, AKM firm wage premium, commute, hours)
  • Group: gender
  • Outcome: hourly wage in new job one month after hire
  • Predicted 'typical wage' of applied-for jobs
Period
September 2015–September 2017
Sample
105,879 individuals, 114,375 UI spells, 2,911,585 job applications Danish UI recipients entering new spells from September 2015 to September 2017 lasting at least 8 weeks, with at least 4 registered applications and finding a job within one year. source

Unemployed Job Search across People and over Time: Evidence from Applied-For Jobs

Jonas Fluchtmann, Anita M. Glenny, Nikolaj A. Harmon and Jonas Maibom · Journal of Labor Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses the logs of job applications made by Danish unemployment insurance recipients to document how the jobs people apply for differ across job seekers and change over an unemployment spell, and what these patterns imply for models of directed search.

Data
Joblog applied-for job records (Danish Agency for Labour Market and Recruitment), DREAM event-history register of public transfers, Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Employment statistics for employees with hours and earnings (BFL), Firm purchases data (FIKS), Auxiliary survey of UI recipients on application coverage, O*NET Related Occupation Matrix

  • Outcome: share of applied-for jobs that are full-time
  • Outcome: scope of search index (commute time, related occupation, related industry)
  • Outcome: share of applications to publicly posted vacancies
  • Time variable: month of unemployment spell
Period
September 2015–September 2017
Sample
127,695 individuals (spells) and over 4 million applied-for jobs Individuals of Danish origin starting UI spells between September 2015 and September 2017, eligible for the full two years of benefits, with spells of at least 8 weeks and at least one registered application. source

Income Effects and Labour Supply: Evidence from a Child Benefits Reform

Mathias Fjællegaard Jensen and Jack Blundell · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

A 2011 Danish cap on child benefits cut payments to families with several young children and left smaller families unaffected. Mothers' labour supply rises substantially after the cut, mainly on the extensive margin, and sterilisation records are used to control for differences in preferences for more children.

Data
Population register (BEF) for family composition, marital status and cohabitation, Monthly payroll records with hours worked (BFL/eIndkomst), Child benefit payment records (BOBO/BOTI), Register OF (monthly demographics and parental leave status), Sterilisation consultations from the National Patient Register (LPRADM) and health insurance service registers (SYSI, SSSY), Annual income register (IND) for self-employment robustness
Key variables
  • Treatment: 2011 cap on child benefits (DKK 35,000) reducing unearned income for larger families with young children
  • Outcome: monthly hours worked (intensive and extensive margins)
  • Outcome: annual earnings (robustness incl. self-employed)
  • Control/restriction: family sterilisation consultation before the reform
  • Design: difference-in-differences, treated vs unaffected families
Period
September 2008–December 2014 (monthly)
Sample
28,968 treated women and 22,477 treated men Native Danish married or cohabiting parents aged 25–59 over the window with 2–4 children (at least two under 18) in January 2011, not self-employed, observed in a balanced monthly panel. source

Mette Foged, Linea Hasager and Giovanni Peri · Journal of Labor Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses quasi-experimental variation from several Danish reforms to compare, in a common framework, the long-run employment and earnings effects of language training, active labour market programmes and benefit cuts for refugees, and ranks the policies by their effects and costs.

Language Training and Refugees' Integration

Mette Foged, Linea Hasager, Giovanni Peri, Jacob Nielsen Arendt and Iben Bolvig · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

A 1999 reform improved language training for newly recognised refugees. A regression discontinuity around the cut-off date shows a permanent, positive effect on earnings that emerges after classes are completed, together with more schooling and a higher probability of working in complex jobs.

Data

  • Outcome: annual earnings and employment
  • Outcome: education, occupation task complexity, crime
  • Outcome (children): lower secondary completion and crime
Period
Admissions 1997–2000; followed until 2016 (children's outcomes until 2018)
Sample
8,558 individuals; children sample of 9,730 Refugees and reunified family members aged 18–49 granted asylum in Denmark between January 1, 1997 and December 31, 2000. source

New Evidence on Welfare's Disincentive for the Youth Using Administrative Panel Data

Olivier B. Bargain and Anders B. Jonassen · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

Maximum social assistance for childless unmarried Danes rose by 55 percent at age 25 between 2000 and 2006. Only the low-skilled respond: employment falls by 2 to 3 percent and benefit take-up rises by 10 to 14 percent, two thirds through moves from work to welfare, and crime shows no discontinuity.

Data
Danish administrative panel data on welfare benefit take-up, employment, earnings and crime (registers not named in abstract)
Key variables
  • Running variable: age relative to 25th birthday
  • Treatment: 55% increase in maximum social assistance at age 25 for childless unmarried individuals
  • Outcome: employment
  • Outcome: welfare benefit take-up
  • Outcome: criminal activity
Period
2000–2006
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Childless unmarried young people in Denmark around age 25, with effects concentrated among the low-skilled. source (abstract)

How Do Households Respond to Job Loss? Lessons from Multiple High-Frequency Datasets

Asger Lau Andersen, Amalie Sofie Jensen, Niels Johannesen, Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Søren Leth-Petersen and Adam Sheridan · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2023 · Individual

Combines bank transaction data with government registers to measure every margin of self-insurance after job loss. Over two years households cut spending by 30 percent of the income loss and cover about half of it by running down liquid balances; spousal labour supply, private transfers, home equity extraction and consumer credit contribute less.

Data
Danske Bank customer transaction and account records (spending, income inflows, balances, loans), Monthly payroll and income transfer records (BFL/eIndkomst) from the Danish Tax Agency, Population register (BEF) household structure and spouse links, End-of-year account-level bank loan and deposit records from the Danish Tax Agency, Loan-level mortgage data from mortgage banks (via Statistics Denmark), Mass layoff notifications to the Danish Agency for Labour Market and Recruitment, Annual income and wealth, education, home ownership and occupation registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: job loss event (main employer wage payments cease, followed by unemployment benefits)
  • Outcome: household spending (card, mobile, bills, ATM withdrawals)
  • Outcome: net saving in liquid assets, non-mortgage debt repayment, mortgage refinancing and equity extraction
  • Outcome: spousal labour income, private transfers, government transfers
  • Design: monthly event study from 18 months before to 24 months after job loss
  • Subsample: involuntary job loss from mass layoffs
Period
January 2009–December 2016 (job losses July 2010–December 2015)
Sample
10,002 individuals (baseline sample of active bank customers); gross sample of 66,844 job losers Danish individuals born 1950–1979 who lost their job between July 2010 and December 2015 after at least 18 months of stable earnings, in stable households that are active customers of Danske Bank. source

Network Connections and Board Seats: Are Female Networks Less Valuable?

Emma von Essen and Nina Smith · Journal of Labor Economics, 2023 · Individual + firm

Uses Danish employer-employee data from 1995 to 2011 to study how professional networks affect first appointments to supervisory boards. Larger networks raise the chance of a board seat, more so for men, because men's networks connect them more often to larger and listed firms and to other men.

Matched employer-employee data for the Danish labour market

  • Explanatory: size of professional network
  • Heterogeneity: gender
  • Network composition: connections to large/listed firms, to men, and to incumbent directors

Estimating Labor Supply Responses to Danish Tax Reforms

Hans Schytte Sigaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2023 · Individual

Applies taxable income elasticity methods to register data on hours actually worked. The intensive-margin labour supply elasticity is about 0.08, close to the labour income and taxable income elasticities, and it comes from changes in contract hours, paid absence and switches between main and secondary jobs.

Data
Wage Statistics Register (Lønstatistikken, LON) for performed hours of work, Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDAN) for workplace controls, Income registers (IND, INDK, INPI) for marginal tax rate simulation, Population register (BEF) and family income register (FAIN), Family register (FAMILIE), Education register (UDDA), Danish tax simulation programs (Kleven and Schultz 2014, extended by the Ministry of Finance)
Key variables
  • Outcome: three-year log change in annual performed hours of work
  • Treatment: three-year log change in the marginal net-of-tax rate on labour income
  • Instrument: mechanical (simulated) change in marginal tax rate from tax reforms, using lagged income
  • Secondary outcomes: labour income and taxable income
  • Controls: income splines, socio-economic and municipality controls
Period
2002–2016
Sample
10,755,733 observations Employed wage earners aged 25–65 covered by the Wage Statistics Register with positive hours in both years of each three-year difference and base-year labour income above DKK 250,000. source

The Anatomy of Sorting: Evidence from Danish Data

Rasmus Lentz, Suphanit Piyapromdee and Jean-Marc Robin · Econometrica, 2023 · Individual + firm

Estimates a flexible model of worker and firm heterogeneity in wages and job mobility on Danish matched employer-employee data. Workers and firms sort positively, with sorting operating through both wages and non-wage amenities, and worker heterogeneity accounts for the bulk of wage dispersion.

Data
Matched employer-employee register data (IDA) with annual wages, Weekly labour market spell/mobility data built from Statistics Denmark registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: log wage rate
  • Job-to-job mobility and transitions to and from non-employment (weekly)
  • Latent worker and firm types (finite mixture, classification EM)
  • Controls: gender, education, potential experience, tenure, calendar time
  • Sorting channels: job preferences, segmentation, layoffs, job finding
Period
1985–2013 (analysis panel starts 1987)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish workers after completing education, employment spells starting before age 50, followed weekly across employers from 1987 to 2013 (read from the 2018 predecessor working paper). source

The Disparate Effects of Information Provision: A Field Experiment on the Work Incentives of Social Welfare

Sofie Cairo and Robert Mahlstedt · Journal of Public Economics, 2023 · Individual

A field experiment in Denmark informed welfare recipients about a minimum work requirement, either with personalised running counts of their hours or with generic rules. Personalised information raised employment among those below the required hours, while generic notices reduced labour supply among those at low near-term risk of sanctions.

Data
Field experiment assignment and messaging data (Danish public employment service, jobnet.dk online tool), Population registers (socio-demographic background), Benefit payment records (DREAM), Monthly income and employment records (eIndkomst/BFL), Public employment service data on sanctions, exemptions, collected working hours, click data and registered job applications
Key variables
  • Treatment: random assignment to personalised online information tool about benefit-reduction risk under the work requirement (three arms)
  • Outcome: employment and earnings
  • Outcome: welfare benefit receipt and exit to alternative income support
  • Outcome: working hours collected toward the requirement and job applications
  • Heterogeneity: job search activity and sanction risk
Period
Intervention from 15 August 2018 with up to five monthly reminders
Sample
47,294 individuals All Danish welfare benefit recipients on 15 August 2018 subject to the 225-hour work requirement who had received the official notification letter. source

Trade Shocks, Firm Hierarchies, and Wage Inequality

Benjamin U. Friedrich · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2022 · Individual + firm

Shows that trade shocks change within-firm wage inequality by changing firm hierarchies, using both firm-level foreign demand shocks and the Muslim boycott of Danish exports after the 2006 cartoon crisis. Adding a hierarchy layer widens pay gaps throughout the firm, and delayering after the boycott compresses wages through cuts, demotions and turnover.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) with DISCO occupation codes, Firm-Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (FIDA), Danish Business Register / firm statistics (GF): sales, value added, industry, Foreign Trade Statistics Register: firm-level exports by CN8 product and destination (UHDI), UN Comtrade bilateral trade flows (HS6), U.S. Imports of Merchandise, Penn World Tables distances, Brent oil prices (transport cost estimation)
Key variables
  • Outcome: within-firm wage inequality (std. dev. of log wages, 90-10 gap)
  • Treatment: number of hierarchy layers based on occupations
  • Instrument: firm-specific world import demand and transport-cost shocks
  • Natural experiment: 2006 Muslim boycott of Danish exports (Cartoon crisis)
Period
1999–2008
Sample
68,090 firm-year observations on 12,470 firms (within-firm inequality regressions) Universe of pre-existing private-sector exporting firms in Denmark, 1999–2008. source

Constraints on Hours within the Firm

Claudio Labanca and Dario Pozzoli · Journal of Labor Economics, 2022 · Individual + firm

Uses Danish linked employer-employee data with hours and tax-rate variation from the 2010 Danish tax reform. Employees in firms with tight constraints on working hours do not adjust their hours when their taxes change, while employees in more flexible firms respond both directly and through spillovers from their coworkers.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Wage statistics with annual regular and overtime hours (Lønstatistikken, LON), Firm Statistics Register (Firmstat), Danish Foreign Trade Statistics Register, Firm-Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (FIDA), Danish Tax Register (income tax records), O*NET occupational data
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual hours worked (regular plus overtime)
  • Treatment: change in simulated marginal tax rate from the 2010 Danish tax reform (2008–2011 interval)
  • Moderator: firm-level hours constraints/coordination measured by within-firm dispersion of hours
  • Hourly wage: annual earnings over total hours
  • Spillovers: tax changes of coworkers
Period
2003–2011
Sample
more than 400,000 employees and approximately 8,300 firms (787,683 individual-year observations) Full-time employees aged 15–65 in private firms where hours are reported for at least 95% of the workforce, 2003–2011 (working-paper version 'Coordination of Hours within the Firm', IZA DP 12062). source

Globalization, Gender, and the Family

Wolfgang Keller and Hale Utar · Review of Economic Studies, 2022 · Individual + firm

Men and women hit by the same import shock from China adjust differently. Women in their late thirties who are displaced respond by having a child, especially high earners and those who would need to retrain, so a gender-neutral trade shock produces a long-run gender gap through the biological clock.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Central Population Register (CPR/BEF) for births, marriages, divorces and cohabitation, Domestic production database by 10-digit product (Industrial commodity statistics, VARS) to identify firms making quota goods, Industry-level imports from China and other advanced countries (instrument), Parental leave records
Key variables
  • Outcome: new births, marriage, divorce, parental leave
  • Outcome: earnings, hours, unemployment
  • Treatment: 1999 employer produces goods subject to MFA quota removal for China after WTO entry
  • Treatment (economy-wide): industry import penetration from China, instrumented with other countries' imports
  • Heterogeneity: gender and age (biological clock)
Period
1999–2007
Sample
about 1,600,000 workers; about 10,000 textile and clothing workers The 1999 cohort of full-time Danish private-sector workers aged 18–56, followed through 2007. source

The Returns to Nursing: Evidence from a Parental-Leave Program

Benjamin U. Friedrich and Martin B. Hackmann · Review of Economic Studies, 2021 · Individual + firm

A Danish parental-leave programme offering up to a year off per child aged 0 to 8 induced a nurse shortage that hospitals and nursing homes could not replace. Nurse employment fell by 15 percent in hospitals and 10 percent in nursing homes, 30-day readmissions of heart attack patients rose, and nursing home mortality increased sharply.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) for health workers, plants and industries, Population register (BEF) for children's ages, residence and household characteristics, Social benefits records of parental leave spells (from 1995), National Patient Register (LPR), inpatient hospitalisations 1990–2000, Register of Causes of Death (DAR), 1991–2000
Key variables
  • Treatment: 1994 parental leave programme; exposure = share of nurses eligible times estimated take-up, by health sector and county
  • Outcome: nurse employment in hospitals and nursing homes
  • Outcome: 30-day hospital readmission rates and other quality-of-care measures
  • Outcome: nursing home (elderly) mortality
  • Eligibility: having a youngest child aged 8 or younger
Period
1990–2000
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish nurses, nursing assistants and doctors, and hospital patients and nursing home residents, aggregated to health sector by county, 1990–2000. source

Does Biology Drive Child Penalties? Evidence from Biological and Adoptive Families

Henrik Kleven, Camille Landais and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · AER: Insights, 2021 · Individual

Compares event-study child penalties for biological and adoptive mothers using almost 40 years of Danish adoption data. Short-run penalties are slightly larger for biological mothers, but long-run penalties are virtually identical, so biology is not a key driver of child-related gender gaps.

Data
Statistics Denmark administrative registers covering the full population 1980–2017, linked with family identifiers, Adoption registry of Statistics Denmark (1988–2009), augmented with foreign adoptions identified via country of origin and Central Person Register immigration records, Earnings and labour-supply registers incl. hours from the ATP mandated pension scheme, Education registers
Key variables
  • Event: arrival of first child (by birth or adoption)
  • Outcome: annual earnings (wages, salaries, self-employment)
  • Outcome: labour force participation, hours worked, wage rates
  • Comparison group: biological vs adoptive families (biological sample reweighted to match adoptive families)
Period
1980–2017
Sample
around 527,000 first births (biological sample) and around 4,600 first arrivals (adoptive sample) Danish parents whose first child arrived by birth or foreign adoption (before age 5) between 1985 and 2007, with both parents known, alive and resident in Denmark from 5 years before to 10 years after. source

Family Labor Supply Responses to Severe Health Shocks: Evidence from Danish Administrative Records

Itzik Fadlon and Torben Heien Nielsen · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2021 · Individual

Compares households hit by a fatal versus a non-fatal health shock at the same time. After a spouse's death the surviving spouse raises labour supply to self-insure, while after a non-fatal shock spouses reduce labour supply to care for the patient. Children of the household adjust less.

Data
Cause of Death Registry, National Patient Registry (LPR) hospitalisation records with ICD diagnoses, Income and transfer registers (earnings, government transfers, pension and annuity payouts, capital income), 1980–2011, Third-party reported liquid wealth balances, 1984–2011, Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), incl. ATP-based full-/part-time employment, Household linkages of spouses and cohabiting partners (population registers)
Key variables
  • Treatment: spousal death (fatal health shock) or first non-fatal heart attack/stroke
  • Control group: households experiencing the same shock a few years later
  • Outcome: surviving/healthy spouse's labour force participation and earnings
  • Outcome: household income from transfers, pensions and insurance; liquid wealth
  • Heterogeneity: household liquidity, income loss, age
Period
1980–2011 (shocks 1985–2011)
Sample
310,720 treated and 409,190 control households (fatal shocks); 37,437 treated and 54,887 control households (non-fatal heart attacks and strokes) Danish married and cohabiting couples with both spouses aged 45–80 in the year of the (actual or placebo) shock; the non-fatal shock sample is limited to spouses under 60 surviving at least three years. source

Estimation of a Roy/Search/Compensating Differential Model of the Labor Market

Christopher Taber and Rune Vejlin · Econometrica, 2020 · Individual + firm

Combines a Roy model, search frictions, compensating differentials and on-the-job human capital accumulation in one framework, establishes what is non-parametrically identified, and estimates it on Danish matched data. Pre-market skills account for most wage variation, while preferences for non-wage job attributes and search frictions matter greatly for turnover and for which jobs workers choose.

Data
Weekly labour market spell data built from Statistics Denmark registers (all individuals aged 15–74), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) with hourly wage measure, Danish Work Environment Cohort Study surveys (1995 and 2000) on reasons for job separation
Key variables

  • Job-to-job transitions between establishments (revealed preference)
  • Employment and non-employment spell durations
  • Worker skill/education group and labour market experience
  • Decomposition of wage variance into skill, search, compensating differential and human capital components
Period
1985–2003
Sample
2,969,454 workers, 25,630,532 spells and 467,983 establishments (estimation sample) Danish workers after labour market entry and up to age 55, followed in weekly spell data across establishments, 1985–2003. source

Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Daniel Reck and Peer Ebbesen Skov · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2020 · Individual

Danish minimum wages jump by 40 percent at age 18. Monthly payroll records for the whole population show employment falling by 33 percent and hours by 45 percent at the discontinuity, leaving total wage payments almost unchanged, with an implied employment elasticity between 0.6 and 1.1.

  • Outcome: monthly earnings
Period

Sample

The Opportunity Costs of Mandatory Military Service: Evidence from a Draft Lottery

Paul Bingley, Petter Lundborg and Stéphanie Vincent Lyk-Jensen · Journal of Labor Economics, 2020 · Individual

Uses the Danish draft lottery to estimate the effect of conscription on later outcomes. Military service delays education and labour market entry, and the paper quantifies the resulting losses in schooling and earnings for those who serve.
