152,269 men without missing earnings (military register covers 155,570 fit-for-service conscripts) Danish men born 1976–1983 declared fit for military service who drew a draft lottery number at the Armed Forces Day. source

Consumption Network Effects

Giacomo De Giorgi, Anders Frederiksen and Luigi Pistaferri · Review of Economic Studies, 2019 · Individual + firm

Measures household consumption from Danish tax records on income and assets and defines peer groups by workplace using matched employer-employee data. Instrumenting peers' consumption with shocks to their own non-overlapping networks and firms, the paper finds sizeable consumption spillovers that can generate macro multipliers, operating through both intertemporal and intratemporal channels.

Data
Administrative income and wealth tax records (tax registry incl. wealth tax data), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) for plant IDs and coworker networks, Danish Expenditure Survey (DES) 1994–1996
Key variables
  • Outcome: household consumption imputed from income and change in assets
  • Endogenous regressor: average consumption of coworkers' households (peer consumption)
  • Instruments: firm-level shocks to distance-3 (non-overlapping) network members
  • Network definition: coworkers in the same plant
  • Mechanisms: budget shares of visible goods (DES)
Period
1980–1996
Sample
757,439 households Danish households with head aged 18–65 where both spouses are working employees, linked to spouses' coworkers. source

Children and Gender Inequality: Evidence from Denmark

Henrik Kleven, Camille Landais and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2019 · Individual

Event studies around the birth of the first child show mothers' earnings fall about 20 percent relative to fathers' in the long run, through hours, participation and wage rates. These child penalties now account for most of the remaining gender earnings gap and are transmitted across generations through mothers' own upbringing.

Data
Full-population Danish administrative registers linked by personal ID (earnings, labour supply, occupation, firm, education), 1980–2013, Family and generational links (population register), ATP pension contributions as third-party measure of hours worked, Additional administrative data back to 1964 for intergenerational analysis, Matched employer-employee data (firm family-friendliness), International Social Survey Program (ISSP) 2002 for gender attitudes
Key variables
  • Treatment/event: birth of first child (event time relative to first birth)
  • Outcome: earnings, labour force participation, hours worked (ATP), wage rates
  • Outcome: occupation rank, sector, firm family-friendliness
  • Child penalty: percentage drop of women's relative to men's earnings
  • Intergenerational: maternal grandparents' labour supply
Period
1980–2013 (with data back to 1964 for intergenerational transmission); first births 1985–2003
Sample
around 470,000 births or 15,040,000 individual-year observations Danish parents with a first child born 1985–2003 who are observed alive and resident in Denmark from 5 years before to 10 years after the birth. source

Labor Supply and Optimization Frictions: Evidence from the Danish Student Labor Market

Jakob Egholt Søgaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2019 · Individual

Danish students lose part of their study grant when annual earnings exceed a threshold. Register data show bunching below the threshold that grows as students gain experience with the rule, so optimisation frictions hide a structural elasticity well above the observed response.

Data
Danish administrative registers for the full population (education, earnings, benefits, demographics), Annual income tax returns, Monthly student benefit (SU) take-up records from the Student Benefit Administration, Monthly payroll records of earnings and hours (BFL/eIndkomst), from 2008
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual labour earnings relative to the student benefit income limit (bunching)
  • Incentive: kink/notch from phase-out (payback) of student grants above the individual income limit
  • Simulated individual marginal tax and phase-out rates
  • Monthly earnings and benefit take-up (timing of adjustment)
Period
2006–2011 (monthly earnings from 2008)
Sample
around 75,000 observations per year (core sample 75,500 individuals per year on average) Individuals aged 18–35 enrolled in tertiary education, fully eligible for student benefits and with positive labour earnings (above DKK 8,000 per year). source

An Empirical Model of Wage Dispersion with Sorting

Jesper Bagger and Rasmus Lentz · Review of Economic Studies, 2019 · Individual + firm

Estimates an equilibrium search model with endogenous search intensity and two-sided heterogeneity on Danish matched data. Finds positive assortative matching between worker and firm productivity, and quantifies how much of wage dispersion is due to sorting versus search frictions.

Data
Weekly labour market spell data from administrative registers (jobs reported to tax authorities, public transfers, mandatory pension contributions), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA): education, hourly wages and firm sector
Key variables
  • Outcome: hourly wage (last week of November)
  • Key variable: job-to-job transitions and unemployment spells
  • Estimated: worker skill and firm productivity types (sorting)
  • Decomposition: log-wage variance into worker heterogeneity, firm heterogeneity, frictions and sorting
Period
1994–2003 (spell data available 1985–2003)
Sample
6,815,884 observations; 782,951 individuals; 1,698,990 job spells; 117,847 firms (NBER 2014 version) Danish workers up to age 55, after labour market entry, never observed in public-sector employment, self-employment, retirement or agriculture. source

Occupations and Import Competition: Evidence from Denmark

Sharon Traiberman · American Economic Review, 2019 · Individual + firm

Estimates a dynamic occupational choice model on Danish worker histories in which workers accumulate occupation-specific human capital that is lost when they switch. Occupations explain 57 percent of the dispersion in worker outcomes from trade, sectors only 16 percent, and rising import competition from 1995 to 2005 lowered lifetime earnings for about 5 percent of workers.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA/IDAS) with ISCO occupation codes, Firm and establishment statistics (FIRE, FIRM) for industry codes, Customs trade data (UHDI), UN Comtrade (CEPII BACI), Danish national accounts, Danish CPI, LIBOR
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual salary income from November employer
  • Choice variable: two-digit occupation by sector
  • Shock: import prices / import competition by industry
  • Worker skill groups: low, medium, high education
  • Occupational tenure
Period
1996–2007 worker panel (tenure built from 1990–1996); import competition 1995–2005
Sample
18,513,301 observations in sample frame (33,502,593 in raw data) Danish individuals aged 23–59 in IDA, 1996–2007, used to estimate an occupational choice model. source

Human Capital and Career Success: Evidence from Linked Employer-Employee Data

Anders Frederiksen and Takao Kato · Economic Journal, 2018 · Individual + firm

Uses complete Danish career histories to study who reaches top management, through internal promotion or external recruitment. Formal schooling and breadth of experience across occupations both help, they are complementary, and breadth acquired within the firm matters more than breadth acquired outside it, consistent with human capital theories of corporate leadership.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) with DISCO occupation codes, Education registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: appointment to top management (DISCO 12/13) in firm with 100+ employees
  • Key regressor: breadth of occupational experience over prior 8 years (TOCC)
  • Formal schooling
  • Tenure, general work experience, age, gender
Period
1992–2002 (appointments in 2000 and 2001)
Sample
773,094 observations (3,813 appointed to top management) Private-sector employees in Denmark with complete 8-year occupational histories forming the contestant pool for top management positions. source

Workers beneath the Floodgates: Low-Wage Import Competition and Workers' Adjustment

Hale Utar · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2018 · Individual + firm

Follows Danish textile and clothing workers for a decade after the removal of quotas on Chinese imports, using within-industry and within-occupation differences in exposure. Earnings and employment fall persistently, job instability in services is the main adjustment friction, and workers with manufacturing-specific education rebuild human capital through further schooling.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA): person, establishment and job files, Firm-Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (FIDA) employer-employee link, Domestic manufacturing production database (VARES), CN 8-digit products, EU SIGL database of MFA import quotas (European Commission)
Key variables
  • Treatment: 1999 employment at a firm producing goods subject to MFA quota removal for China
  • Outcome: cumulative 2002–2010 wage earnings normalized by pre-shock earnings
  • Outcome: cumulative hours, hourly wage, employment and unemployment
  • Controls: 1999 age, gender, immigrant status, occupation, education, union and UI membership
Period
1999–2010
Sample
10,521 workers All workers with primary employment in the Danish textile and clothing sector in 1999, born 1943–1982 (aged 20–67 over 2002–2010). source

Parental Responses to Child Support Obligations: Evidence from Administrative Data

Maya Rossin-Slater and Miriam Wüst · Journal of Public Economics, 2018 · Individual

Uses discontinuities in the Danish child support schedule by fathers' income to estimate how higher obligations affect separated parents. The paper documents responses in fathers' income, subsequent family formation and the parents' living arrangements.

Data
Danish administrative registers on children born 1985–2008 linked to parents and siblings (population/family registers), Income and tax records including tax-deductible child support payments, Labour market registers (employment, earnings, disability insurance, early retirement), Birth records (fertility through 2010), Education registers, Danish Longitudinal Survey of Children (DALSC) and Children and Youth in Denmark (CYD) survey (descriptive, supplementary)
Key variables
  • Treatment: father's average child support obligation (from Danish child support guideline schedule)
  • Identifying variation: income thresholds in the child support guidelines (simulated instrument)
  • Outcome: father's annual child support payment
  • Outcome: father–child co-residence (physical custody)
  • Outcomes: post-separation fertility and labour supply of both parents
Period
1998–2010 (separation years 1999–2008)
Sample
73,303 fathers (71,575 unique mothers) Fathers observed every year 1998–2010 who separated from their oldest child's mother (or had a first child while not cohabiting) in 1999–2008, with one or two children under 18 and separation-year income near the first three guideline thresholds. source

Estimating Equilibrium Effects of Job Search Assistance

Pieter Gautier, Paul Muller, Bas van der Klaauw, Michael Rosholm and Michael Svarer · Journal of Labor Economics, 2018 · Individual

A randomised activation experiment in two Danish counties helped treated job seekers find work faster, but untreated job seekers in the same counties found work more slowly than in comparison counties. An equilibrium search model fitted to the data shows that these displacement effects make a large-scale rollout far less beneficial than the partial estimates suggest.

Data
National Labour Market Board administrative data on unemployment benefit spells (all Danish counties), Randomised activation experiment assignment (Storstrøm and South Jutland, birth date 1st–15th of month), Income transfer data (exit destinations), National Labour Market Board monthly stock of open vacancies by county (from local job centres)
Key variables
  • Outcome: unemployment duration / exit rate from benefits (weeks)
  • Treatment: assignment to intensive activation programme (born 1st–15th of month)
  • Spillover comparison: non-participants in experiment counties vs comparison counties (difference-in-differences)
  • Outcome: log stock of open vacancies by county and month
  • Structural equilibrium search model parameters
Period
Benefit inflows November 2004–February 2005 (pre-experiment) and November 2005–February 2006 (experiment), followed up to two years; vacancies January 2004–November 2007
Sample
40,403 benefit claimants in experiment period (3,751 experiment participants: 1,814 treated, 1,937 controls); 49,063 in pre-experiment sample All individuals in Denmark who started collecting regular unemployment benefits between November 2005 and February 2006, plus a pre-experiment inflow cohort one year earlier. source

Can Women Have Children and a Career? IV Evidence from IVF Treatments

Petter Lundborg, Erik Plug and Astrid Würtz Rasmussen · American Economic Review, 2017 · Individual

Uses success in in vitro fertilisation treatment among childless Danish women as an instrument for having children, since IVF success is unrelated to labour market history. Children lower women's earnings substantially and persistently, mostly through hourly earnings rather than hours, and the effect is much larger for the first child than for later ones.

Data
IVF register held by the Danish National Board of Health (all IVF treatments in public and private clinics, 1994–2005), Linked administrative registers on education, age, marital status and number of children, Linked labour market registers: labour force status, hours worked, hourly wages, annual earnings, occupation, sector and firm
Key variables
  • Instrument: success of the first IVF treatment
  • Endogenous treatment: having children (fertility at the extensive margin)
  • Outcome: annual earnings
  • Outcome: hourly wages and hours worked / employment
  • Mechanisms: occupation, sector and firm changes
Period
1991–2009 (IVF treatments 1995–2005)
Sample
18,538 treated women Childless women entering their first IVF treatment with embryo implants between 1995 and 2005. source

Tax Reforms and Intertemporal Shifting of Wage Income: Evidence from Danish Monthly Payroll Records

Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Søren Leth-Petersen and Peer Ebbesen Skov · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2016 · Individual + firm

The 2010 cut in the top marginal tax rate was announced in advance, and monthly payroll data show top earners shifting wage income from December 2009 into January 2010. Shifting is concentrated among managers and employees of small firms, and it inflates short-run elasticity estimates.

Data
Monthly payroll records (eIndkomst / eIncome register) from the tax agency SKAT, Background registers on individuals and firms at Statistics Denmark (incl. firm size, liquid assets), Telephone survey on awareness of income-shifting opportunity (January 2011), merged to eIncome register
Key variables
  • Outcome: percentage change in monthly gross wage relative to 2008 average
  • Treatment: private-sector employees with 2008 monthly earnings above DKK 35,000 (top-tax incentive from 2010 reform)
  • Control group: monthly earnings DKK 30,000–35,000
  • Heterogeneity: firm size, liquid assets, earnings percentile
  • Elasticity of taxable income with and without shifting component
Period
January 2008–December 2011 (monthly)
Sample
328,679 individuals, of which 219,179 in the treatment group; survey sample of 878 taxpayers Private-sector employees with a wage record in every month of 2008 and positive income in all years, earning above DKK 30,000 per month in 2008. source

Mathematics and Gender: Heterogeneity in Causes and Consequences

Juanna Schrøter Joensen and Helena Skyt Nielsen · Economic Journal, 2016 · Individual

A pilot scheme in Danish high schools that let students combine advanced mathematics with chemistry increased advanced maths take-up, especially among girls. The induced maths qualification raised later earnings, with the gains concentrated among students who would otherwise have taken less maths and operating through higher education choices.

Data
Education registers: detailed educational histories, high school branch choice and GPA (Statistics Denmark), Labour market registers: earnings, occupation, industry, self-employment, part-time work
Key variables
  • Outcome: log earnings (measured 18 years after high school graduation)
  • Outcomes: college graduation, field of major, occupation/top corporate jobs, sector
  • Treatment: choice of advanced high school math
  • Instrument: unexpected introduction of high school pilot scheme allowing math–chemistry combination
Period
High school entrants 1984–1986, followed into the labour market
Sample
about 25,658 females and 19,406 males from about 140 high schools Danish high school entrants of 1984–86 who graduated in three years and have at least one year of employment. source

Immigrants' Effect on Native Workers: New Analysis on Longitudinal Data

Mette Foged and Giovanni Peri · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2016 · Individual

Refugee-country immigration into Danish municipalities from 1991 to 2008 pushed less-educated native workers toward more complex, less manual occupations. Native wages and employment rose rather than fell, and effects were positive even for low-skilled natives who moved across firms.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Occupation codes linked to O*NET task intensities (manual, communication, cognitive), Full population registers for municipality immigrant shares, Refugee spatial dispersal policy 1986–1998 (initial refugee allocation by municipality)
Key variables
  • Treatment: share of refugee-country immigrants in the municipality of work
  • Instrument: immigrant inflow predicted from the 1986–1998 refugee dispersal policy
  • Outcome: occupational complexity index (O*NET-based)
  • Outcome: log hourly wage (incl. mandatory pension contributions)
  • Outcome: fraction of year worked; occupational mobility
  • Controls: age, experience, tenure, education, marital status, region, industry
Period
1991–2008
Sample
1,864,027 observations of employed low-skilled natives, 1995–2008, in the fixed-effects panel (20 percent random sample of natives); balanced-panel difference-in-differences regressions use 71,053 natives employed in 1994 Danish-born individuals aged 18–65, not in school or permanently disabled, focusing on low-skilled (non-college) natives, across 97 municipalities. source

Taxation and the Long Run Allocation of Labor: Theory and Danish Evidence

Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Jakob Roland Munch and Hans Jørgen Whitta-Jacobsen · Journal of Public Economics, 2015 · Individual + firm

Estimates a search model with non-linear taxation, in which workers keep looking for better-paid jobs, on employer-employee register data for all Danish workers and workplaces in 2004 to 2006. Danish taxes reduce total income by about 12 percent through the allocation of labour across jobs, and moving to low-tax OECD levels would recover four fifths of the loss.

Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Wage Statistics Register (Lønstatistikken) for hours-adjusted wages (robustness), Empirical effective marginal tax rate schedule for 2006 from Kleven and Kreiner (2006)

  • Workplace size, number of stayers and new hires (job-to-job flows)
  • New hires from non-employment
  • Effective marginal tax rates by income level (used to estimate structural search model and efficiency loss)
Period
2004–2006 (base year 2005)
Sample
126,400 workplaces employing 1.4 million workers Full population of private-sector workplaces in Denmark in 2005 and their workers aged 18–65, with wages between DKK 90 and DKK 714 per hour. source

The Wage Effects of Offshoring: Evidence from Danish Matched Worker-Firm Data

David Hummels, Rasmus Jørgensen, Jakob Munch and Chong Xiang · American Economic Review, 2014 · Individual + firm

Links product-level firm imports and exports to worker wages. Offshoring raises wages of high-skilled workers and lowers wages of low-skilled workers within job spells, while exporting raises wages for all. Tasks that are routine or easily offshored lose the most.

Data
Firm Statistics Register (FirmStat/FIRM), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-worker link (FIDA), Foreign Trade Statistics Register, Extrastat and Intrastat (UHDI), UN COMTRADE world trade data for instruments, Firm accounts and other firm registers (Data Appendix)
Key variables
  • Outcome: log hourly wage of workers within job spells
  • Treatment: firm offshoring (imports of inputs)
  • Treatment: firm exports
  • Instruments: world export supply and world import demand shocks by product and country (COMTRADE) and transport costs
  • Heterogeneity: worker skill type (high vs low skilled)
  • Outcome: earnings losses of displaced workers
Period
1995–2006 (trade data 1990–2006)
Sample
about 1.95 million worker-firm-year and 9,800 firm-year observations Manufacturing workers in Danish firms that both import and export, excluding single-year job spells, workers changing skill level, and firms with fewer than 50 employees and less than 0.6 million DKK in imports. source

When the Floodgates Open: Northern Firms' Response to Removal of Trade Quotas on Chinese Goods

Hale Utar · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2014 · Individual + firm

The dismantling of Multi-Fibre Arrangement quotas on Chinese textiles at WTO accession exposed Danish textile firms to low-wage competition. Within firms, employment, value added and intangible assets fell and the workforce shifted toward skilled workers, while firms refocused away from products where China's advantage grew.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm accounting statistics (Regnskabsdata / FIRE), International trade data from customs records (Udenrigshandel / UHDI), Domestic production/sales by product (Varestatistik / VARES), 1995–2005, EU SIGL textile and clothing quota licence database

  • Treatment: firm produced 2005 MFA-quota goods for China in 1999 (exposure to quota removal)
  • Outcome: firm employment, value added, intangible assets, capital intensity
  • Outcome: workforce skill composition by occupation and education
  • Outcome: product scope / product innovation from transaction-level data
  • Firm fixed effects, year effects
Period
1995–2007
Sample
1,090 unique textile and clothing firms (875 firms, 1995–2005, for the product-portfolio analysis) All Danish textile and clothing firms with at least 0.5 full-time-equivalent employees, 1995–2007, matched to their employees and product transactions. source

Tenure, Experience, Human Capital, and Wages: A Tractable Equilibrium Search Model of Wage Dynamics

Jesper Bagger, François Fontaine, Fabien Postel-Vinay and Jean-Marc Robin · American Economic Review, 2014 · Individual + firm

Builds and estimates an equilibrium search model with on-the-job search and human capital accumulation using Danish career histories. Human capital accumulation drives most early-career wage growth, while job-to-job mobility and employer competition explain the later part of wage profiles.

Wage Dispersion and Decentralization of Wage Bargaining

Christian M. Dahl, Daniel le Maire and Jakob R. Munch · Journal of Labor Economics, 2013 · Individual + firm

Exploits the shift in Denmark during the 1990s from centralised to firm-level wage bargaining. Workers covered by firm-level bargaining earn a wage premium, returns to skills are higher, and wage dispersion is greater than under sector-level bargaining.

Sample
about 1.14 million worker-year observations (1,137,367) 30% random sample of full-time private-sector workers aged 25–65 in 36 DA/LO bargaining segments, in plants with at least five employees. source

Is Business Failure Due to Lack of Effort? Empirical Evidence from a Large Administrative Sample

Mette Ejrnæs and Stefan Hochguertel · Economic Journal, 2013 · Individual

Danish self-employed can voluntarily buy unemployment insurance, which creates a moral hazard test. Using a large administrative panel, insured entrepreneurs are more likely to close their businesses and enter unemployment, with effects that strengthen as the eligibility date approaches, consistent with reduced effort rather than selection alone.

Data
CAM 10% Sample: 10% random sample of the Danish population aged 16+ (linked administrative registers incl. tax and benefit records), Unemployment insurance fund membership records
Key variables
  • Outcome: transition from self-employment to unemployment (registered unemployed >10% of year)
  • Treatment: UI fund membership (insurance status)
  • Instrument: eligibility for early retirement scheme (VERP) available only to UI members
  • Estimator: bivariate random effects probit
Period
1981–1998
Sample
13,434 individuals and 86,092 observations (estimation sample) Denmark-born male Danish citizens aged 25–59 who were self-employed in their first three observed years, excluding agriculture, from the CAM 10% sample. source

Unemployment Insurance, Wage Dynamics and Inequality Over the Life Cycle

Paul Bingley, Lorenzo Cappellari and Niels Westergård-Nielsen · Economic Journal, 2013 · Individual

Models permanent and transitory wage dynamics over the life cycle for Danish men, allowing dynamics to differ by unemployment insurance membership. Insurance is associated with lower wage inequality and less persistence of shocks, and the paper quantifies how much of life-cycle wage inequality UI members avoid relative to non-members.

Data
Administrative register data on the Danish labour force (Statistics Denmark): annual labour earnings and hours-derived hourly wages, UI fund membership contributions reported to tax authorities, Mandatory pension contributions (ATP) for year of labour market entry

  • Birth cohort (1943–1973, 31 cohorts)
Period
1980–2003
Sample
roughly 810,000 individuals, about 12.5 million person-year observations Danish men aged 21–55, full-time private-sector employees, born 1943–1973, with at least five consecutive valid wage observations. source

Productivity Spillovers Across Firms through Worker Mobility

Andrey Stoyanov and Nikolay Zubanov · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2012 · Individual + firm

Tracks firm-to-firm worker moves in Danish manufacturing. Firms that hire workers from more productive firms see productivity gains of about 0.35 percent a year for an average firm, rising with the education, tenure and skill of the new hires and persisting for several years, consistent with knowledge spillovers carried by workers.

Data
Matched employer-employee data from Statistics Denmark (worker records from 1983, ages 15–65), Manufacturing firm-level accounts data with sales, value added, materials, capital and investment, 1995–2007
Key variables
  • Outcome: receiving firm's log value added per worker normalised by 4-digit industry-year mean
  • Treatment: productivity gap between sending and receiving firms of newly hired workers, weighted by hires' employment share
  • Positive and negative productivity gaps (hires from more vs. less productive firms)
  • Worker characteristics: education, occupation/skill level, experience, wage
  • Firm characteristics: size, industry
Period
1995–2007
Sample
173,929 firm-year observations (about 5.8 million worker-year observations, 668,034 job changers); benchmark regressions use 74,507 and 61,112 firm-year observations All Danish manufacturing firms and their employees with positive annual salary, 1995–2007. source

Adjustment Costs, Firm Responses, and Micro vs. Macro Labor Supply Elasticities: Evidence from Danish Tax Records

Raj Chetty, John N. Friedman, Tore Olsen and Luigi Pistaferri · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2011 · Individual + firm

Uses bunching at kinks in the Danish tax schedule to show that observed earnings responses are shaped by search costs and by firms coordinating hours around tax thresholds. Once adjustment frictions are accounted for, the structural labour supply elasticity is around 0.3, far above the small elasticities from individual-level micro studies, which helps reconcile micro and macro estimates.

Data
Income tax register / Income Statistics Register (IND), Population register (BEF), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) with firm ID and occupation
Key variables
  • Outcome: bunching (excess mass) of taxable income at the top-tax kink
  • Treatment: marginal tax rate changes at bracket cutoffs (tax simulator)
  • Key variable: firm and occupation-level bunching (hours constraints / firm responses)
  • Key variable: size of tax change across kinks
  • Comparison: wage earners vs self-employed
Period
1994–2001
Sample
17.9 million observations of wage earners (6.8 million within DKr 50,000 of the top tax cutoff; 1.8 million self-employed observations) Danish wage earners aged 15–70, excluding the self-employed and pensioners, with self-employed studied separately. source

Job Satisfaction and Co-worker Wages: Status or Signal?

Andrew E. Clark, Nicolai Kristensen and Niels Westergård-Nielsen · Economic Journal, 2009 · Individual + firm

Links survey measures of job satisfaction to register wages of all co-workers at the same establishment. Satisfaction is higher when co-workers earn more, the opposite of the usual income comparison result, which the authors interpret as co-worker pay signalling one's own future earnings; the effect is stronger for men and in the private sector.

Data
Danish sample of the European Community Household Panel (ECHP), eight waves, Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), matched via CPR number
Key variables
  • Outcome: overall job satisfaction (1–6 scale)
  • Key regressor: co-workers' wages within the same establishment
  • Own wage (gross annual income from IDA)
  • Wage deficit relative to establishment wage distribution
Period
1994–2001
Sample
about 16,000 observations on around 4,100 individuals Employees in the Danish ECHP 1994–2001 matched to IDA, in establishments with at least 5 colleagues. source

Ethnic Enclaves and Immigrant Labor Market Outcomes: Quasi-Experimental Evidence

Anna Piil Damm · Journal of Labor Economics, 2009 · Individual

Uses the quasi-random dispersal of refugees across Danish municipalities between 1986 and 1998 to separate the effect of living in an ethnic enclave from self-selection. Larger enclaves raise refugee men's annual earnings without changing their employment rates, and the gains are larger where more co-nationals are employed, pointing to job information networks.

Data
Longitudinal administrative registers on the immigrant population (Statistics Denmark), 1984–2000, Population register (BEF) incl. country of origin, year of immigration and municipality, Survey-based register on immigrants' pre-immigration education and integrated pupil register, Municipality statistics: unemployment register, election statistics, register-based labour force statistics, housing statistics, Danish Refugee Council administrative statistics on temporary housing (to identify assignment municipality)
Key variables
  • Outcome: employment probability
  • Outcome: real annual labour earnings
  • Key regressor: ethnic enclave (co-national group) size in municipality
  • Instrument / identification: initial assignment municipality under the 1986–1998 spatial dispersal policy
  • Controls: gender, age, marital status, children, education, country of origin, year of immigration
Period
Registers 1984–2000; refugee immigration cohorts 1986–1993
Sample
13,927 individuals (balanced panel observed annually for seven years after immigration) Adult refugees from 11 main refugee-sending countries assigned to Danish municipalities under the dispersal policy, observed annually from receipt of residence permit to seven years after. source

Brian Krogh Graversen and Jan C. van Ours · Journal of Public Economics, 2008 · Individual

A randomised experiment in two Danish counties assigned newly unemployed workers to an intensive activation programme with early job search courses, frequent meetings and mandatory activation. Treated workers found jobs substantially faster, with the threat of mandatory activation an important part of the effect.

Inside the Family Firm: The Role of Families in Succession Decisions and Performance

Morten Bennedsen, Kasper Meisner Nielsen, Francisco Pérez-González and Daniel Wolfenzon · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2007 · Individual + firm

Uses the gender of a departing CEO's first-born child as an instrument for family succession in Danish firms. Family CEOs hurt performance: operating profitability falls by at least four percentage points relative to firms that hire outside CEOs, with the largest costs in fast-growing industries and large firms.

  • Treatment: family CEO succession (incoming CEO related by blood or marriage)
  • Instrument: gender of the departing CEO's firstborn child
  • Heterogeneity: industry growth, skill intensity and firm size
Period
1994–2002 (management data); financial data 1991–2003
Sample
5,334 successions CEO successions in Danish limited liability (public and private) firms between 1994 and 2002, first transition per firm. source

Paul Bingley and Gauthier Lanot · Journal of Public Economics, 2007 · Individual

Estimates a dynamic discrete choice model of couples' joint retirement and saving on a 1 percent register sample of Danish couples, using the introduction and reforms of the early retirement programme (efterløn) for identification. Retirement responds asymmetrically for men and women to their own and their spouse's income flows.

  • Explanatory: own and spouse's income flows
  • Model: dynamic discrete choice model with saving

Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked A 1% register sample of Danish married couples of potential retirement age. source (abstract)

Are Homeowners Really More Unemployed?

Jakob Roland Munch, Michael Rosholm and Michael Svarer · Economic Journal, 2006 · Individual

Tests the Oswald hypothesis that home ownership raises unemployment. Danish homeowners are less geographically mobile, but they find local jobs faster, so their unemployment spells are shorter overall. Ownership does not raise aggregate unemployment.

Data
Administrative register data on unemployment spells for a 2.5% random sample of the Danish population, Regional unemployment measure from the Institute for Local Government Studies (1987–2000)
Key variables
  • Outcome: unemployment duration in weeks
  • Outcome: exit destination (new job locally, job in another commuting area, other states)
  • Key regressor: home ownership indicator
  • Controls: age, gender, children, household type, immigrant status, education, UI fund membership and replacement rate
Period
1997–2000
Sample
18,992 persons with 52,738 unemployment spells Flow sample of all unemployment spells in 1997–2000 for individuals aged 19–66 in a 2.5% random sample of the Danish population. source

Lars Pico Geerdsen · Economic Journal, 2006 · Individual

Exploits the timing rules for compulsory activation in the Danish unemployment insurance system to test whether the prospect of being activated speeds up exit from unemployment. Exit rates rise markedly as the activation deadline approaches, which identifies a sizeable threat effect of active labour market programmes.

Danish UI system administrative data (not specified in abstract)
Key variables
  • Outcome: exit from unemployment to employment before programme participation
  • Identification: legislative changes to the length of the UI benefit period before compulsory activation
  • Treatment: approach of compulsory ALMP participation (threat effect)

Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Unemployed UI recipients in Denmark subject to compulsory labour market programmes after the benefit period. source (abstract)

On-the-Job Search and the Wage Distribution

Bent Jesper Christensen, Rasmus Lentz, Dale T. Mortensen, George R. Neumann and Axel Werwatz · Journal of Labor Economics, 2005 · Individual + firm

Estimates an on-the-job search model on the Danish Integrated Database for Labour Market Research, splitting each firm's separation rate into an exogenous part and a job-to-job part that falls with the firm's wage. Workers search less intensely the higher their wage, and the estimated model accounts for the gap between the distribution of wages earned and the distribution of wage offers.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), matched employer-employee data
Key variables
  • Outcome: job separations (stayers one year later) by establishment
  • Establishment average hourly wage (Statistics Denmark hourly wage estimate)
  • Wage offer distribution weighted by hires from non-employment
  • Wage earned distribution weighted by establishment employment
  • Sub-samples by occupation: skilled, unskilled, managers, salaried
Period
November 1994–November 1995
Sample
113,332 private-sector establishments All establishments in the Danish private sector, with workers aged 18–65, split by occupational category. source

Rent Control and Unemployment Duration

Michael Svarer, Michael Rosholm and Jakob Roland Munch · Journal of Public Economics, 2005 · Individual

Uses Danish register data on unemployment spells and housing to study whether living in rent-controlled housing affects job finding. Unemployed workers in rent-controlled dwellings have lower exit rates from unemployment, consistent with rent control reducing geographic mobility.

Data
10% random sample of the Danish adult population from administrative registers (Statistics Denmark), Housing unit characteristics for dwellings occupied by the sample, Weekly labour market event histories, Danish Tax Authorities 1999 hedonic model for owner-occupied housing (to predict uncontrolled rent)
Key variables
  • Outcome: unemployment duration in weeks (exit to job in local vs national labour market)
  • Explanatory: rent control benefit decile (predicted uncontrolled rent minus actual rent), time-varying
  • Mobility: job finding with move to another of 51 commuting areas
  • Tenure type: private rental housing vs other
Period
1997–2000
Sample
14,645 persons contributing 31,966 unemployment spells Inflow sample of unemployment spells 1997–2000 from a 10% random sample of the Danish adult population (IZA DP 842 working-paper version). source

Worker Flows and Job Flows in Danish Manufacturing, 1980–91

Karsten Albæk and Bent E. Sørensen · Economic Journal, 1998 · Individual + firm

One of the first uses of the IDA matched employer-employee data to measure job creation, job destruction and worker flows across Danish manufacturing plants. Worker turnover far exceeds job reallocation, and both are large and persistent at the plant level.

Data
Plant-level data on all Danish manufacturing plants and their workers (register not named in abstract)
Key variables
  • Outcome: worker flows (hires and separations)
  • Outcome: job flows (job creation and destruction)
  • Replacement hiring (separations from and hires into existing jobs)
  • Business-cycle variation
Period
1980–1991
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked All individual manufacturing plants in Denmark, 1980–91. source (abstract)

Trade and offshoring

Firm-level imports and exports by product linked to the wages of the workers inside those firms.

Do Export Support Programs Affect Prices, Quality, Markups, and Marginal Costs? Evidence From a Natural Policy Experiment

Magnus T. Buus, Jakob R. Munch, Joel Rodrigue and Georg Schaur · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Firm

Uses a quasi-natural experiment in the Danish Trade Council's export support to firms. Support raises firm-level exports within destination markets but leaves prices, quality, markups and marginal costs unchanged, consistent with firms growing abroad by shifting demand rather than changing products.

Data
Trade Council export promotion records: firm purchases and Trade Council approaches by destination, International Trade in Goods statistics: Intrastat and Extrastat firm exports by CN8 product and destination (UHDI), Manufacturers' Sales of Goods (VARES/Prodcom): firm sales by product, value and quantity, Firm Statistics Register (FirmStat) and Firm Accounts Statistics (FIRE)
Key variables
  • Treatment: purchase of export promotion services for a destination in year t or t-1
  • Instrument: whether the Trade Council approached the firm
  • Outcome: firm-product-destination export sales
  • Outcome: export unit values (prices), quality, markups and marginal costs
Period
2002–2015
Sample
18,181 firm-years (manufacturing exporters in Prodcom data, 77 destinations) Danish manufacturing exporters with at least 10 employees present in the production data, exporting to the 77 destinations served by the Trade Council, 2002–2015. source

Trade Shocks, Firm Hierarchies, and Wage Inequality

Benjamin U. Friedrich · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2022 · Individual + firm

Shows that trade shocks change within-firm wage inequality by changing firm hierarchies, using both firm-level foreign demand shocks and the Muslim boycott of Danish exports after the 2006 cartoon crisis. Adding a hierarchy layer widens pay gaps throughout the firm, and delayering after the boycott compresses wages through cuts, demotions and turnover.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) with DISCO occupation codes, Firm-Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (FIDA), Danish Business Register / firm statistics (GF): sales, value added, industry, Foreign Trade Statistics Register: firm-level exports by CN8 product and destination (UHDI), UN Comtrade bilateral trade flows (HS6), U.S. Imports of Merchandise, Penn World Tables distances, Brent oil prices (transport cost estimation)
Key variables
  • Outcome: within-firm wage inequality (std. dev. of log wages, 90-10 gap)
  • Treatment: number of hierarchy layers based on occupations
  • Instrument: firm-specific world import demand and transport-cost shocks
  • Natural experiment: 2006 Muslim boycott of Danish exports (Cartoon crisis)
Period
1999–2008
Sample
68,090 firm-year observations on 12,470 firms (within-firm inequality regressions) Universe of pre-existing private-sector exporting firms in Denmark, 1999–2008. source

Globalization, Gender, and the Family

Wolfgang Keller and Hale Utar · Review of Economic Studies, 2022 · Individual + firm

Men and women hit by the same import shock from China adjust differently. Women in their late thirties who are displaced respond by having a child, especially high earners and those who would need to retrain, so a gender-neutral trade shock produces a long-run gender gap through the biological clock.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Central Population Register (CPR/BEF) for births, marriages, divorces and cohabitation, Domestic production database by 10-digit product (Industrial commodity statistics, VARS) to identify firms making quota goods, Industry-level imports from China and other advanced countries (instrument), Parental leave records
Key variables
  • Outcome: new births, marriage, divorce, parental leave
  • Outcome: earnings, hours, unemployment
  • Treatment: 1999 employer produces goods subject to MFA quota removal for China after WTO entry
  • Treatment (economy-wide): industry import penetration from China, instrumented with other countries' imports
  • Heterogeneity: gender and age (biological clock)
Period
1999–2007
Sample
about 1,600,000 workers; about 10,000 textile and clothing workers The 1999 cohort of full-time Danish private-sector workers aged 18–56, followed through 2007. source

The Impact of Immigration on Firm-Level Offshoring

William W. Olney and Dario Pozzoli · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2021 · Individual + firm

Uses the Danish refugee dispersal policy as a natural experiment and matched data on all firms and workers from 1995 to 2011. An exogenous inflow of immigrants into a municipality reduces firm-level offshoring on both margins, while immigrants' ties to their origin countries raise offshoring to those particular countries.

Data
Firm Statistics Register (FirmStat), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (FIDA), Foreign Trade Statistics Register: Intrastat and Extrastat firm-level imports (UHDI), Central Business Register (foreign ownership)
Key variables
  • Outcome: extensive margin of offshoring (firm imports in same HS4 as own production)
  • Outcome: log volume of narrow offshoring
  • Treatment: non-EU immigrant share of municipality workforce
  • Instrument: refugee placement under the 1986–1998 Spatial Dispersal Policy (shift-share)
Period
1995–2011
Sample
approximately 35,000 firms and 1 million workers Unbalanced panel of Danish private-sector firms with more than one employee in 97 municipalities, 1995–2011. source

Occupations and Import Competition: Evidence from Denmark

Sharon Traiberman · American Economic Review, 2019 · Individual + firm

Estimates a dynamic occupational choice model on Danish worker histories in which workers accumulate occupation-specific human capital that is lost when they switch. Occupations explain 57 percent of the dispersion in worker outcomes from trade, sectors only 16 percent, and rising import competition from 1995 to 2005 lowered lifetime earnings for about 5 percent of workers.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA/IDAS) with ISCO occupation codes, Firm and establishment statistics (FIRE, FIRM) for industry codes, Customs trade data (UHDI), UN Comtrade (CEPII BACI), Danish national accounts, Danish CPI, LIBOR
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual salary income from November employer
  • Choice variable: two-digit occupation by sector
  • Shock: import prices / import competition by industry
  • Worker skill groups: low, medium, high education
  • Occupational tenure
Period
1996–2007 worker panel (tenure built from 1990–1996); import competition 1995–2005
Sample
18,513,301 observations in sample frame (33,502,593 in raw data) Danish individuals aged 23–59 in IDA, 1996–2007, used to estimate an occupational choice model. source

Workers beneath the Floodgates: Low-Wage Import Competition and Workers' Adjustment

Hale Utar · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2018 · Individual + firm

Follows Danish textile and clothing workers for a decade after the removal of quotas on Chinese imports, using within-industry and within-occupation differences in exposure. Earnings and employment fall persistently, job instability in services is the main adjustment friction, and workers with manufacturing-specific education rebuild human capital through further schooling.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA): person, establishment and job files, Firm-Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (FIDA) employer-employee link, Domestic manufacturing production database (VARES), CN 8-digit products, EU SIGL database of MFA import quotas (European Commission)
Key variables
  • Treatment: 1999 employment at a firm producing goods subject to MFA quota removal for China
  • Outcome: cumulative 2002–2010 wage earnings normalized by pre-shock earnings
  • Outcome: cumulative hours, hourly wage, employment and unemployment
  • Controls: 1999 age, gender, immigrant status, occupation, education, union and UI membership
Period
1999–2010
Sample
10,521 workers All workers with primary employment in the Danish textile and clothing sector in 1999, born 1943–1982 (aged 20–67 over 2002–2010). source

The Effect of Export Promotion on Firm-Level Performance

Jakob Munch and Georg Schaur · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2018 · Firm

Evaluates the Danish Trade Council's export promotion services, separating firms that sought help from firms the council approached on observables. Promotion raises sales, value added, employment and value added per worker, and for small firms the gain in value added is roughly three times the direct cost.

Data
Danish Trade Council data on export promotion services (self-selected vs. Council-approached firms), Danish firm-level performance data (sales, value added, employment); specific registers not stated in abstract
Key variables
  • Treatment: firm receives export promotion services from the Danish Trade Council
  • Comparison: firms that self-selected into services vs. firms approached by the Trade Council
  • Outcome: sales, value added, employment, value added per worker
  • Controls: extensive set of firm characteristics (selection on observables)
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish firms, comparing users of Trade Council export promotion services with comparable non-users. source (abstract)

The Wage Effects of Offshoring: Evidence from Danish Matched Worker-Firm Data

David Hummels, Rasmus Jørgensen, Jakob Munch and Chong Xiang · American Economic Review, 2014 · Individual + firm

Links product-level firm imports and exports to worker wages. Offshoring raises wages of high-skilled workers and lowers wages of low-skilled workers within job spells, while exporting raises wages for all. Tasks that are routine or easily offshored lose the most.

Data
Firm Statistics Register (FirmStat/FIRM), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-worker link (FIDA), Foreign Trade Statistics Register, Extrastat and Intrastat (UHDI), UN COMTRADE world trade data for instruments, Firm accounts and other firm registers (Data Appendix)
Key variables
  • Outcome: log hourly wage of workers within job spells
  • Treatment: firm offshoring (imports of inputs)
  • Treatment: firm exports
  • Instruments: world export supply and world import demand shocks by product and country (COMTRADE) and transport costs
  • Heterogeneity: worker skill type (high vs low skilled)
  • Outcome: earnings losses of displaced workers
Period
1995–2006 (trade data 1990–2006)
Sample
about 1.95 million worker-firm-year and 9,800 firm-year observations Manufacturing workers in Danish firms that both import and export, excluding single-year job spells, workers changing skill level, and firms with fewer than 50 employees and less than 0.6 million DKK in imports. source

When the Floodgates Open: Northern Firms' Response to Removal of Trade Quotas on Chinese Goods

Hale Utar · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2014 · Individual + firm

The dismantling of Multi-Fibre Arrangement quotas on Chinese textiles at WTO accession exposed Danish textile firms to low-wage competition. Within firms, employment, value added and intangible assets fell and the workforce shifted toward skilled workers, while firms refocused away from products where China's advantage grew.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm accounting statistics (Regnskabsdata / FIRE), International trade data from customs records (Udenrigshandel / UHDI), Domestic production/sales by product (Varestatistik / VARES), 1995–2005, EU SIGL textile and clothing quota licence database
Key variables
  • Treatment: firm produced 2005 MFA-quota goods for China in 1999 (exposure to quota removal)
  • Outcome: firm employment, value added, intangible assets, capital intensity
  • Outcome: workforce skill composition by occupation and education
  • Outcome: product scope / product innovation from transaction-level data
  • Firm fixed effects, year effects
Period
1995–2007
Sample
1,090 unique textile and clothing firms (875 firms, 1995–2005, for the product-portfolio analysis) All Danish textile and clothing firms with at least 0.5 full-time-equivalent employees, 1995–2007, matched to their employees and product transactions. source

Firms, ownership and innovation

Firm accounts, management and family ownership, and product innovation.

Disaggregated Economic Accounts

Asger Lau Andersen, Kilian Huber, Niels Johannesen, Ludwig Straub and Emil Toft Vestergaard · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2026 · Individual + firm

Builds a system of national accounts broken down into bilateral money flows between region-by-industry consumer cells, producer cells, the government and the rest of the world, and implements it for Denmark using bank transaction data and registers. Spending is locally and urban biased, and fiscal transfers raise GDP more when targeted at cells whose spending reaches cells with high unemployment.

Data
Danske Bank geo-coded transaction-level data (about 20% of Danish adult consumers), 2018–2019, Government tax and income registers (labour compensation, mixed income, interest, dividends, transfers), Employment registers linking adults to industry of main employment and region, Danish national accounts (SNA positions, input-output tables), 2018, CrediWire producer-to-producer transactions (about 5 million), Firm-level export and import data for manufacturing firms
Key variables
  • Outcome: bilateral cell-to-cell flows (consumer spending, labour and profit income, taxes, transfers, intermediates trade)
  • Unit: consumer cells (industry of main employment x region of residence)
  • Unit: producer cells (industry x region of establishments)
  • Derived: domestic spending share, gravity, urban bias and spending intensity measures
Period
2018 (consumer spending 2018–2019)
Sample
approx. 2,700 consumer cells and approx. 2,600 producer cells; around 43 million entries All Danish adults and production establishments grouped into region-by-industry cells, with consumer spending measured from Danske Bank customers. source

Managing Inequality: Manager-Specific Wage Premiums and Selection in the Managerial Labor Market

Alex Xi He and Daniel le Maire · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual + firm

Builds a manager-firm-worker matched dataset for the whole Danish population to estimate manager effects on wages. Manager-specific premiums are almost as important as firm premiums for between-firm wage inequality, are unrelated to productivity, and high-paying managers are more likely to be replaced after acquisitions.

Data
Matched employer-employee data covering all Danish workers and firms (IDA), Occupation codes used to identify managers, Firm balance sheet data for private-sector firms (FIRE), Mergers and acquisitions identified from firm and establishment identifiers
Key variables
  • Outcome: worker log wages
  • Key estimate: manager-specific wage premiums (manager fixed effects identified from worker and manager mobility)
  • Comparison: firm-specific wage premiums
  • Event: firm acquisition and manager replacement
  • Heterogeneity: industry concentration (HHI)
Period
1995–2011 (per 2019 working-paper slides)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Manager-firm-worker matched panel covering the entire Danish population; acquisitions sample of about 3,700 private-sector acquisitions in the 2019 working-paper version. source (abstract)

No Pain, No Gain: Work Demand, Work Effort, and Worker Health

David Hummels, Jakob R. Munch and Chong Xiang · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual + firm

Links Danish worker-firm data to hospital and prescription records to study how rising workload affects health. Increases in firm sales lead workers to log longer hours and raise the probability of stress, depression, heart disease and stroke, most for older and already strained workers; the welfare loss from sickness offsets nearly a quarter of the earnings gain.

Data
Firm Statistics Register (FIRM), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-worker link (FIDA), Accounts Statistics (Regnskabsstatistik), Prodcom firm sales by product (VARS), Health care registers: prescription drug purchases, doctor visits and hospitalizations (National Patient Register), Monthly payroll records with earnings and hours (eIndkomst/BFL), Monthly firm VAT sales and material purchases (FIKS)
Key variables
  • Treatment: log firm sales (Prodcom), with a Bartik/shift-share instrument in robustness checks
  • Outcome: monthly hours worked and earnings
  • Outcome: stress/depression indicators (anti-depressant use, psychiatrist visits, burnout hospitalizations)
  • Outcome: heart disease drugs, heart attack/stroke and alcoholism hospitalizations
  • Fixed effects: job-spell fixed effects
Period
1995–2012 (health data 1996–2012; monthly payroll data 2008–2012)
Sample
3,336,244 annual worker-year observations; 8,834,846 monthly observations Full-time workers aged 20–60 in private-sector Danish firms in the Prodcom sales data with at least 50 employees and positive imports and exports, excluding single-year employment relationships. source

Healthy at Work? Evidence from a Social Experimental Evaluation of a Firm-Based Wellness Program

Marianne Simonsen and Lars Skipper · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2025 · Individual + firm

A large social experiment across worksites, followed for up to twelve years in the registers, evaluates an employer-sponsored health programme. Employees at treated worksites see fewer GP visits and buy fewer prescription drugs for up to seven years, with no change in hospitalisations, absence or turnover and some spillovers within the family.

Data
Healthy-at-Work randomised experiment records (work-unit assignment, September 2008), Primary care physician consultation records (Statistics Denmark registers), Prescription drug purchase records, Hospital contact / hospitalisation records, Employment, sickness absence and turnover registers, Socio-economic background registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: work-unit randomised to the Healthy-at-Work wellness program
  • Outcome: primary care physician visits
  • Outcome: prescription drug purchases (any, months supply)
  • Outcome: hospitalisations (somatic, psychiatric)
  • Outcome: sickness absence and turnover
  • Spillover outcome: spouses' health care use
Period
Randomisation 2008; follow-up to 2020 (PCP visits and work outcomes), 2019 (prescriptions), 2018 (hospitalisations)
Sample
7,541 workers (3,735 treatment, 3,806 control) in 314 work-units Elderly-care health workers in 314 work-units of 110 employers across 11 Danish municipalities enrolled in the Healthy-at-Work experiment. source

Is Parental Leave Costly for Firms and Coworkers?

Anne Ardila Brenøe, Serena Canaan, Nikolaj A. Harmon and Heather N. Royer · Journal of Labor Economics, 2024 · Individual + firm

Compares small Danish firms where a female employee is about to give birth with similar firms without an impending birth. There is little evidence of negative effects on firm output or coworkers: most firms cover the absence by hiring temporary workers and raising other employees' hours, and only firms unable to reallocate work show losses.

Data
Central Person Registry with parent-child links (CPR/BEF), Parental leave benefit payouts and employer leave reimbursements, Central education register (UDDA), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Annual employer wage payment records (CON and RAS), Mandatory pension contributions for approximate hours (ATP), Value-added tax (VAT) firm sales and purchases data
Key variables
  • Treatment: a female employee at a small private firm giving birth (event year)
  • Outcome: firm employment, FTE hours, wage bill (total and excluding leave), sales, gross profit proxy, survival
  • Outcome: coworker hours, earnings, employment and sick days
  • Mechanism: parental leave days at the firm
  • Design: dynamic difference-in-differences with matched control events
Period
2001–2013 (births 2003–2012)
Sample
24,829 treatment events (births) and 162,151 control events covering 45,940 unique firms; 9,934 treatment events in 16,080 firms after trimming Births to prime-age (21–35) women employed in small private-sector Danish firms, compared with matched control firms without such births. source

Credit Supply Shocks and Prices: Evidence from Danish Firms

Tobias Renkin and Gabriel Züllig · AEJ: Macroeconomics, 2024 · Firm

Combines loan-level data on Danish firm–bank relationships with survey producer prices and export unit values. Firms whose banks were exposed to the global financial crisis saw loans fall and rates rise, and they raised output prices by 3 to 5 percent to generate cash, with smaller increases where demand is more elastic.

Data
Bank account-level loan and deposit reports to the Danish Tax Authority (firm part), Bank balance sheets from the Danish FSA (Finanstilsynet) and Danmarks Nationalbank monetary and financial statistics, Producer Price Index survey microdata, Customs export data for export unit values, Firm register, Accounting statistics (regnskabsstatistik) and VAT sales data
Key variables
  • Treatment: firm exposure to banks with high pre-crisis loan-to-deposit ratios
  • Outcome: producer prices (PPI) and export unit values
  • Intermediate outcome: firm bank loans and average interest rate
  • Heterogeneity: product demand elasticity
Period
2005–2010
Sample
1,753 firms (213 linked to the PPI survey, 1,176 to export data) Danish manufacturing firms active 2005–2010 with at least ten employees and an active bank lending relationship in 2007. source

Network Connections and Board Seats: Are Female Networks Less Valuable?

Emma von Essen and Nina Smith · Journal of Labor Economics, 2023 · Individual + firm

Uses Danish employer-employee data from 1995 to 2011 to study how professional networks affect first appointments to supervisory boards. Larger networks raise the chance of a board seat, more so for men, because men's networks connect them more often to larger and listed firms and to other men.

Data
Matched employer-employee data for the Danish labour market
Key variables
  • Outcome: first-time appointment to a supervisory board
  • Explanatory: size of professional network
  • Heterogeneity: gender
  • Network composition: connections to large/listed firms, to men, and to incumbent directors
Period
1995–2011
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish workers observed in employer-employee data 1995–2011, studied for first-time supervisory board appointments. source (abstract)

Constraints on Hours within the Firm

Claudio Labanca and Dario Pozzoli · Journal of Labor Economics, 2022 · Individual + firm

Uses Danish linked employer-employee data with hours and tax-rate variation from the 2010 Danish tax reform. Employees in firms with tight constraints on working hours do not adjust their hours when their taxes change, while employees in more flexible firms respond both directly and through spillovers from their coworkers.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Wage statistics with annual regular and overtime hours (Lønstatistikken, LON), Firm Statistics Register (Firmstat), Danish Foreign Trade Statistics Register, Firm-Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (FIDA), Danish Tax Register (income tax records), O*NET occupational data
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual hours worked (regular plus overtime)
  • Treatment: change in simulated marginal tax rate from the 2010 Danish tax reform (2008–2011 interval)
  • Moderator: firm-level hours constraints/coordination measured by within-firm dispersion of hours
  • Hourly wage: annual earnings over total hours
  • Spillovers: tax changes of coworkers
Period
2003–2011
Sample
more than 400,000 employees and approximately 8,300 firms (787,683 individual-year observations) Full-time employees aged 15–65 in private firms where hours are reported for at least 95% of the workforce, 2003–2011 (working-paper version 'Coordination of Hours within the Firm', IZA DP 12062). source
