Do Employer Pension Contributions Reflect Employee Preferences? Evidence from a Retirement Savings Reform in Denmark

Itzik Fadlon, Jessica Laird and Torben Heien Nielsen · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2016 · Individual + firm

A reform that cut the subsidy to capital pension accounts for top-bracket workers led employers to reduce contributions to those accounts immediately, by more the larger the share of affected employees, and to shift the money to annuity accounts. Employer contributions track employees' savings preferences.

Data
Income tax register (incl. third-party reported pension contributions), Population register (BEF), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA)
Key variables
  • Outcome: employer contribution rate to capital and annuity pension accounts (median within workplace)
  • Treatment: share of workplace employees above the top income tax threshold
  • Policy variation: 1999 reform cutting the deduction for capital pension contributions for top-bracket taxpayers
  • Unit: workplace = 2-digit occupation × firm cell
  • Comparison: individual private (IRA-type) pension contributions
Period
1996–2001
Sample
2,020,705 worker-year observations; 84,764 workplace-year observations (26,775 unique workplaces) Private-sector white-collar wage earners aged 20–60 without self-employment income, in occupation-firm cells with at least five employees. source

The Wage Effects of Offshoring: Evidence from Danish Matched Worker-Firm Data

David Hummels, Rasmus Jørgensen, Jakob Munch and Chong Xiang · American Economic Review, 2014 · Individual + firm

Links product-level firm imports and exports to worker wages. Offshoring raises wages of high-skilled workers and lowers wages of low-skilled workers within job spells, while exporting raises wages for all. Tasks that are routine or easily offshored lose the most.

Data
Firm Statistics Register (FirmStat/FIRM), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-worker link (FIDA), Foreign Trade Statistics Register, Extrastat and Intrastat (UHDI), UN COMTRADE world trade data for instruments, Firm accounts and other firm registers (Data Appendix)
Key variables
  • Outcome: log hourly wage of workers within job spells
  • Treatment: firm offshoring (imports of inputs)
  • Treatment: firm exports
  • Instruments: world export supply and world import demand shocks by product and country (COMTRADE) and transport costs
  • Heterogeneity: worker skill type (high vs low skilled)
  • Outcome: earnings losses of displaced workers
Period
1995–2006 (trade data 1990–2006)
Sample
about 1.95 million worker-firm-year and 9,800 firm-year observations Manufacturing workers in Danish firms that both import and export, excluding single-year job spells, workers changing skill level, and firms with fewer than 50 employees and less than 0.6 million DKK in imports. source

When the Floodgates Open: Northern Firms' Response to Removal of Trade Quotas on Chinese Goods

Hale Utar · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2014 · Individual + firm

The dismantling of Multi-Fibre Arrangement quotas on Chinese textiles at WTO accession exposed Danish textile firms to low-wage competition. Within firms, employment, value added and intangible assets fell and the workforce shifted toward skilled workers, while firms refocused away from products where China's advantage grew.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm accounting statistics (Regnskabsdata / FIRE), International trade data from customs records (Udenrigshandel / UHDI), Domestic production/sales by product (Varestatistik / VARES), 1995–2005, EU SIGL textile and clothing quota licence database
Key variables
  • Treatment: firm produced 2005 MFA-quota goods for China in 1999 (exposure to quota removal)
  • Outcome: firm employment, value added, intangible assets, capital intensity
  • Outcome: workforce skill composition by occupation and education
  • Outcome: product scope / product innovation from transaction-level data
  • Firm fixed effects, year effects
Period
1995–2007
Sample
1,090 unique textile and clothing firms (875 firms, 1995–2005, for the product-portfolio analysis) All Danish textile and clothing firms with at least 0.5 full-time-equivalent employees, 1995–2007, matched to their employees and product transactions. source

Tax Bunching, Income Shifting and Self-Employment

Daniel le Maire and Bertel Schjerning · Journal of Public Economics, 2013 · Individual

Self-employed Danes bunch sharply at the top tax kink, but much of the bunching reflects intertemporal shifting of income through retained earnings in the business rather than real changes in effort. Accounting for shifting implies a much smaller elasticity of real income.

Data
Tax records of Danish self-employed individuals (taxable income, retained earnings)
Key variables
  • Outcome: bunching of taxable income at kinks of the tax schedule
  • Mechanism: intertemporal income shifting via retained earnings in the business tax schemes
  • Estimate: taxable income elasticity (0.43–0.53 ignoring shifting; structural 0.14–0.20)
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish self-employed taxpayers. source (abstract)

Is Business Failure Due to Lack of Effort? Empirical Evidence from a Large Administrative Sample

Mette Ejrnæs and Stefan Hochguertel · Economic Journal, 2013 · Individual

Danish self-employed can voluntarily buy unemployment insurance, which creates a moral hazard test. Using a large administrative panel, insured entrepreneurs are more likely to close their businesses and enter unemployment, with effects that strengthen as the eligibility date approaches, consistent with reduced effort rather than selection alone.

Data
CAM 10% Sample: 10% random sample of the Danish population aged 16+ (linked administrative registers incl. tax and benefit records), Unemployment insurance fund membership records
Key variables
  • Outcome: transition from self-employment to unemployment (registered unemployed >10% of year)
  • Treatment: UI fund membership (insurance status)
  • Instrument: eligibility for early retirement scheme (VERP) available only to UI members
  • Estimator: bivariate random effects probit
Period
1981–1998
Sample
13,434 individuals and 86,092 observations (estimation sample) Denmark-born male Danish citizens aged 25–59 who were self-employed in their first three observed years, excluding agriculture, from the CAM 10% sample. source

Productivity Spillovers Across Firms through Worker Mobility

Andrey Stoyanov and Nikolay Zubanov · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2012 · Individual + firm

Tracks firm-to-firm worker moves in Danish manufacturing. Firms that hire workers from more productive firms see productivity gains of about 0.35 percent a year for an average firm, rising with the education, tenure and skill of the new hires and persisting for several years, consistent with knowledge spillovers carried by workers.

Data
Matched employer-employee data from Statistics Denmark (worker records from 1983, ages 15–65), Manufacturing firm-level accounts data with sales, value added, materials, capital and investment, 1995–2007
Key variables
  • Outcome: receiving firm's log value added per worker normalised by 4-digit industry-year mean
  • Treatment: productivity gap between sending and receiving firms of newly hired workers, weighted by hires' employment share
  • Positive and negative productivity gaps (hires from more vs. less productive firms)
  • Worker characteristics: education, occupation/skill level, experience, wage
  • Firm characteristics: size, industry
Period
1995–2007
Sample
173,929 firm-year observations (about 5.8 million worker-year observations, 668,034 job changers); benchmark regressions use 74,507 and 61,112 firm-year observations All Danish manufacturing firms and their employees with positive annual salary, 1995–2007. source

An Empirical Model of Growth Through Product Innovation

Rasmus Lentz and Dale T. Mortensen · Econometrica, 2008 · Firm

Estimates a Schumpeterian model of firm growth through product innovation on a Danish firm panel. Reallocation of labour from less to more innovative firms accounts for roughly half of aggregate productivity growth, with the remainder coming from innovation within firms.

Data
Danish Business Statistics Register: annual panel of privately owned firms (value added, total wage bill, full-time equivalent employment)
Key variables
  • Firm value added (Y)
  • Total wage bill (W) used to build quality-adjusted employment
  • Full-time equivalent employment (N)
  • Labor productivity: value added per worker and per quality-adjusted worker
  • Moments: firm growth rates including exit, and productivity growth decomposition (within, between, cross, exit)
Period
1992–1997
Sample
unbalanced panel of 4,872 firms All privately owned Danish firms existing in 1992 with more than 20 workers, followed through 1997, excluding later entrants. source

Inside the Family Firm: The Role of Families in Succession Decisions and Performance

Morten Bennedsen, Kasper Meisner Nielsen, Francisco Pérez-González and Daniel Wolfenzon · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2007 · Individual + firm

Uses the gender of a departing CEO's first-born child as an instrument for family succession in Danish firms. Family CEOs hurt performance: operating profitability falls by at least four percentage points relative to firms that hire outside CEOs, with the largest costs in fast-growing industries and large firms.

Data
Købmandsstandens Oplysningsbureau (KOB) firm financial and management data (private, from annual reports), Danish Civil Registration System (CPR) with family links, Danish Commerce and Companies Agency (Erhvervs- og Selskabsstyrelsen) management/board records with CPR numbers, Industry-level data from Statistics Denmark and OECD STAN
Key variables
  • Outcome: operating return on assets (OROA) around CEO transitions
  • Treatment: family CEO succession (incoming CEO related by blood or marriage)
  • Instrument: gender of the departing CEO's firstborn child
  • Heterogeneity: industry growth, skill intensity and firm size
Period
1994–2002 (management data); financial data 1991–2003
Sample
5,334 successions CEO successions in Danish limited liability (public and private) firms between 1994 and 2002, first transition per firm. source

Worker Flows and Job Flows in Danish Manufacturing, 1980–91

Karsten Albæk and Bent E. Sørensen · Economic Journal, 1998 · Individual + firm

One of the first uses of the IDA matched employer-employee data to measure job creation, job destruction and worker flows across Danish manufacturing plants. Worker turnover far exceeds job reallocation, and both are large and persistent at the plant level.

Data
Plant-level data on all Danish manufacturing plants and their workers (register not named in abstract)
Key variables
  • Outcome: worker flows (hires and separations)
  • Outcome: job flows (job creation and destruction)
  • Replacement hiring (separations from and hires into existing jobs)
  • Business-cycle variation
Period
1980–1991
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked All individual manufacturing plants in Denmark, 1980–91. source (abstract)

Gender and the family

Child penalties and the family as the unit of labour supply, health behaviour and insurance.

Working Hours, Top Management Appointments, and Gender: Evidence from Linked Employer-Employee Data

Anders Frederiksen, Takao Kato and Nina Smith · Journal of Labor Economics, 2026 · Individual + firm

Links the Danish Labour Force Survey's detailed hours to register career histories. Long hours, hours relative to peers and nonstandard hours raise the odds of a top management appointment within the firm, and the gender gap in hours, driven mainly by supply-side factors, is a major contributor to the gender gap in reaching top management.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Danish Labour Force Survey (DLFS/AKU) linked on person identifier, Organizational hierarchy codes (occupation-based) from registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: appointment to a top management position between year t and t+1
  • Explanatory: weekly working hours from the Labour Force Survey in year t
  • Heterogeneity: gender (gender gap in hours and promotions)
  • Hierarchy level: top manager, manager, white-collar, blue/grey-collar
  • Controls: experience, tenure, education, children, marital status
Period
1994–2010
Sample
168,499 employees Danish employees randomly sampled in the Labour Force Survey 1994–2010, merged with IDA register data (IZA DP 11675 working-paper version). source

Fertility and Family Labor Supply

Katrine Jakobsen, Thomas H. Jørgensen and Hamish Low · American Economic Review (forthcoming), 2026 · Individual

Uses the 2009 Danish tax reform to show that higher net-of-tax wages for women reduce fertility while higher wages for men raise it. A life-cycle model of couples choosing the timing and number of children, estimated on register data, implies that allowing fertility to adjust raises women's labour supply responsiveness substantially and that human capital depreciation around births drives much of the long-run gender wage gap.

Data
Monthly payroll records (BFL/eIndkomst), Annual income tax data (IND), Population and family register (BEF), family definitions and parent links, Education register (UDDA), Labour market experience from ATP payments, Parental leave spell register (OF)
Key variables
  • Outcome: fertility (births)
  • Treatment: 2009–2018 tax reform changes in marginal net-of-tax wage rate
  • Outcome: labour market participation, part-time and full-time work from pay-slip hours
  • Outcome: annual labour income
  • Control: education (high vs less skilled), labour market experience
Period
2004–2018 (tax sample 2004–2018; estimation sample 2010–2018)
Sample
10,851,444 individual-year observations (estimation sample); 2,531,181 (tax sample) Danish individuals aged 25–60 with an opposite-sex partner, not students, self-employed, retired or on disability pension; tax sample restricted to cohabiting or married women aged 25–40. source

Universal Daycare and Mothers' Working Lifetime

Sarah Sander · Economic Journal, 2026 · Individual

Exploits the roll-out of daycare centres across Danish municipalities to follow mothers over their working lives. Daycare availability raises participation, hours and earnings sixteen years after the first birth, with effects driven by low-educated mothers and mediated partly through fertility and separation.

Data
1970 Census (FT70) with parish of residence, Historical daycare institution yearbooks digitised (1964–1975) and daycare institutions in registers from 1976, Population register with parent-child links and fertility histories (BEF), Earnings from tax records (income registers), 1980–2015, Supplementary Pension Fund register (ATP) contributions for hours and participation, Education registers (UDDA)
Key variables
  • Treatment: daycare availability in the mother's 1970 parish after first birth
  • Outcome: labour force participation by age of first child
  • Outcome: log annual earnings
  • Outcome: weekly hours and full-time employment from ATP contributions
  • Mediators: fertility and separation from child's father
Period
First births 1964–1975; earnings 1980–2015
Sample
370,602 mothers Non-immigrant women resident in Denmark in the 1970 census who had their first child between 1964 and 1975 and stayed in Denmark until the child turned 16. source

Depressed Peers in Early Parenthood

Anne Toft Hansen, Jonas Cuzulan Hirani and Miriam Wüst · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual

Municipal nurses assign new mothers to postnatal peer groups essentially arbitrarily conditional on a few characteristics. Exposure to a depressed peer raises a mother's own mental health care use by 11 percent two years after birth and worsens her self-reported mental health and labour market outcomes.

Data
Municipal health visitor records with mothers' group assignments and EPDS postpartum depression screening, Statistics Denmark registers: GP consultations, psychologist/psychiatrist appointments, hospital contacts for mental illness, sick leave
Key variables
  • Treatment: having a peer in the new-mother group at risk of postnatal depression (EPDS)
  • Outcome: mental health care uptake in the two years after birth
  • Outcome: hospital visits for mental illness
  • Outcome: sick leave / labour market outcomes
Period
2012–2017 (health visitor records)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked First-time mothers assigned by municipal health visitors to new-mother groups in Danish municipalities. source (abstract)

Is Parental Leave Costly for Firms and Coworkers?

Anne Ardila Brenøe, Serena Canaan, Nikolaj A. Harmon and Heather N. Royer · Journal of Labor Economics, 2024 · Individual + firm

Compares small Danish firms where a female employee is about to give birth with similar firms without an impending birth. There is little evidence of negative effects on firm output or coworkers: most firms cover the absence by hiring temporary workers and raising other employees' hours, and only firms unable to reallocate work show losses.

Data
Central Person Registry with parent-child links (CPR/BEF), Parental leave benefit payouts and employer leave reimbursements, Central education register (UDDA), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Annual employer wage payment records (CON and RAS), Mandatory pension contributions for approximate hours (ATP), Value-added tax (VAT) firm sales and purchases data
Key variables
  • Treatment: a female employee at a small private firm giving birth (event year)
  • Outcome: firm employment, FTE hours, wage bill (total and excluding leave), sales, gross profit proxy, survival
  • Outcome: coworker hours, earnings, employment and sick days
  • Mechanism: parental leave days at the firm
  • Design: dynamic difference-in-differences with matched control events
Period
2001–2013 (births 2003–2012)
Sample
24,829 treatment events (births) and 162,151 control events covering 45,940 unique firms; 9,934 treatment events in 16,080 firms after trimming Births to prime-age (21–35) women employed in small private-sector Danish firms, compared with matched control firms without such births. source

Untying the Knot: How Child Support and Alimony Affect Couples' Dynamic Decisions and Welfare

Hanno Foerster · Review of Economic Studies, 2024 · Individual

Estimates a dynamic model of family labour supply, home production, saving and divorce on Danish register and survey data to study post-marital maintenance payments. Alimony carries larger labour supply disincentives and insures consumption less efficiently than child support, so welfare rises when child support is increased and alimony reduced.

Data
Danish register data on income, labour force status and hours worked, Marital histories and birth register (BEF/family links), Maintenance (child support and alimony) payment records and child residence after divorce, Danish Time Use Survey 2001 (DTUS)
Key variables
  • Outcome: labour supply (employer-recorded hours bins) and annual labour income
  • Outcome: divorce
  • Policy: child support and alimony (maintenance) payments
  • Survey: housework hours and relative consumption in couples (DTUS)
Period
1980–2013
Sample
322,732 couples (645,464 individuals) and 4,312,826 couple-year observations; DTUS 2,105 households Danish couples in their first marriage, both spouses aged 25–58, at least one working, excluding couples with children from previous relationships. source

The Gender Application Gap: Do Men and Women Apply for the Same Jobs?

Jonas Fluchtmann, Anita M. Glenny, Nikolaj A. Harmon and Jonas Maibom · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

Uses administrative records of the job applications made by every Danish unemployment insurance recipient. Men and women apply to very different job types even when observationally similar, and these differences in applications account for more than 70 percent of the residual gender wage gap.

Data
Joblog job application records from the public employment service platform (Jobnet), Unemployment insurance payment records / DREAM event-history data, Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Employment Statistics for Employees, monthly earnings and hours (BFL)
Key variables
  • Outcome: characteristics of applied-for jobs (occupation, industry, AKM firm wage premium, commute, hours)
  • Group: gender
  • Outcome: hourly wage in new job one month after hire
  • Predicted 'typical wage' of applied-for jobs
Period
September 2015–September 2017
Sample
105,879 individuals, 114,375 UI spells, 2,911,585 job applications Danish UI recipients entering new spells from September 2015 to September 2017 lasting at least 8 weeks, with at least 4 registered applications and finding a job within one year. source

Does Granting Refugee Status to Family-Reunified Women Improve Their Integration?

Linea Hasager · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

Women who enter Denmark through family reunification usually hold permits that depend on staying married. Granting them independent refugee status improves their economic integration, raises divorce rates and lowers the risk of violence against them, consistent with reduced residency uncertainty and greater bargaining power.

Data
Admission register on immigrant status and residence permit application/approval dates (Danish Immigration Service data via Statistics Denmark), Labour market data from the Danish tax authorities (employment; hours and earnings from 2008), Police registers on victimisation and criminal convictions, Hospital admissions data (National Patient Register) for assault injuries, Central population register (BEF) for demographics, spouse links and divorce
Key variables
  • Treatment: family-reunified woman granted her own refugee status (asylum), staggered timing
  • Outcome: quarterly employment, hours of work and earnings
  • Outcome: victimisation of violence (police records or assault-related hospitalisation)
  • Outcome: divorce
  • Design: staggered difference-in-differences with not-yet-treated women as controls
Period
2001–2019 (hours and earnings from 2008)
Sample
1,349 adult women Women aged 18+ who were family-reunified to a refugee spouse in Denmark between 2001 and 2017 and later applied for and obtained their own refugee status. source

Income Effects and Labour Supply: Evidence from a Child Benefits Reform

Mathias Fjællegaard Jensen and Jack Blundell · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

A 2011 Danish cap on child benefits cut payments to families with several young children and left smaller families unaffected. Mothers' labour supply rises substantially after the cut, mainly on the extensive margin, and sterilisation records are used to control for differences in preferences for more children.

Data
Population register (BEF) for family composition, marital status and cohabitation, Monthly payroll records with hours worked (BFL/eIndkomst), Child benefit payment records (BOBO/BOTI), Register OF (monthly demographics and parental leave status), Sterilisation consultations from the National Patient Register (LPRADM) and health insurance service registers (SYSI, SSSY), Annual income register (IND) for self-employment robustness
Key variables
  • Treatment: 2011 cap on child benefits (DKK 35,000) reducing unearned income for larger families with young children
  • Outcome: monthly hours worked (intensive and extensive margins)
  • Outcome: annual earnings (robustness incl. self-employed)
  • Control/restriction: family sterilisation consultation before the reform
  • Design: difference-in-differences, treated vs unaffected families
Period
September 2008–December 2014 (monthly)
Sample
28,968 treated women and 22,477 treated men Native Danish married or cohabiting parents aged 25–59 over the window with 2–4 children (at least two under 18) in January 2011, not self-employed, observed in a balanced monthly panel. source

The Division of Parental Leave: Empirical Evidence and Policy Design

Thomas Høgholm Jørgensen and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses sharp incentives in the Danish parental leave benefit system for about 190,000 births to estimate parents' willingness to pay for leave. Parents bunch at kinks and are willing to pay for a traditional split with little leave for fathers, and simulations show that earmarked leave works much better when combined with high replacement rates.

Data
Parental leave benefit spell data (parents of children born 2008–2015), incl. benefits paid to parent or employer, Monthly earnings records for all employees (BFL/eIndkomst), from 2008, Danish administrative registers on demographics, family links and firm links (population and labour market registers)
Key variables
  • Outcome: weeks of parental leave taken by mother and father within three years of birth
  • Incentive: individual benefit replacement rates and benefit cap creating kinks in the household budget set
  • Incentive: employer-provided full wage compensation (from benefits paid to employer)
  • Policy: earmarked (father quota) leave and replacement rate counterfactuals in a structural model
Period
Births 2008–2015
Sample
189,974 births Births 2008–2015 to parents in stable employment and not on non-birth public benefits in the three months before birth, excluding births followed by another child within 15 months. source

Neighbourhood Gangs, Crime Spillovers and Teenage Motherhood

Christian Dustmann, Mikkel Mertz and Anna Okatenko · Economic Journal, 2023 · Individual

Uses the random assignment of refugees to municipalities between 1986 and 1998. Gang crime in the assignment neighbourhood raises boys' probability of offending before 19 and girls' probability of teenage motherhood, with lasting consequences: more inactivity for men and lower earnings and more benefit claims for women at ages 19 to 28.

Data
Immigration register, Annual population registers (BEF), Central Police Register (criminal charges and convictions), Education registers (including survey-based register of immigrants' education), Income registers and register-based labour force statistics (RAS), National Patient Register (abortions)
Key variables
  • Outcome: teenage motherhood (child born while aged 15–18); youth crime convictions for boys
  • Treatment: municipal group (gang) crime conviction rate of males 15–25 in assignment year
  • Identification: quasi-random assignment of refugees to municipalities under the 1986–1998 dispersal policy
  • Long-run outcomes: earnings, employment, education
Period
Refugee assignment 1986–1998; population registers 1980–2019
Sample
8,172 assigned refugee children; 3,792 girls in teenage motherhood estimation sample Refugee children from eight refugee-sending countries assigned to Danish municipalities at age 0–14 between 1986 and 1998, observed every year until age 19. source

Network Connections and Board Seats: Are Female Networks Less Valuable?

Emma von Essen and Nina Smith · Journal of Labor Economics, 2023 · Individual + firm

Uses Danish employer-employee data from 1995 to 2011 to study how professional networks affect first appointments to supervisory boards. Larger networks raise the chance of a board seat, more so for men, because men's networks connect them more often to larger and listed firms and to other men.

Matched employer-employee data for the Danish labour market

  • Explanatory: size of professional network
  • Heterogeneity: gender
  • Network composition: connections to large/listed firms, to men, and to incumbent directors
Period
1995–2011
Sample

Globalization, Gender, and the Family

Wolfgang Keller and Hale Utar · Review of Economic Studies, 2022 · Individual + firm

Men and women hit by the same import shock from China adjust differently. Women in their late thirties who are displaced respond by having a child, especially high earners and those who would need to retrain, so a gender-neutral trade shock produces a long-run gender gap through the biological clock.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Central Population Register (CPR/BEF) for births, marriages, divorces and cohabitation, Domestic production database by 10-digit product (Industrial commodity statistics, VARS) to identify firms making quota goods, Industry-level imports from China and other advanced countries (instrument), Parental leave records
Key variables
  • Outcome: new births, marriage, divorce, parental leave
  • Outcome: earnings, hours, unemployment
  • Treatment: 1999 employer produces goods subject to MFA quota removal for China after WTO entry
  • Treatment (economy-wide): industry import penetration from China, instrumented with other countries' imports
  • Heterogeneity: gender and age (biological clock)
Period
1999–2007
Sample
about 1,600,000 workers; about 10,000 textile and clothing workers The 1999 cohort of full-time Danish private-sector workers aged 18–56, followed through 2007. source

Does Biology Drive Child Penalties? Evidence from Biological and Adoptive Families

Henrik Kleven, Camille Landais and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · AER: Insights, 2021 · Individual

Compares event-study child penalties for biological and adoptive mothers using almost 40 years of Danish adoption data. Short-run penalties are slightly larger for biological mothers, but long-run penalties are virtually identical, so biology is not a key driver of child-related gender gaps.

Data
Statistics Denmark administrative registers covering the full population 1980–2017, linked with family identifiers, Adoption registry of Statistics Denmark (1988–2009), augmented with foreign adoptions identified via country of origin and Central Person Register immigration records, Earnings and labour-supply registers incl. hours from the ATP mandated pension scheme, Education registers
Key variables
  • Event: arrival of first child (by birth or adoption)
  • Outcome: annual earnings (wages, salaries, self-employment)
  • Outcome: labour force participation, hours worked, wage rates
  • Comparison group: biological vs adoptive families (biological sample reweighted to match adoptive families)
Period
1980–2017
Sample
around 527,000 first births (biological sample) and around 4,600 first arrivals (adoptive sample) Danish parents whose first child arrived by birth or foreign adoption (before age 5) between 1985 and 2007, with both parents known, alive and resident in Denmark from 5 years before to 10 years after. source

Gender Differences in Negotiation: Evidence from Real Estate Transactions

Steffen Andersen, Julie Marx, Kasper Meisner Nielsen and Lise Vesterlund · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Men appear to secure better prices than women in Danish real estate transactions, but the gap shrinks with better controls for property value and disappears in repeat-sales and inherited-property samples. The price differences reflect men and women demanding different properties, not differences in negotiation.

Data
Property transactions from the Danish Tax and Customs Administration (SKAT), Housing register (BBR), Civil Registration System (CPR), Income and tax-assessed property values from SKAT, Education registers (Ministry of Education), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) employment status, Cause-of-Death Register (Danish National Board of Health)
Key variables
  • Outcome: realised transaction price of house or apartment
  • Key regressor: gender of single buyer/seller
  • Controls: tax-assessed property value, BBR property characteristics, property fixed effects (repeated sales)
  • Death sales: properties inherited by an only child from a deceased parent
Period
1994–2013
Sample
337,685 real estate transactions (97,216 in repeated-sales sample; 13,953 inherited houses and apartments in death-sales sample) Arm's-length transactions of Danish houses and apartments involving single female or single male buyers and sellers, 1994–2013. source

Exposure to More Female Peers Widens the Gender Gap in STEM Participation

Anne Ardila Brenøe and Ulf Zölitz · Journal of Labor Economics, 2020 · Individual

Exploits idiosyncratic variation in the share of female peers across cohorts within Danish high schools. A higher share of female peers lowers women's probability of enrolling in and graduating from STEM programmes and raises men's, widening the gender gap in STEM.

Data
Student/education registers with high school, track and cohort identifiers (UDDA), Population register with parental links (BEF), Parental income and education registers (IND, UDDA), Earnings and employment registers, Occupation codes (STEM occupations), Fertility from population/birth registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: STEM college enrolment and completion (ISCED fields 05, 06, 07)
  • Treatment: proportion of female peers in school-cohort of the math track
  • Identification: within-school across-cohort variation (school and cohort fixed effects)
  • Long-run outcomes: STEM occupation, earnings at age 36, gender wage gap, fertility
Period
High school entry 1980–1994; outcomes followed up to 20 years after entry
Sample
182,211 students in 127 schools (1,877 school-cohort observations) All students entering the math track of Danish academic high schools between 1980 and 1994, excluding schools with tiny or atypical cohorts. source

Children and Gender Inequality: Evidence from Denmark

Henrik Kleven, Camille Landais and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2019 · Individual

Event studies around the birth of the first child show mothers' earnings fall about 20 percent relative to fathers' in the long run, through hours, participation and wage rates. These child penalties now account for most of the remaining gender earnings gap and are transmitted across generations through mothers' own upbringing.

Data
Full-population Danish administrative registers linked by personal ID (earnings, labour supply, occupation, firm, education), 1980–2013, Family and generational links (population register), ATP pension contributions as third-party measure of hours worked, Additional administrative data back to 1964 for intergenerational analysis, Matched employer-employee data (firm family-friendliness), International Social Survey Program (ISSP) 2002 for gender attitudes
Key variables
  • Treatment/event: birth of first child (event time relative to first birth)
  • Outcome: earnings, labour force participation, hours worked (ATP), wage rates
  • Outcome: occupation rank, sector, firm family-friendliness
  • Child penalty: percentage drop of women's relative to men's earnings
  • Intergenerational: maternal grandparents' labour supply
Period
1980–2013 (with data back to 1964 for intergenerational transmission); first births 1985–2003
Sample
around 470,000 births or 15,040,000 individual-year observations Danish parents with a first child born 1985–2003 who are observed alive and resident in Denmark from 5 years before to 10 years after the birth. source

Can Women Have Children and a Career? IV Evidence from IVF Treatments

Petter Lundborg, Erik Plug and Astrid Würtz Rasmussen · American Economic Review, 2017 · Individual

Uses success in in vitro fertilisation treatment among childless Danish women as an instrument for having children, since IVF success is unrelated to labour market history. Children lower women's earnings substantially and persistently, mostly through hourly earnings rather than hours, and the effect is much larger for the first child than for later ones.

Data
IVF register held by the Danish National Board of Health (all IVF treatments in public and private clinics, 1994–2005), Linked administrative registers on education, age, marital status and number of children, Linked labour market registers: labour force status, hours worked, hourly wages, annual earnings, occupation, sector and firm
Key variables
  • Instrument: success of the first IVF treatment
  • Endogenous treatment: having children (fertility at the extensive margin)
  • Outcome: annual earnings
  • Outcome: hourly wages and hours worked / employment
  • Mechanisms: occupation, sector and firm changes
Period
1991–2009 (IVF treatments 1995–2005)
Sample
18,538 treated women Childless women entering their first IVF treatment with embryo implants between 1995 and 2005. source

Mathematics and Gender: Heterogeneity in Causes and Consequences

Juanna Schrøter Joensen and Helena Skyt Nielsen · Economic Journal, 2016 · Individual

A pilot scheme in Danish high schools that let students combine advanced mathematics with chemistry increased advanced maths take-up, especially among girls. The induced maths qualification raised later earnings, with the gains concentrated among students who would otherwise have taken less maths and operating through higher education choices.

Data
Education registers: detailed educational histories, high school branch choice and GPA (Statistics Denmark), Labour market registers: earnings, occupation, industry, self-employment, part-time work
Key variables
  • Outcome: log earnings (measured 18 years after high school graduation)
  • Outcomes: college graduation, field of major, occupation/top corporate jobs, sector
  • Treatment: choice of advanced high school math
  • Instrument: unexpected introduction of high school pilot scheme allowing math–chemistry combination
Period
High school entrants 1984–1986, followed into the labour market
Sample
about 25,658 females and 19,406 males from about 140 high schools Danish high school entrants of 1984–86 who graduated in three years and have at least one year of employment. source

The Dynamics of Marriage and Divorce

Gustaf Bruze, Michael Svarer and Yoram Weiss · Journal of Labor Economics, 2015 · Individual

Estimates a dynamic model of marriage, divorce and remarriage with learning about match quality on long register histories of Danish cohorts. Spousal education is complementary in generating marital surplus, education stabilises marriages, education raises men's share of the surplus, and divorce costs are high both early and late in a marriage.

Data
Population register with marital and cohabitation histories (BEF), Education registers (UDDA), completed education at age 46
Key variables
  • Outcome: transitions into and out of marriage, divorce and remarriage
  • Key variable: completed education group at 46 (high school or less, vocational, college)
  • Assortative matching by education of spouses
  • Structural model parameters: match quality, gains from marriage, divorce costs
Period
1980–2006 (ages 20 to 46)
Sample
61,780 individuals (31,835 men and 29,945 women) All men and women born in Denmark in 1960 with complete residence records 1980–2006 and not married at age 20. source

Family responses and insurance

How spouses and children respond to shocks hitting another family member.

Educational Ambition, Marital Sorting, and Inequality

Frederik Almar, Benjamin Friedrich, Ana Reynoso, Bastian Schulz and Rune Vejlin · Journal of Labor Economics, 2026 · Individual

Classifies detailed Danish education programmes into ambition types by their graduates' starting wages and wage growth. Assortative matching by educational ambition has risen sharply even though sorting by conventional education levels looks flat, and the marriage market accounts for more than 40 percent of the rise in household income inequality since 1980.

Data
Population register with partner links (BEF), Education register with four-digit ISCED programme codes (UDDA), Employment register with hourly wages (IDAN), Income register (IND), Danish Labour Force Survey (LFS/AKU), 2000–2018, Employment register for hours, part-time/full-time (RAS)
Key variables
  • Classification: educational 'ambition types' from programme-level starting wages and wage growth (about 1,800 programmes)
  • Outcome: marital sorting by ambition type (assortative matching)
  • Outcome: household labour income inequality
  • Wage measure: residualised log hourly wages by programme
  • LFS job attributes: hours, evening/weekend/home work
Period
1980–2018
Sample
on average 3,031,511 individuals per year aged 19–60; 1,800,866 individuals in couples per year All individuals living in Denmark aged 19–60 in 1980–2018, with couples defined as married or cohabiting opposite-sex partners. source

Survivors' Mental Health and the Protective Role of Income Stability

Itzik Fadlon, Astrid Sophie Fugleholm and Torben Heien Nielsen · AER: Insights, 2026 · Individual

Documents the immediate, large and lingering rise in mental health medication after a spouse's death across the universe of Danish households. A large share of the spike is explained by the income loss the death imposes, so safety-net liquidity protects survivors' mental health.

Data
Population Registry (BEF) with family linkages, Death Registry, National Patient Registry (LPR), Income Registry (earnings, transfers, pension payouts, capital income), Education Registry, Pharmaceutical Database / prescription register (LMDB), Health Insurance Registry (psychiatrist visits)
Key variables
  • Treatment: spouse's fatal heart attack or stroke (death within 12 months), treated vs later-event control group
  • Outcome: take-up of mental health medication (ATC N05, N06)
  • Mechanism: household income loss from spousal death (instrumented by deceased spouse's earnings share)
  • Heterogeneity: baseline income, net wealth and insurance (DI/OAP, sick pay)
Period
1995–2018
Sample
12,637 treatment and 15,212 control households Danish couples aged 45–80 in which one spouse had a heart attack or stroke between 1999 and 2018 and died within 12 months, excluding the self-employed, balanced four years before and after. source

Fertility and Family Labor Supply

Katrine Jakobsen, Thomas H. Jørgensen and Hamish Low · American Economic Review (forthcoming), 2026 · Individual

Uses the 2009 Danish tax reform to show that higher net-of-tax wages for women reduce fertility while higher wages for men raise it. A life-cycle model of couples choosing the timing and number of children, estimated on register data, implies that allowing fertility to adjust raises women's labour supply responsiveness substantially and that human capital depreciation around births drives much of the long-run gender wage gap.

Data
Monthly payroll records (BFL/eIndkomst), Annual income tax data (IND), Population and family register (BEF), family definitions and parent links, Education register (UDDA), Labour market experience from ATP payments, Parental leave spell register (OF)
Key variables
  • Outcome: fertility (births)
  • Treatment: 2009–2018 tax reform changes in marginal net-of-tax wage rate
  • Outcome: labour market participation, part-time and full-time work from pay-slip hours
  • Outcome: annual labour income
  • Control: education (high vs less skilled), labour market experience
Period
2004–2018 (tax sample 2004–2018; estimation sample 2010–2018)
Sample
10,851,444 individual-year observations (estimation sample); 2,531,181 (tax sample) Danish individuals aged 25–60 with an opposite-sex partner, not students, self-employed, retired or on disability pension; tax sample restricted to cohabiting or married women aged 25–40. source

Effects of Parental Death on Labor Market Outcomes

Mathias Fjællegaard Jensen and Ning Zhang · American Economic Review, 2026 · Individual

Uses sudden first parental deaths and matched controls in Danish administrative data. Earnings fall by about 2 percent for men and 3 percent for women, mental health worsens for both, and women with young children lose the most, around 4 percent, consistent with the loss of informal childcare from grandparents.

Data
Cause of death registers (DODSAARS/DODSAARG), Population register with parent-child links (BEF), Income and labour market registers (IND, AKM, IDAS/IDAN/IDAP), ATP-based hours proxy, Health Insurance Register for GP/psychologist/psychiatrist visits (SYSI/SSSY), National Patient Register (LPR_ADM/LPR_DIAG), Psychiatric Central Research Register (PSYK_ADM/PSYK_DIAG), Prescription register (LMDB), Childcare registers (DAGI/BOERNFB)
Key variables
  • Treatment: first sudden parental death (heart disease, cerebrovascular disease, acute respiratory infection, accidents)
  • Outcome: annual earnings (indexed to pre-death gender mean)
  • Outcome: employment and hours proxy
  • Outcome: mental health care use and prescriptions
  • Control group: matched not-yet-treated individuals
Period
1980–2019 (parental deaths 1985–2014)
Sample
Treated: 21919 daughters and 24354 sons whose mother died first; 74991 daughters and 85022 sons whose father died first (plus matched controls) Danish adults aged 25–50 with two known parents who experienced a sudden first parental death between 1985 and 2014, with matched controls. source

Germs in the Family: The Short- and Long-Term Consequences of Intrahousehold Disease Spread

N. Meltem Daysal, Hui Ding, Maya Rossin-Slater and Hannes Schwandt · American Economic Review, 2026 · Individual

Shows that infant younger siblings are hospitalised for respiratory conditions two to three times as often as their older siblings were, because preschool-aged children bring disease home. Combining birth order with local variation in respiratory disease prevalence, early-life exposure lowers younger siblings' later earnings and education and worsens chronic respiratory and mental health.

Data
National Patient Register (LPR), Medical Birth Register (MFR), Population register (BEF), Register-Based Labour Force Statistics (RAS), Income Statistics Register (IND), Education Register (UDDA), Academic Achievement Register (9th grade test scores), Psychiatric Central Research Register (PCRR), Health Insurance Register (Sygesikringsregisteret), Childcare enrollment data, National Child Database (breastfeeding)
Key variables
  • Outcome: acute respiratory hospitalizations in the first year of life
  • Treatment: birth order (second-born vs first-born) interacted with municipality respiratory disease exposure index
  • Outcome: earnings, labour force participation and income rank at ages 18–32
  • Outcome: high school and college completion; 9th grade test scores
  • Outcome: chronic respiratory and mental healthcare use
Period
Children born 1981–2017; outcomes 1980–2019
Sample
1,230,180 children First- and second-born singleton siblings born in Denmark 1981–2017, at least 11 months apart, in 91 municipalities. source

Universal Daycare and Mothers' Working Lifetime

Sarah Sander · Economic Journal, 2026 · Individual

Exploits the roll-out of daycare centres across Danish municipalities to follow mothers over their working lives. Daycare availability raises participation, hours and earnings sixteen years after the first birth, with effects driven by low-educated mothers and mediated partly through fertility and separation.

Data
1970 Census (FT70) with parish of residence, Historical daycare institution yearbooks digitised (1964–1975) and daycare institutions in registers from 1976, Population register with parent-child links and fertility histories (BEF), Earnings from tax records (income registers), 1980–2015, Supplementary Pension Fund register (ATP) contributions for hours and participation, Education registers (UDDA)

  • Treatment: daycare availability in the mother's 1970 parish after first birth
  • Outcome: labour force participation by age of first child
  • Outcome: log annual earnings
  • Outcome: weekly hours and full-time employment from ATP contributions
  • Mediators: fertility and separation from child's father
Period
First births 1964–1975; earnings 1980–2015
Sample
370,602 mothers Non-immigrant women resident in Denmark in the 1970 census who had their first child between 1964 and 1975 and stayed in Denmark until the child turned 16. source

Depressed Peers in Early Parenthood

Anne Toft Hansen, Jonas Cuzulan Hirani and Miriam Wüst · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual

Municipal nurses assign new mothers to postnatal peer groups essentially arbitrarily conditional on a few characteristics. Exposure to a depressed peer raises a mother's own mental health care use by 11 percent two years after birth and worsens her self-reported mental health and labour market outcomes.

Data
Municipal health visitor records with mothers' group assignments and EPDS postpartum depression screening, Statistics Denmark registers: GP consultations, psychologist/psychiatrist appointments, hospital contacts for mental illness, sick leave
Key variables
  • Treatment: having a peer in the new-mother group at risk of postnatal depression (EPDS)
  • Outcome: mental health care uptake in the two years after birth
  • Outcome: hospital visits for mental illness
  • Outcome: sick leave / labour market outcomes
Period
2012–2017 (health visitor records)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked First-time mothers assigned by municipal health visitors to new-mother groups in Danish municipalities. source (abstract)

Is Parental Leave Costly for Firms and Coworkers?

Anne Ardila Brenøe, Serena Canaan, Nikolaj A. Harmon and Heather N. Royer · Journal of Labor Economics, 2024 · Individual + firm

Compares small Danish firms where a female employee is about to give birth with similar firms without an impending birth. There is little evidence of negative effects on firm output or coworkers: most firms cover the absence by hiring temporary workers and raising other employees' hours, and only firms unable to reallocate work show losses.

Data
Central Person Registry with parent-child links (CPR/BEF), Parental leave benefit payouts and employer leave reimbursements, Central education register (UDDA), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Annual employer wage payment records (CON and RAS), Mandatory pension contributions for approximate hours (ATP), Value-added tax (VAT) firm sales and purchases data

  • Treatment: a female employee at a small private firm giving birth (event year)
  • Outcome: firm employment, FTE hours, wage bill (total and excluding leave), sales, gross profit proxy, survival
  • Outcome: coworker hours, earnings, employment and sick days
  • Mechanism: parental leave days at the firm
  • Design: dynamic difference-in-differences with matched control events
Period
2001–2013 (births 2003–2012)
Sample
24,829 treatment events (births) and 162,151 control events covering 45,940 unique firms; 9,934 treatment events in 16,080 firms after trimming Births to prime-age (21–35) women employed in small private-sector Danish firms, compared with matched control firms without such births. source

Joint Retirement of Couples: Evidence from Discontinuities in Denmark

Esteban García-Miralles and Jonathan M. Leganza · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses age discontinuities in eligibility for Danish early retirement and public pensions to show that when one spouse becomes eligible and retires, the other spouse is substantially more likely to retire as well, quantifying spillovers that matter for evaluating retirement reforms.

Data
Danish administrative registers for the full population 1986–2014 (labour market outcomes, pension benefits, socio-demographics, family/couple links), Annual taxable earnings registers, Monthly earnings records for all employees (eIndkomst/BFL) and monthly pension benefit records, from 2008, Voluntary early retirement pension (VERP) contribution records

  • Running variable (reform-based design): birth date relative to 1 January 1954 cutoff of the 2011 reform raising VERP eligibility age by 6 months
Period

Sample

Untying the Knot: How Child Support and Alimony Affect Couples' Dynamic Decisions and Welfare

Hanno Foerster · Review of Economic Studies, 2024 · Individual

Estimates a dynamic model of family labour supply, home production, saving and divorce on Danish register and survey data to study post-marital maintenance payments. Alimony carries larger labour supply disincentives and insures consumption less efficiently than child support, so welfare rises when child support is increased and alimony reduced.

Key variables

  • Survey: housework hours and relative consumption in couples (DTUS)
Period
1980–2013
Sample
322,732 couples (645,464 individuals) and 4,312,826 couple-year observations; DTUS 2,105 households Danish couples in their first marriage, both spouses aged 25–58, at least one working, excluding couples with children from previous relationships. source

Does Granting Refugee Status to Family-Reunified Women Improve Their Integration?

Linea Hasager · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

Women who enter Denmark through family reunification usually hold permits that depend on staying married. Granting them independent refugee status improves their economic integration, raises divorce rates and lowers the risk of violence against them, consistent with reduced residency uncertainty and greater bargaining power.

Data
Admission register on immigrant status and residence permit application/approval dates (Danish Immigration Service data via Statistics Denmark), Labour market data from the Danish tax authorities (employment; hours and earnings from 2008), Police registers on victimisation and criminal convictions, Hospital admissions data (National Patient Register) for assault injuries, Central population register (BEF) for demographics, spouse links and divorce
Key variables
  • Treatment: family-reunified woman granted her own refugee status (asylum), staggered timing
  • Outcome: quarterly employment, hours of work and earnings
  • Outcome: victimisation of violence (police records or assault-related hospitalisation)
  • Outcome: divorce
  • Design: staggered difference-in-differences with not-yet-treated women as controls
Period
2001–2019 (hours and earnings from 2008)
Sample
1,349 adult women Women aged 18+ who were family-reunified to a refugee spouse in Denmark between 2001 and 2017 and later applied for and obtained their own refugee status. source

Income Effects and Labour Supply: Evidence from a Child Benefits Reform

Mathias Fjællegaard Jensen and Jack Blundell · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

A 2011 Danish cap on child benefits cut payments to families with several young children and left smaller families unaffected. Mothers' labour supply rises substantially after the cut, mainly on the extensive margin, and sterilisation records are used to control for differences in preferences for more children.

Data
Population register (BEF) for family composition, marital status and cohabitation, Monthly payroll records with hours worked (BFL/eIndkomst), Child benefit payment records (BOBO/BOTI), Register OF (monthly demographics and parental leave status), Sterilisation consultations from the National Patient Register (LPRADM) and health insurance service registers (SYSI, SSSY), Annual income register (IND) for self-employment robustness
Key variables
  • Treatment: 2011 cap on child benefits (DKK 35,000) reducing unearned income for larger families with young children
  • Outcome: monthly hours worked (intensive and extensive margins)
  • Outcome: annual earnings (robustness incl. self-employed)
  • Control/restriction: family sterilisation consultation before the reform
  • Design: difference-in-differences, treated vs unaffected families
Period
September 2008–December 2014 (monthly)
Sample
28,968 treated women and 22,477 treated men Native Danish married or cohabiting parents aged 25–59 over the window with 2–4 children (at least two under 18) in January 2011, not self-employed, observed in a balanced monthly panel. source

On the Family Origins of Human Capital Formation: Evidence from Donor Children

Petter Lundborg, Erik Plug and Astrid Würtz Rasmussen · Review of Economic Studies, 2024 · Individual

Uses Danish children conceived through sperm and egg donation to separate nurture from genetic transmission, since donors are not selected on parental traits. Only mothers' education matters once genes are removed: fathers' schooling has no effect on children's test scores, while mothers' schooling raises reading scores as much as among non-donor children.

Data
Danish IVF register (Danish Health Data Authority), Danish Education Register (UDDA), National test scores in reading and math (from 2010), Administrative registers on demographics and labour market (birth year, gender, marital status, children, earnings)
Key variables
  • Outcome: child average standardized national test score (reading and math)
  • Explanatory: mother's and father's years of schooling
  • Variation: donor sperm / donor egg conception (genetically unrelated parent)
  • Controls: month of first donor treatment and clinic fixed effects (waiting-list assignment)
Period
IVF treatments 1994–2007; demographics 1991–2016; labour market 1991–2012; tests from 2010
Sample
19,509 non-donor IVF children, 820 sperm donor children and 157 egg donor children (97 matched to egg donors) Children conceived through IVF treatments in Denmark 1994–2007 with at least one national test score, compared with 30% random samples of non-adopted (650,930) and adopted (2,674) children. source

The Division of Parental Leave: Empirical Evidence and Policy Design

Thomas Høgholm Jørgensen and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses sharp incentives in the Danish parental leave benefit system for about 190,000 births to estimate parents' willingness to pay for leave. Parents bunch at kinks and are willing to pay for a traditional split with little leave for fathers, and simulations show that earmarked leave works much better when combined with high replacement rates.

Data
Parental leave benefit spell data (parents of children born 2008–2015), incl. benefits paid to parent or employer, Monthly earnings records for all employees (BFL/eIndkomst), from 2008, Danish administrative registers on demographics, family links and firm links (population and labour market registers)
Key variables
  • Outcome: weeks of parental leave taken by mother and father within three years of birth
  • Incentive: individual benefit replacement rates and benefit cap creating kinks in the household budget set
  • Incentive: employer-provided full wage compensation (from benefits paid to employer)
  • Policy: earmarked (father quota) leave and replacement rate counterfactuals in a structural model
Period
Births 2008–2015
Sample
189,974 births Births 2008–2015 to parents in stable employment and not on non-birth public benefits in the three months before birth, excluding births followed by another child within 15 months. source

Globalization, Gender, and the Family

Wolfgang Keller and Hale Utar · Review of Economic Studies, 2022 · Individual + firm

Men and women hit by the same import shock from China adjust differently. Women in their late thirties who are displaced respond by having a child, especially high earners and those who would need to retrain, so a gender-neutral trade shock produces a long-run gender gap through the biological clock.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Central Population Register (CPR/BEF) for births, marriages, divorces and cohabitation, Domestic production database by 10-digit product (Industrial commodity statistics, VARS) to identify firms making quota goods, Industry-level imports from China and other advanced countries (instrument), Parental leave records
Key variables
  • Outcome: new births, marriage, divorce, parental leave
  • Outcome: earnings, hours, unemployment
  • Treatment: 1999 employer produces goods subject to MFA quota removal for China after WTO entry
  • Treatment (economy-wide): industry import penetration from China, instrumented with other countries' imports
  • Heterogeneity: gender and age (biological clock)
Period
1999–2007
Sample
about 1,600,000 workers; about 10,000 textile and clothing workers The 1999 cohort of full-time Danish private-sector workers aged 18–56, followed through 2007. source

Child's Gender, Young Fathers' Crime, and Spillover Effects in Criminal Behavior

Christian Dustmann and Rasmus Landersø · Journal of Political Economy, 2021 · Individual

Uses the gender of a first-born child as a natural experiment for young fathers in Denmark. Having a son rather than a daughter lowers the father's subsequent criminal behaviour, and the reduction spills over to the father's peers and later to the child, which identifies both a direct parenting response and network effects in crime.

Data
Birth register (child's gender, parent links), Population register (BEF) for demographics and family links, Central Police Register crime data (charges and convictions), Victim register (from 2001), Income, education and occupation registers, Residential register with neighbourhood classification
Key variables
  • Outcome: young father's crime convictions and charges
  • Treatment: first child is a son vs a daughter
  • Spillover outcome: crime of male peers in the father's neighbourhood
  • Peer group: males within ±3 years of father's age in the same neighbourhood
  • Outcome: neighbourhood crime victimisation
Period
Children born 1991–2004; crime data from 1990
Sample
2,803 first-time fathers aged 20 or younger; 101,132 male peers in 2,114 neighbourhoods Danish men who fathered their first child at age 20 or younger between 1991 and 2004, plus male peers in their neighbourhoods. source

Does Biology Drive Child Penalties? Evidence from Biological and Adoptive Families

Henrik Kleven, Camille Landais and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · AER: Insights, 2021 · Individual

Compares event-study child penalties for biological and adoptive mothers using almost 40 years of Danish adoption data. Short-run penalties are slightly larger for biological mothers, but long-run penalties are virtually identical, so biology is not a key driver of child-related gender gaps.

Data
Statistics Denmark administrative registers covering the full population 1980–2017, linked with family identifiers, Adoption registry of Statistics Denmark (1988–2009), augmented with foreign adoptions identified via country of origin and Central Person Register immigration records, Earnings and labour-supply registers incl. hours from the ATP mandated pension scheme, Education registers
Key variables
  • Event: arrival of first child (by birth or adoption)
  • Outcome: annual earnings (wages, salaries, self-employment)
  • Outcome: labour force participation, hours worked, wage rates
  • Comparison group: biological vs adoptive families (biological sample reweighted to match adoptive families)
Period
1980–2017
Sample
around 527,000 first births (biological sample) and around 4,600 first arrivals (adoptive sample) Danish parents whose first child arrived by birth or foreign adoption (before age 5) between 1985 and 2007, with both parents known, alive and resident in Denmark from 5 years before to 10 years after. source

Family Labor Supply Responses to Severe Health Shocks: Evidence from Danish Administrative Records

Itzik Fadlon and Torben Heien Nielsen · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2021 · Individual

Compares households hit by a fatal versus a non-fatal health shock at the same time. After a spouse's death the surviving spouse raises labour supply to self-insure, while after a non-fatal shock spouses reduce labour supply to care for the patient. Children of the household adjust less.

Data
Cause of Death Registry, National Patient Registry (LPR) hospitalisation records with ICD diagnoses, Income and transfer registers (earnings, government transfers, pension and annuity payouts, capital income), 1980–2011, Third-party reported liquid wealth balances, 1984–2011, Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), incl. ATP-based full-/part-time employment, Household linkages of spouses and cohabiting partners (population registers)
Key variables
  • Treatment: spousal death (fatal health shock) or first non-fatal heart attack/stroke
  • Control group: households experiencing the same shock a few years later
  • Outcome: surviving/healthy spouse's labour force participation and earnings
  • Outcome: household income from transfers, pensions and insurance; liquid wealth
  • Heterogeneity: household liquidity, income loss, age
Period
1980–2011 (shocks 1985–2011)
Sample
310,720 treated and 409,190 control households (fatal shocks); 37,437 treated and 54,887 control households (non-fatal heart attacks and strokes) Danish married and cohabiting couples with both spouses aged 45–80 in the year of the (actual or placebo) shock; the non-fatal shock sample is limited to spouses under 60 surviving at least three years. source

Education, Birth Order and Family Size

Jesper Bagger, Javier A. Birchenall, Hani Mansour and Sergio Urzúa · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Develops a model of parental investment with birth order preferences and human capital cost differences that delivers a generalised quantity–quality trade-off. Danish administrative data confirm its predictions: at any given parity, children in smaller families have more education, and the average child's education falls with family size even after accounting for birth order.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), 1980–2006, with mother-child links, Precise date of birth from Statistics Denmark (to identify twin/multiple births)
Key variables
  • Outcome: years of education (measured in 2006)
  • Key regressors: birth order and family size
  • Instrument: twin birth at last birth (twins at 2nd, 3rd or 4th birth)
  • Controls: parental education, gender, age
Period
1980–2006 (outcomes measured in 2006)
Sample
1,256,031 children in 581,159 families (excluding single-child families) Individuals aged 25–41 in 2006 whose mother was alive and present in IDA during 1980–2006, in families with completed fertility, siblings defined by common mother. source

Home Prices, Fertility, and Early-Life Health Outcomes

N. Meltem Daysal, Michael Lovenheim, Nikolaj Siersbæk and David N. Wasser · Journal of Public Economics, 2021 · Individual

For Danish homeowners aged 20 to 44, a 100,000 kroner rise in home prices raises the probability of giving birth by about 2.35 percent. Birth outcomes improve too, but mostly because of who gives birth, and there is no effect on children's health in the first five years.

  • Comparison group: renters

1992–2011 (fertility analysis 1995–2006)
Sample
1,105,559 observations on 198,435 women (125,903 births) Women aged 20–44 who became first-time homeowners between 1993 and 2004, purchasing between ages 18 and 42. source

Sibling Spillovers

Sandra E. Black, Sanni Breining, David N. Figlio, Jonathan Guryan, Krzysztof Karbownik, Helena Skyt Nielsen, Jeffrey Roth and Marianne Simonsen · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Identifies spillovers within the family from having a disabled younger sibling using administrative data from both Denmark and Florida. Comparing first- and second-born children in families with three or more children, the second child, who spends more early childhood with the younger sibling, is differentially affected when the third child is disabled.

  • Comparison: second-born vs first-born child (difference-in-differences)
  • Controls: parental age, education, labour market status, earnings, immigrant status
Period
Danish births 1990–2001; Florida births 1994–2002
Sample
Denmark: 134,277 families with first two children observed, 28,581 families with first three children (4,430 with a disabled third child by age five); Florida: 80,879 and 9,987 families Families in Denmark (births 1990–2001) and Florida (births 1994–2002) with at least three children where the first two children have the same father. source

Birth Order and Delinquency: Evidence from Denmark and Florida

Sanni Breining, Joseph Doyle, David N. Figlio, Krzysztof Karbownik and Jeffrey Roth · Journal of Labor Economics, 2020 · Individual

In both Denmark and Florida, second-born boys are substantially more likely than their first-born brothers to be disciplined in school and to enter the criminal justice system. The evidence points to reduced parental time investment for later-born children, rather than differences in health at birth or neighbourhood, as the mechanism.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR), Population register with parent and sibling links (BEF), Central Crime Register (convictions, sentences, incarceration), School registers (9th grade exit exam grades), Register of Daycare Institutions, National Patient Register (LPR) and Psychiatric Central Register, Parental education, employment, earnings and maternity leave registers, Florida birth records linked to school and juvenile justice records
Key variables
  • Outcome: criminal conviction by age 16 and 21; sentenced to prison; incarcerated
  • Treatment: being second-born vs first-born boy (sibling fixed effects)
  • Outcome: 9th grade exit exam grades in reading and math
  • Mechanisms: maternal leave and employment, daycare enrolment ages 1–3, school starting age, psychiatric diagnoses by age 10
  • Controls: parental age, education, employment, family income, immigrant status
Period
Danish birth cohorts 1981–1990; Florida births 1994–2002
Sample
Denmark: 121,100 children in 60,550 families (first- and second-born boys); 448,979 singleton births 1981–1990 in the full Danish population Danish families with two or more children with the same father, comparing first-born and second-born boys born 1981–1990 (plus Florida comparison samples). source

Children and Gender Inequality: Evidence from Denmark

Henrik Kleven, Camille Landais and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2019 · Individual

Event studies around the birth of the first child show mothers' earnings fall about 20 percent relative to fathers' in the long run, through hours, participation and wage rates. These child penalties now account for most of the remaining gender earnings gap and are transmitted across generations through mothers' own upbringing.

Data
Full-population Danish administrative registers linked by personal ID (earnings, labour supply, occupation, firm, education), 1980–2013, Family and generational links (population register), ATP pension contributions as third-party measure of hours worked, Additional administrative data back to 1964 for intergenerational analysis, Matched employer-employee data (firm family-friendliness), International Social Survey Program (ISSP) 2002 for gender attitudes
Key variables
  • Treatment/event: birth of first child (event time relative to first birth)
  • Outcome: earnings, labour force participation, hours worked (ATP), wage rates
  • Outcome: occupation rank, sector, firm family-friendliness
  • Child penalty: percentage drop of women's relative to men's earnings
  • Intergenerational: maternal grandparents' labour supply
Period
1980–2013 (with data back to 1964 for intergenerational transmission); first births 1985–2003
Sample
around 470,000 births or 15,040,000 individual-year observations Danish parents with a first child born 1985–2003 who are observed alive and resident in Denmark from 5 years before to 10 years after the birth. source

Family Health Behaviors

Itzik Fadlon and Torben Heien Nielsen · American Economic Review, 2019 · Individual

A severe health shock to one family member, such as a heart attack or stroke, makes spouses and adult children raise their own preventive behaviour: more check-ups, more preventive medication and lifestyle changes. Responses are sharp, immediate and persistent, and they reflect updating about one's own risk.

Data
Prescription Drug Database (LMDB), 1995–2011, Health Insurance Registry (SSR, primary-care and specialist contacts), 1985–2011, National Patient Registry (LPR), inpatient 1980–2011 and outpatient 1994–2011, Cause of Death Registry (DAR), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) for demographics, education and coworker links, Family links (spouses, parents–children) from population registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: family member's first heart attack or stroke (non-fatal), or death (fatal shock)
  • Outcome: statin and other cardiovascular drug take-up
  • Outcome: preventive care such as GP visits and cholesterol testing
  • Control group: households experiencing the same shock five years later
  • Exposure groups: spouses, adult biological children, siblings, in-laws, coworkers
Period
1980–2011 (shocks between 1985 and 2011)
Sample
Non-fatal shocks, spouses: 20,381 treated and 28,699 control (prime age 25–55), 37,828 treated and 36,392 control (older 55–85); biological children: 63,323 treated and 68,437 control (25–40), 39,783 treated and 32,926 control (40–65) Family members and close coworkers of all Danish individuals who had a first heart attack or stroke (or died) between 1985 and 2011. source

Spillovers in Education Choice

Juanna Schrøter Joensen and Helena Skyt Nielsen · Journal of Public Economics, 2018 · Individual

Exploits the Danish high school pilot that made advanced mathematics cheaper to take for some cohorts. An older sibling's advanced maths choice raises the probability that the younger sibling also chooses advanced maths, with spillovers that depend on the siblings' gender composition.

Data
Danish administrative registers on high school enrolment, course packages and completion (Statistics Denmark education registers), Parent and sibling links from population registers, Parental education and income registers, Earnings registers (younger siblings' earnings at ages 30–35)
Key variables
  • Outcome: younger sibling chooses advanced math-science course package in high school
  • Endogenous regressor: older sibling's advanced math-science choice
  • Instrument: older sibling enrolled in a high school that unexpectedly introduced the pilot scheme (PilotIntro)
  • Outcomes: younger sibling's college/STEM education at age 30 and log earnings at ages 30–35
  • Controls: parental education and income, county and entry-cohort fixed effects
Period
High school entry cohorts 1984–1987 (older siblings) and 1988–1991 (younger siblings, main sample); register data from 1980 onwards
Sample
7,786 sibling pairs (main closely spaced sample); 17,691 sibling pairs in the wider sample with age gap < 10 years Older siblings entering academic high school 1984–1987 who graduated in three years, paired with younger siblings entering high school 1988–1991 with an age gap of at most 4 years. source

Parental Responses to Child Support Obligations: Evidence from Administrative Data

Maya Rossin-Slater and Miriam Wüst · Journal of Public Economics, 2018 · Individual

Uses discontinuities in the Danish child support schedule by fathers' income to estimate how higher obligations affect separated parents. The paper documents responses in fathers' income, subsequent family formation and the parents' living arrangements.

Data
Danish administrative registers on children born 1985–2008 linked to parents and siblings (population/family registers), Income and tax records including tax-deductible child support payments, Labour market registers (employment, earnings, disability insurance, early retirement), Birth records (fertility through 2010), Education registers, Danish Longitudinal Survey of Children (DALSC) and Children and Youth in Denmark (CYD) survey (descriptive, supplementary)
Key variables
  • Treatment: father's average child support obligation (from Danish child support guideline schedule)
  • Identifying variation: income thresholds in the child support guidelines (simulated instrument)
  • Outcome: father's annual child support payment
  • Outcome: father–child co-residence (physical custody)
  • Outcomes: post-separation fertility and labour supply of both parents
Period
1998–2010 (separation years 1999–2008)
Sample
73,303 fathers (71,575 unique mothers) Fathers observed every year 1998–2010 who separated from their oldest child's mother (or had a first child while not cohabiting) in 1999–2008, with one or two children under 18 and separation-year income near the first three guideline thresholds. source

Can Women Have Children and a Career? IV Evidence from IVF Treatments

Petter Lundborg, Erik Plug and Astrid Würtz Rasmussen · American Economic Review, 2017 · Individual

Uses success in in vitro fertilisation treatment among childless Danish women as an instrument for having children, since IVF success is unrelated to labour market history. Children lower women's earnings substantially and persistently, mostly through hourly earnings rather than hours, and the effect is much larger for the first child than for later ones.

Data
IVF register held by the Danish National Board of Health (all IVF treatments in public and private clinics, 1994–2005), Linked administrative registers on education, age, marital status and number of children, Linked labour market registers: labour force status, hours worked, hourly wages, annual earnings, occupation, sector and firm
Key variables
  • Instrument: success of the first IVF treatment
  • Endogenous treatment: having children (fertility at the extensive margin)
  • Outcome: annual earnings
  • Outcome: hourly wages and hours worked / employment
  • Mechanisms: occupation, sector and firm changes
Period
1991–2009 (IVF treatments 1995–2005)
Sample
18,538 treated women Childless women entering their first IVF treatment with embryo implants between 1995 and 2005. source

The Dynamics of Marriage and Divorce

Gustaf Bruze, Michael Svarer and Yoram Weiss · Journal of Labor Economics, 2015 · Individual

Estimates a dynamic model of marriage, divorce and remarriage with learning about match quality on long register histories of Danish cohorts. Spousal education is complementary in generating marital surplus, education stabilises marriages, education raises men's share of the surplus, and divorce costs are high both early and late in a marriage.

Data
Population register with marital and cohabitation histories (BEF), Education registers (UDDA), completed education at age 46
Key variables
  • Outcome: transitions into and out of marriage, divorce and remarriage
  • Key variable: completed education group at 46 (high school or less, vocational, college)
  • Assortative matching by education of spouses
  • Structural model parameters: match quality, gains from marriage, divorce costs
Period
1980–2006 (ages 20 to 46)
Sample
61,780 individuals (31,835 men and 29,945 women) All men and women born in Denmark in 1960 with complete residence records 1980–2006 and not married at age 20. source

Public Pension Programmes and the Retirement of Married Couples in Denmark

Paul Bingley and Gauthier Lanot · Journal of Public Economics, 2007 · Individual

Estimates a dynamic discrete choice model of couples' joint retirement and saving on a 1 percent register sample of Danish couples, using the introduction and reforms of the early retirement programme (efterløn) for identification. Retirement responds asymmetrically for men and women to their own and their spouse's income flows.

Data
1% sample of Danish couples of potential retirement age from an administrative register
Key variables
  • Outcome: retirement decisions of husband and wife (joint retirement)
  • Identifying variation: introduction and subsequent reforms of the publicly financed early retirement programme (efterløn)
  • Explanatory: own and spouse's income flows
  • Model: dynamic discrete choice model with saving
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked A 1% register sample of Danish married couples of potential retirement age. source (abstract)

Health

Hospital diagnoses, prescriptions and births as shocks and as outcomes.
