Survivors' Mental Health and the Protective Role of Income Stability

Itzik Fadlon, Astrid Sophie Fugleholm and Torben Heien Nielsen · AER: Insights, 2026 · Individual

Documents the immediate, large and lingering rise in mental health medication after a spouse's death across the universe of Danish households. A large share of the spike is explained by the income loss the death imposes, so safety-net liquidity protects survivors' mental health.

Data
Population Registry (BEF) with family linkages, Death Registry, National Patient Registry (LPR), Income Registry (earnings, transfers, pension payouts, capital income), Education Registry, Pharmaceutical Database / prescription register (LMDB), Health Insurance Registry (psychiatrist visits)
Key variables
  • Treatment: spouse's fatal heart attack or stroke (death within 12 months), treated vs later-event control group
  • Outcome: take-up of mental health medication (ATC N05, N06)
  • Mechanism: household income loss from spousal death (instrumented by deceased spouse's earnings share)
  • Heterogeneity: baseline income, net wealth and insurance (DI/OAP, sick pay)
Period
1995–2018
Sample
12,637 treatment and 15,212 control households Danish couples aged 45–80 in which one spouse had a heart attack or stroke between 1999 and 2018 and died within 12 months, excluding the self-employed, balanced four years before and after. source

Universal Investments in Toddler Health: Learning from a Large Government Trial

Jennifer L. Baker, Lise G. Bjerregaard, Christian M. Dahl, Torben S. D. Johansen, Emil N. Sørensen and Miriam Wüst · Economic Journal, 2026 · Individual

A 1960s Copenhagen government trial of preventive health care for toddlers, documented from handwritten nurse records and linked to the registers, improved a good adult health index by five percent of a standard deviation. Gains are largest for initially disadvantaged children and girls and fade for later cohorts as the counterfactual policy environment improved.

Data
Transcribed handwritten Copenhagen nurse records (Copenhagen City Archives), 1959–1967 cohorts, Central Person Register (CPR) linkage, Education registers (UDDA), Income registers (IND), Death register, National Patient Register (LPR), Copenhagen Perinatal Cohort (CPC), births 1959–1961, Family links to parents, siblings, children and spouses
Key variables
  • Treatment: born on the first three days of the month (extended toddler home-visiting trial)
  • First stage: nurse record contains a treatment table (take-up)
  • Outcome: adult SES index (years of education, post-secondary education, earnings and employment ages 30–50)
  • Outcome: adult good-health index (hospital contacts and diagnoses), mortality
  • Second-generation outcomes of trial children's own children
Period
Births 1959–1967 (trial covered births January 1959–April 1968); register outcomes 1977–2020
Sample
87,336 individuals Children born in Copenhagen 1959–1967 with transcribed nurse records linked to Statistics Denmark administrative data, excluding twins. source

Sick of Your Poor Neighborhood? Quasi-Experimental Evidence on Neighborhood Effects on Health

Linea Hasager and Mia Jørgensen · Journal of Public Economics, 2026 · Individual

Uses the quasi-random dispersal of refugees across Danish neighbourhoods from 1986 to 1998. Refugees placed in low-income neighbourhoods had a 12 percent higher risk of developing a lifestyle-related disease 8 to 15 years later, driven by neighbours and the local environment rather than health care access, ethnic networks or labour market outcomes.

Data
National Patient Register (LPR), Health Insurance Register (SYSI, SSSY), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Income register (IND), Population register (BEF) and address/mobility registers (BEFBOP, BEFADR), Migration register (VNDS), Criminal convictions register (KRAF), O*NET occupational data merged via occupation codes
Key variables
  • Treatment: assignment to a bottom- vs top-income-third parish (neighbourhood median household disposable income) under the 1986–1998 Refugee Spatial Dispersal Policy
  • Outcome: lifestyle-related disease diagnosis (circulatory, nutritional/metabolic, COPD, hip arthrosis, alcohol-related) 2–15 and 8–15 years after immigration
  • Outcome: mental disorder diagnoses
  • Mechanisms: health care access, employment and earnings, neighbour characteristics
Period
1985–2017 (refugees arriving 1986–1998)
Sample
21,965 refugees Refugees aged 18–64 at arrival from nine refugee-sending countries who arrived in Denmark in 1986–1998 and were assigned a municipality under the dispersal policy (excluding family-reunified spouses). source

Effects of Parental Death on Labor Market Outcomes

Mathias Fjællegaard Jensen and Ning Zhang · American Economic Review, 2026 · Individual

Uses sudden first parental deaths and matched controls in Danish administrative data. Earnings fall by about 2 percent for men and 3 percent for women, mental health worsens for both, and women with young children lose the most, around 4 percent, consistent with the loss of informal childcare from grandparents.

Data
Cause of death registers (DODSAARS/DODSAARG), Population register with parent-child links (BEF), Income and labour market registers (IND, AKM, IDAS/IDAN/IDAP), ATP-based hours proxy, Health Insurance Register for GP/psychologist/psychiatrist visits (SYSI/SSSY), National Patient Register (LPR_ADM/LPR_DIAG), Psychiatric Central Research Register (PSYK_ADM/PSYK_DIAG), Prescription register (LMDB), Childcare registers (DAGI/BOERNFB)
Key variables
  • Treatment: first sudden parental death (heart disease, cerebrovascular disease, acute respiratory infection, accidents)
  • Outcome: annual earnings (indexed to pre-death gender mean)
  • Outcome: employment and hours proxy
  • Outcome: mental health care use and prescriptions
  • Control group: matched not-yet-treated individuals
Period
1980–2019 (parental deaths 1985–2014)
Sample
Treated: 21919 daughters and 24354 sons whose mother died first; 74991 daughters and 85022 sons whose father died first (plus matched controls) Danish adults aged 25–50 with two known parents who experienced a sudden first parental death between 1985 and 2014, with matched controls. source

Germs in the Family: The Short- and Long-Term Consequences of Intrahousehold Disease Spread

N. Meltem Daysal, Hui Ding, Maya Rossin-Slater and Hannes Schwandt · American Economic Review, 2026 · Individual

Shows that infant younger siblings are hospitalised for respiratory conditions two to three times as often as their older siblings were, because preschool-aged children bring disease home. Combining birth order with local variation in respiratory disease prevalence, early-life exposure lowers younger siblings' later earnings and education and worsens chronic respiratory and mental health.

Data
National Patient Register (LPR), Medical Birth Register (MFR), Population register (BEF), Register-Based Labour Force Statistics (RAS), Income Statistics Register (IND), Education Register (UDDA), Academic Achievement Register (9th grade test scores), Psychiatric Central Research Register (PCRR), Health Insurance Register (Sygesikringsregisteret), Childcare enrollment data, National Child Database (breastfeeding)
Key variables
  • Outcome: acute respiratory hospitalizations in the first year of life
  • Treatment: birth order (second-born vs first-born) interacted with municipality respiratory disease exposure index
  • Outcome: earnings, labour force participation and income rank at ages 18–32
  • Outcome: high school and college completion; 9th grade test scores
  • Outcome: chronic respiratory and mental healthcare use
Period
Children born 1981–2017; outcomes 1980–2019
Sample
1,230,180 children First- and second-born singleton siblings born in Denmark 1981–2017, at least 11 months apart, in 91 municipalities. source

Breaking Bad: How Health Shocks Prompt Crime

Steffen Andersen, Elin Colmsjö, Gianpaolo Parise and Kim Peijnenburg · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2026 · Individual

Uses plausibly exogenous timing of cancer diagnoses in Danish registers to show that a health shock raises the probability of committing a crime, both first offences and re-offences, persistently. Patients replace lost legal income with illegal income and face a lower expected cost of punishment because of reduced survival, and welfare programmes that cushion the income loss dampen the effect.

Data
Danish administrative registers on cancer diagnoses (timing of diagnosis), Crime registers (first crimes and re-offenses), Income and welfare program registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: timing of a cancer diagnosis (health shock)
  • Outcome: probability of committing a crime (first crimes and re-offenses)
  • Mechanism: loss of legal income
  • Mechanism: survival probability (expected cost of punishment)
  • Heterogeneity: welfare program coverage
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish individuals diagnosed with cancer, using variation in the timing of diagnosis. source (abstract)

Changing Tracks: Human Capital Investment after Loss of Ability

Anders Humlum, Jakob R. Munch and Pernille Plato · American Economic Review, 2025 · Individual

Uses quasi-random work accidents in Danish administrative data to show that injured workers enrol in bachelor's programmes that build on their previous training. Exploiting differences in how stackable degrees are, higher education moves injured workers from disability benefits into full-time jobs paying 25 percent more than before injury, and reskilling subsidies pay for themselves four times over.

Data
Industrial Injury Register (Arbejdsskaderegisteret) / workers' compensation claims from AES, National Patient Register (LPR), Health Insurance Register (Sygesikringsregisteret), Prescription Drug Database (LMDB), Education Register (UDDA), Course Participant Register (VEUV), Danish Register for Evaluation of Marginalization (DREAM), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), linked to O*NET, Population register (BEF/POP)
Key variables
  • Treatment: work accident causing loss of earning capacity (vs. matched non-injured workers)
  • Outcome: enrollment in and completion of formal degrees and courses
  • Outcome: earnings, hours and occupation (task content)
  • Outcome: disability and other social transfers
  • Severity: assessed earning capacity loss and personal impairment
Period
1995–2017 (work injury claims 1998–2017)
Sample
14,481 injury events (14,369 distinct individuals); 4,568 events (4,528 individuals) for workers with vocational degrees with access to higher education Full-time employed Danish workers (excluding military) who suffered an accepted physical work accident with positive earning capacity loss, compared with matched non-injured workers. source

Depressed Peers in Early Parenthood

Anne Toft Hansen, Jonas Cuzulan Hirani and Miriam Wüst · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual

Municipal nurses assign new mothers to postnatal peer groups essentially arbitrarily conditional on a few characteristics. Exposure to a depressed peer raises a mother's own mental health care use by 11 percent two years after birth and worsens her self-reported mental health and labour market outcomes.

Data
Municipal health visitor records with mothers' group assignments and EPDS postpartum depression screening, Statistics Denmark registers: GP consultations, psychologist/psychiatrist appointments, hospital contacts for mental illness, sick leave
Key variables
  • Treatment: having a peer in the new-mother group at risk of postnatal depression (EPDS)
  • Outcome: mental health care uptake in the two years after birth
  • Outcome: hospital visits for mental illness
  • Outcome: sick leave / labour market outcomes
Period
2012–2017 (health visitor records)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked First-time mothers assigned by municipal health visitors to new-mother groups in Danish municipalities. source (abstract)

The Causal Effect of Scaling up Access to Psychotherapy

Benjamin Ly Serena · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual

A 2008 reform introduced 60 percent coverage of private psychotherapy for patients aged 18 to 37 with depression or anxiety. Using registers from 1995 to 2019, coverage reduces psychiatric hospital contacts, physical health care use and suicide attempts, leaves employment unchanged, and saves more in other health spending than it costs.

Data
National Patient Register (LPR), psychiatric and somatic hospital contacts, National Health Insurance Service Register (Sygesikringsregistret), primary care and psychologist services, Prescription drug purchases (psychiatric drugs), DREAM register (employment, sick leave, disability pension), Yearly socioeconomic registers (population, income, education)
Key variables
  • Treatment: eligibility for 60% psychotherapy coverage introduced in 2008 for depression/anxiety patients aged 18–37
  • Running variable: age relative to the age-38 eligibility cutoff (RD design)
  • Comparison: ages 18–37 vs 38–50 before/after the reform (difference-in-differences)
  • Outcome: psychiatric hospital contacts and other mental health services
  • Outcome: physical health care use and suicide attempts
  • Outcome: employment, sick leave and disability pension receipt
Period
1995–2019 (published abstract; the 2021 CEBI working paper uses 2000–2016)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked All Danish residents, comparing those just below and above the age-38 eligibility cutoff and ages 18–37 versus 38–50 around the 2008 reform. source

No Pain, No Gain: Work Demand, Work Effort, and Worker Health

David Hummels, Jakob R. Munch and Chong Xiang · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual + firm

Links Danish worker-firm data to hospital and prescription records to study how rising workload affects health. Increases in firm sales lead workers to log longer hours and raise the probability of stress, depression, heart disease and stroke, most for older and already strained workers; the welfare loss from sickness offsets nearly a quarter of the earnings gain.

Data
Firm Statistics Register (FIRM), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-worker link (FIDA), Accounts Statistics (Regnskabsstatistik), Prodcom firm sales by product (VARS), Health care registers: prescription drug purchases, doctor visits and hospitalizations (National Patient Register), Monthly payroll records with earnings and hours (eIndkomst/BFL), Monthly firm VAT sales and material purchases (FIKS)
Key variables
  • Treatment: log firm sales (Prodcom), with a Bartik/shift-share instrument in robustness checks
  • Outcome: monthly hours worked and earnings
  • Outcome: stress/depression indicators (anti-depressant use, psychiatrist visits, burnout hospitalizations)
  • Outcome: heart disease drugs, heart attack/stroke and alcoholism hospitalizations
  • Fixed effects: job-spell fixed effects
Period
1995–2012 (health data 1996–2012; monthly payroll data 2008–2012)
Sample
3,336,244 annual worker-year observations; 8,834,846 monthly observations Full-time workers aged 20–60 in private-sector Danish firms in the Prodcom sales data with at least 50 employees and positive imports and exports, excluding single-year employment relationships. source

The Physician-Patient Match and Health Inequality

Ida Lykke Kristiansen and Sophie Yanying Sheng · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual

Measures general practitioners' childhood socioeconomic status from their parents' education in the registers. Low-SES patients matched to a GP of similar background have substantially lower mortality, while high-SES patients are unaffected, cutting the SES mortality gradient by a quarter through more intensive care, better detection of chronic conditions and better adherence.

Data
National Health Service Register: patient-to-GP clinic assignment, Service Provider Registry (physician IDs), Cause of Death Register, National Health Insurance Service Registry: visits, services, reimbursements, Hospital/patient data and first-line treatments for chronic conditions, Education registers and parent links (BEF/UDDA) for patient SES and physicians' parental education
Key variables
  • Treatment: assignment to a low-SES (parental education) physician after clinic closure
  • Outcome: patient mortality and cause of death
  • Outcome: detection/treatment of chronic conditions (CVC, cancer, diabetes, COPD)
  • Outcome: health care utilization (visits, services per visit, reimbursements)
  • Patient SES: highest completed education (primary school = low SES)
Period
1995–2019 (clinic closures 1999–2016)
Sample
over 352,000 adult patients affected by 894 clinic closures; 1,019,005 observations in the main mortality regression Danish adults aged 40–70 whose primary care clinic closed between 1999 and 2016, observed at least four years before the closure. source

Healthy at Work? Evidence from a Social Experimental Evaluation of a Firm-Based Wellness Program

Marianne Simonsen and Lars Skipper · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2025 · Individual + firm

A large social experiment across worksites, followed for up to twelve years in the registers, evaluates an employer-sponsored health programme. Employees at treated worksites see fewer GP visits and buy fewer prescription drugs for up to seven years, with no change in hospitalisations, absence or turnover and some spillovers within the family.

Data
Healthy-at-Work randomised experiment records (work-unit assignment, September 2008), Primary care physician consultation records (Statistics Denmark registers), Prescription drug purchase records, Hospital contact / hospitalisation records, Employment, sickness absence and turnover registers, Socio-economic background registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: work-unit randomised to the Healthy-at-Work wellness program
  • Outcome: primary care physician visits
  • Outcome: prescription drug purchases (any, months supply)
  • Outcome: hospitalisations (somatic, psychiatric)
  • Outcome: sickness absence and turnover
  • Spillover outcome: spouses' health care use
Period
Randomisation 2008; follow-up to 2020 (PCP visits and work outcomes), 2019 (prescriptions), 2018 (hospitalisations)
Sample
7,541 workers (3,735 treatment, 3,806 control) in 314 work-units Elderly-care health workers in 314 work-units of 110 employers across 11 Danish municipalities enrolled in the Healthy-at-Work experiment. source

Do Medical Treatments Work for Work? Evidence from Breast Cancer Patients

N. Meltem Daysal, William N. Evans, Mikkel Hasse Pedersen and Mircea Trandafir · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2025 · Individual

A Danish guideline change shifted which breast cancer patients received radiation therapy, giving quasi-experimental variation in treatment. Radiotherapy reproduces the survival gains found in trials and has economic returns: ten years after diagnosis it raises employment by 37 percent and earnings by 45 percent, undoing most of the post-diagnosis employment drop.

Data
Danish Breast Cancer Cooperative Group (DBCG) clinical Breast Cancer Database, Register of Causes of Death, Register-Based Labour Force Statistics (RAS), Income Statistics Register, DREAM weekly transfer register, Population Register (BEF), Education Register
Key variables
  • Treatment: receipt of radiotherapy
  • Instrument: January 1995 DBCG guideline change expanding radiotherapy eligibility (diagnosed after 1995 x eligible group)
  • Outcome: all-cause and breast cancer mortality
  • Outcome: employment, annual labour earnings, gross income
  • Outcome: sickness and disability benefit receipt
Period
1990–2008 (diagnoses 1990–1998)
Sample
2,823 observations (women) High-risk pre-menopausal women under 55 diagnosed with breast cancer in Denmark 1990–1998 and enrolled in the DBCG89 clinical trial. source

Understanding the Rise in Life Expectancy Inequality

Gordon B. Dahl, Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Torben Heien Nielsen and Benjamin Ly Serena · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

Decomposes widening life expectancy gaps between rich and poor into changes in age-specific mortality and differences in survivability to each age. Lower survivability of the poor explains the entire rise in Danish life expectancy inequality; cardiovascular gains helped everyone, but lifestyle-related survivability diverged.

Data
Population registers (BEF, FAIN): sex and age, Income register (IND): household income net of universal transfers, Death registers (DODAARS, DODAASG) with cause of death, US mortality by income from Chetty et al. (2016), IRS tax data
Key variables
  • Outcome: period life expectancy by income group
  • Income rank: household income measured two years before mortality
  • Decomposition: mortality changes vs. survivability
  • Cause of death: cardiovascular, cancer, lifestyle-related, other
Period
2001–2014
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Universe of Danish residents 2001–2014, compared with US tax-return-based mortality data. source

Public Health Policy at Scale: Impact of a Government-Sponsored Information Campaign on Infant Mortality in Denmark

Onur Altındağ, Jane Greve and Erdal Tekin · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

A nationwide campaign against sudden infant death syndrome, delivered mainly through universal nurse home visits, cut infant mortality by 17 percent in a regression discontinuity on population birth data. Effects were 11 to 14 times larger for low-birthweight and preterm infants and concentrated among low-SES families.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR): birth weight, gestational age, parity, maternal age, immigrant status, Register of Causes of Death (exact date and cause of death), Education register (parental education)
Key variables
  • Running variable: birth month relative to December 1991
  • Treatment: birth after the 1991 change in infant sleep-position guidelines
  • Outcome: SIDS mortality per 10,000 births
  • Outcome: infant and child mortality per 10,000 births
  • Heterogeneity: low birth weight, preterm, maternal education
Period
Live births 1973–2006
Sample
568,106 births (SIDS outcome, MSE-optimal bandwidth of 52 months); 632,449 births for infant mortality (58 months) All live births in Denmark within the RD bandwidth around the December 1991 guideline change. source

Spillover Effects of Early-Life Medical Interventions

N. Meltem Daysal, Marianne Simonsen, Mircea Trandafir and Sanni Breining · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2022 · Individual

Extra medical treatment for very-low-birth-weight newborns, identified from a birth weight threshold, improves not only the treated infant but also the outcomes of older siblings, who do better in school. Family resources and parental time respond to the child's health.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR): birth weight, gestational age, parity, Parent and sibling links (BEF), 9th-grade exam test score register (reading and math), Education/enrollment registers, Prescription drug register (antidepressant use, from 1995), Health registers for diagnoses (intellectual disability, ADHD) and mortality, Income and labour market registers for parents
Key variables
  • Running variable: focal child's birth weight relative to 1,500-gram VLBW cutoff
  • Outcome: siblings' standardized 9th-grade reading and math test scores
  • Outcome: sibling enrollment beyond compulsory education by age 19 and high-school track
  • Mechanisms: focal-child mortality and mental health; maternal antidepressant use; parental income, employment, days worked
Period
Focal children born 1982–1993; test scores 2001–2010
Sample
5,827 sibling observations (siblings of 3,677 focal children) Siblings (same mother) of Danish children born 1982–1993 with birth weight between 1,300 and 1,700 grams. source

The Returns to Nursing: Evidence from a Parental-Leave Program

Benjamin U. Friedrich and Martin B. Hackmann · Review of Economic Studies, 2021 · Individual + firm

A Danish parental-leave programme offering up to a year off per child aged 0 to 8 induced a nurse shortage that hospitals and nursing homes could not replace. Nurse employment fell by 15 percent in hospitals and 10 percent in nursing homes, 30-day readmissions of heart attack patients rose, and nursing home mortality increased sharply.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) for health workers, plants and industries, Population register (BEF) for children's ages, residence and household characteristics, Social benefits records of parental leave spells (from 1995), National Patient Register (LPR), inpatient hospitalisations 1990–2000, Register of Causes of Death (DAR), 1991–2000
Key variables
  • Treatment: 1994 parental leave programme; exposure = share of nurses eligible times estimated take-up, by health sector and county
  • Outcome: nurse employment in hospitals and nursing homes
  • Outcome: 30-day hospital readmission rates and other quality-of-care measures
  • Outcome: nursing home (elderly) mortality
  • Eligibility: having a youngest child aged 8 or younger
Period
1990–2000
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish nurses, nursing assistants and doctors, and hospital patients and nursing home residents, aggregated to health sector by county, 1990–2000. source

Family Labor Supply Responses to Severe Health Shocks: Evidence from Danish Administrative Records

Itzik Fadlon and Torben Heien Nielsen · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2021 · Individual

Compares households hit by a fatal versus a non-fatal health shock at the same time. After a spouse's death the surviving spouse raises labour supply to self-insure, while after a non-fatal shock spouses reduce labour supply to care for the patient. Children of the household adjust less.

Data
Cause of Death Registry, National Patient Registry (LPR) hospitalisation records with ICD diagnoses, Income and transfer registers (earnings, government transfers, pension and annuity payouts, capital income), 1980–2011, Third-party reported liquid wealth balances, 1984–2011, Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), incl. ATP-based full-/part-time employment, Household linkages of spouses and cohabiting partners (population registers)
Key variables
  • Treatment: spousal death (fatal health shock) or first non-fatal heart attack/stroke
  • Control group: households experiencing the same shock a few years later
  • Outcome: surviving/healthy spouse's labour force participation and earnings
  • Outcome: household income from transfers, pensions and insurance; liquid wealth
  • Heterogeneity: household liquidity, income loss, age
Period
1980–2011 (shocks 1985–2011)
Sample
310,720 treated and 409,190 control households (fatal shocks); 37,437 treated and 54,887 control households (non-fatal heart attacks and strokes) Danish married and cohabiting couples with both spouses aged 45–80 in the year of the (actual or placebo) shock; the non-fatal shock sample is limited to spouses under 60 surviving at least three years. source

Spot Price Biases in Non-Linear Health Insurance Contracts

Marianne Simonsen, Lars Skipper, Niels Skipper and Anne Illemann Christensen · Journal of Public Economics, 2021 · Individual

The Danish prescription drug reimbursement scheme has coverage that rises with annual spending and resets each year. Consumers look forward-looking around the reset but react too strongly to current prices within the year, and liquidity constraints rather than plan literacy or cognitive decline explain the spot-price bias.

Data
Population-wide individual data on prescription drug purchases and reimbursement under the Danish non-linear drug insurance scheme (registers not named in the abstract)
Key variables
  • Incentive: spot price of drugs as a non-linear function of year-to-date spending
  • Incentive: end-of-year reset of the coverage (deductible) year
  • Outcome: timing and amount of prescription drug purchases
  • Heterogeneity: liquidity constraints, plan literacy, cognitive decline
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Population-wide data on Danish consumers of prescription drugs covered by the non-linear reimbursement scheme (details not available from the abstract). source (abstract)

Home Prices, Fertility, and Early-Life Health Outcomes

N. Meltem Daysal, Michael Lovenheim, Nikolaj Siersbæk and David N. Wasser · Journal of Public Economics, 2021 · Individual

For Danish homeowners aged 20 to 44, a 100,000 kroner rise in home prices raises the probability of giving birth by about 2.35 percent. Birth outcomes improve too, but mostly because of who gives birth, and there is no effect on children's health in the first five years.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR), National Patient Register (LPR), State's Sales and Valuation Registry (public property valuations and sales prices), Population register (BEF) and other registers for demographics, education, household income and partner status
Key variables
  • Treatment: once-lagged one-year change in estimated home value (public valuation adjusted by local sale-price ratio)
  • Outcome: indicator for giving birth
  • Outcomes: birth weight, low birth weight, very low birth weight, prematurity
  • Outcomes: hospital admissions, ER visits and days hospitalised in first year and ages 1–5
  • Comparison group: renters
Period
1992–2011 (fertility analysis 1995–2006)
Sample
1,105,559 observations on 198,435 women (125,903 births) Women aged 20–44 who became first-time homeowners between 1993 and 2004, purchasing between ages 18 and 42. source

Sibling Spillovers

Sandra E. Black, Sanni Breining, David N. Figlio, Jonathan Guryan, Krzysztof Karbownik, Helena Skyt Nielsen, Jeffrey Roth and Marianne Simonsen · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Identifies spillovers within the family from having a disabled younger sibling using administrative data from both Denmark and Florida. Comparing first- and second-born children in families with three or more children, the second child, who spends more early childhood with the younger sibling, is differentially affected when the third child is disabled.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR), National Patient Register (LPR) for disability diagnoses, 9th grade exit exam grades (school/education registers), Parental education, labour market, earnings and leave registers, Florida birth records linked to Florida public school records (FCAT)
Key variables
  • Outcome: cohort-standardised 9th grade exit exam GPA (Denmark); FCAT test scores (Florida)
  • Treatment: third-born sibling has a disability (diagnosed in medical registers) observed by age five
  • Comparison: second-born vs first-born child (difference-in-differences)
  • Controls: parental age, education, labour market status, earnings, immigrant status
Period
Danish births 1990–2001; Florida births 1994–2002
Sample
Denmark: 134,277 families with first two children observed, 28,581 families with first three children (4,430 with a disabled third child by age five); Florida: 80,879 and 9,987 families Families in Denmark (births 1990–2001) and Florida (births 1994–2002) with at least three children where the first two children have the same father. source

What Is the Added Value of Preschool for Poor Children? Long-Term and Intergenerational Impacts and Interactions with an Infant Health Intervention

Maya Rossin-Slater and Miriam Wüst · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2020 · Individual

Access to preschool targeted at low-income children between 1933 and 1960 raised adult education, earnings and survival past 65, and the children of exposed women obtained more education by 25. Exposure to the nurse home visiting programme in infancy reduces preschool's added value, so the two programmes are partial substitutes.

Data
Archival data on all approved Danish preschools 1933–1960 (establishment and approval year, slots), Nurse Home Visiting program implementation dates by municipality, Statistical Commune Data Archive (elections and censuses) for municipality controls, Municipal live births and infant deaths, DigDag historical administrative geography (parish-municipality mapping), Administrative population register data 1980–2012 (education, earnings, survival, hospital admissions, diagnoses), Mother-child links (Danish Civil Registration System)
Key variables
  • Treatment: approved preschool in municipality of birth at age 3
  • Interaction: exposure to the infant Nurse Home Visiting program
  • Outcome: education and earnings around age 50
  • Outcome: survival past age 65, hospital admissions, diagnoses
  • Intergenerational outcome: oldest child's educational attainment by age 25
  • Controls: municipality and birth-year fixed effects, county-by-year trends, municipality characteristics
Period
Cohorts born 1930–1957; outcomes 1980–2012; children born 1955–1987
Sample
1,640,214 observations (Danish-born with valid parish codes); 880,708 observations in municipalities with an approved preschool by 1960, collapsed to 3,862 municipality × birth-year cells Danish-born individuals born 1930–1957 with a valid parish of birth who are observed after 1980, focusing on the 138 municipalities that ever had an approved preschool. source

Family Health Behaviors

Itzik Fadlon and Torben Heien Nielsen · American Economic Review, 2019 · Individual

A severe health shock to one family member, such as a heart attack or stroke, makes spouses and adult children raise their own preventive behaviour: more check-ups, more preventive medication and lifestyle changes. Responses are sharp, immediate and persistent, and they reflect updating about one's own risk.

Data
Prescription Drug Database (LMDB), 1995–2011, Health Insurance Registry (SSR, primary-care and specialist contacts), 1985–2011, National Patient Registry (LPR), inpatient 1980–2011 and outpatient 1994–2011, Cause of Death Registry (DAR), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) for demographics, education and coworker links, Family links (spouses, parents–children) from population registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: family member's first heart attack or stroke (non-fatal), or death (fatal shock)
  • Outcome: statin and other cardiovascular drug take-up
  • Outcome: preventive care such as GP visits and cholesterol testing
  • Control group: households experiencing the same shock five years later
  • Exposure groups: spouses, adult biological children, siblings, in-laws, coworkers
Period
1980–2011 (shocks between 1985 and 2011)
Sample
Non-fatal shocks, spouses: 20,381 treated and 28,699 control (prime age 25–55), 37,828 treated and 36,392 control (older 55–85); biological children: 63,323 treated and 68,437 control (25–40), 39,783 treated and 32,926 control (40–65) Family members and close coworkers of all Danish individuals who had a first heart attack or stroke (or died) between 1985 and 2011. source

Universal Investment in Infants and Long-Run Health: Evidence from Denmark's 1937 Home Visiting Program

Jonas Hjort, Mikkel Sølvsten and Miriam Wüst · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2017 · Individual

Uses the staggered municipal roll-out of the 1937 universal infant home visiting programme and follows treated cohorts into middle age in the registers. Treated individuals have higher survival between ages 45 and 64, fewer hospital nights and less cardiovascular disease, consistent with early nutrition and disease environment programming adult health.

Data
Archival records of home visiting program implementation dates by municipality, 1937–1949 (Danish National Archives), Danish Commune Archive / Statistical Commune Data Archive (pre-1937 municipal characteristics from censuses and elections), Medical Reports for the Kingdom of Denmark (1936 infant mortality by medical district), Administrative population data with date and parish of birth (cohorts 1935–1949), Danish Inpatient Register / National Patient Register (LPR), 1980–2012, Danish Death Register (cause of death), 1980–2012, DigDag historical administrative geography
Key variables
  • Treatment: born in a municipality after it implemented the home visiting program
  • Variation: staggered municipal implementation timing 1937–1949
  • Outcome: age-specific survival rates, ages 46–64
  • Outcome: hospital nights between ages 45–64
  • Outcome: diagnoses of heart disease, cardiovascular disease, diabetes, cancer; cause of death
  • Controls: municipality and cohort fixed effects, pre-1937 municipal characteristics
Period
Birth cohorts 1935–1949; outcomes 1980–2012
Sample
876,912 individuals, collapsed to 20,078 municipality × birth cohort × treatment-status cells Danish-born individuals born 1935–1949 outside hospitals who survived and lived in Denmark to age 45, matched to birth municipality. source

Migration, neighbourhoods and refugees

Refugee dispersal as quasi-random assignment, immigration effects on natives, and benefit cuts.

Sick of Your Poor Neighborhood? Quasi-Experimental Evidence on Neighborhood Effects on Health

Linea Hasager and Mia Jørgensen · Journal of Public Economics, 2026 · Individual

Uses the quasi-random dispersal of refugees across Danish neighbourhoods from 1986 to 1998. Refugees placed in low-income neighbourhoods had a 12 percent higher risk of developing a lifestyle-related disease 8 to 15 years later, driven by neighbours and the local environment rather than health care access, ethnic networks or labour market outcomes.

Data
National Patient Register (LPR), Health Insurance Register (SYSI, SSSY), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Income register (IND), Population register (BEF) and address/mobility registers (BEFBOP, BEFADR), Migration register (VNDS), Criminal convictions register (KRAF), O*NET occupational data merged via occupation codes
Key variables
  • Treatment: assignment to a bottom- vs top-income-third parish (neighbourhood median household disposable income) under the 1986–1998 Refugee Spatial Dispersal Policy
  • Outcome: lifestyle-related disease diagnosis (circulatory, nutritional/metabolic, COPD, hip arthrosis, alcohol-related) 2–15 and 8–15 years after immigration
  • Outcome: mental disorder diagnoses
  • Mechanisms: health care access, employment and earnings, neighbour characteristics
Period
1985–2017 (refugees arriving 1986–1998)
Sample
21,965 refugees Refugees aged 18–64 at arrival from nine refugee-sending countries who arrived in Denmark in 1986–1998 and were assigned a municipality under the dispersal policy (excluding family-reunified spouses). source

Permanent Residency and Refugee Immigrants' Skill Investment

Jacob Nielsen Arendt, Christian Dustmann and Hyejin Ku · Journal of Labor Economics, 2025 · Individual

A Danish reform tightened the employment and language requirements refugees must meet to obtain permanent residency. Rather than encouraging investment, it lowered overall employment and barely moved language proficiency, because refugees with weak pre-reform prospects reduced effort when the requirements looked out of reach.

Data
Population, income, education and immigration registers linked via the Central Person Registry, Danish language test results, Employment in annual full-time equivalents (register data)
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual employment in full-time equivalents
  • Outcome: passing the Danish language test at intermediate or higher level
  • Treatment: temporary residency in Jan–Oct 2004 (exposed to the 2007 permanent-residency reform) vs Jan–Oct 2003
  • Heterogeneity: pre-reform employment or language performance type
  • Time: years since temporary residency (YSR 1–7)
Period
Residency cohorts 2003–2004, followed seven years since temporary residency (to 2011)
Sample
767 unique individuals; 5,122 person-year observations Refugees aged 18–64 who applied for asylum after 28 February 2002 and received temporary residency in Jan–Oct 2003 (control) or Jan–Oct 2004 (treatment). source

Background Matters, but Not Whether Parents Are Immigrants: Outcomes of Children Born in Denmark

Mathias Fjællegaard Jensen and Alan Manning · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2025 · Individual

Children born in Denmark to immigrant parents have lower earnings, more unemployment, less education, more transfers and more convictions than children of Danish-born parents. Conditional on parental socioeconomic characteristics, however, they do as well or slightly better, so the gaps reflect deprived backgrounds rather than immigrant parentage.

Data
Danish register data linking children to parents (Statistics Denmark, 1980–2017), Income and earnings registers (annual labour income incl. self-employment, transfers), Monthly employment register (hours, hourly wages, from 2008), Education registers (incl. survey-based registration of foreign education of immigrants), Criminal justice registers (convictions, prison sentences, dropped charges / not-guilty verdicts), Occupation codes (ISCO88)
Key variables
  • Group: number of parents born outside Denmark (0, 1, 2; Greenland/Faroe Islands)
  • Outcome: earnings and unemployment at age 30
  • Outcome: public transfers, completed education by age 30
  • Outcome: criminal convictions, prison sentences, dropped charges
  • Controls: parental income, unemployment years, occupation and education during childhood
Period
1980–2017
Sample
396,417 children (Table 1 total); 362,460 with two Danish-born parents, 10,399 with two immigrant parents, 20,908 with one immigrant parent, 2,650 with a parent from Greenland/Faroe Islands All children born in Denmark 1980–1987 with both parents known, followed to age 30. source

Refugee Benefit Cuts

Christian Dustmann, Rasmus Landersø and Lars Højsgaard Andersen · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

A 2002 reform cut welfare benefits for newly arrived refugees by roughly half. Employment rose modestly in the short run and then faded, while disposable income fell sharply. The cut raised crime among adults and harmed children's school enrolment and outcomes.

Data
Danish Immigration Service residence permit records, Income register from tax authority records (earnings, transfers, taxes), Hourly wage and occupation data, Labour market register (employment status), Population register (BEF), Crime registers: police and court records of charges and convictions, Job openings data from Jobindex.dk
Key variables
  • Treatment: residency granted after the July 2002 Start Aid benefit cut
  • Running variable: date residency was granted relative to the reform
  • Outcome: employment, labour earnings, transfer and disposable income
  • Outcome: crime convictions (property crime)
  • Heterogeneity: local labour demand in assignment municipality (low-skill job openings per unemployed)
Period
Residency granted 2001–2003; outcomes up to 10 years after residency
Sample
4,843 individuals Refugees and family-reunified refugees granted residency in Denmark between January 2001 and December 2003 aged 18–55, excluding Afghans, former Yugoslavs and re-migrants. source

Unintended Consequences of Welfare Cuts on Children and Adolescents

Christian Dustmann, Rasmus Landersø and Lars Højsgaard Andersen · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2024 · Individual

The 2002 benefit cut for newly arrived refugees reduced family disposable income by 30 percent over five years. Children exposed at preschool or school age obtained lower grades, less education and lower adult earnings, and teenagers at exposure saw large increases in convictions for violent and property crime.

Data
Danish Immigration Service residence permit records, Historical Migrations database, Income register from tax authority records, Register on Completed Education, Daycare register, 9th-grade exam grades, 6th-grade national language test scores and school well-being survey (Ministry of Education), Crime registers: police and court records
Key variables
  • Treatment: parents granted residency after the 2002 Start Aid welfare cut
  • Running variable: residency date relative to the reform
  • Outcome: 9th-grade GPA, 6th-grade language test scores, completed education
  • Outcome: adult earnings and employment
  • Outcome: criminal convictions
Period
Residency granted January 2001–December 2003; income data through 2019
Sample
6,888 individuals (3,406 adults aged 18–45 with children and 3,482 children aged 0–18) Refugee families with children granted residency in Denmark within 18 months of the 2002 reform, excluding Afghans, former Yugoslavs and emigrants. source

Does Granting Refugee Status to Family-Reunified Women Improve Their Integration?

Linea Hasager · Journal of Public Economics, 2024 · Individual

Women who enter Denmark through family reunification usually hold permits that depend on staying married. Granting them independent refugee status improves their economic integration, raises divorce rates and lowers the risk of violence against them, consistent with reduced residency uncertainty and greater bargaining power.

Data
Admission register on immigrant status and residence permit application/approval dates (Danish Immigration Service data via Statistics Denmark), Labour market data from the Danish tax authorities (employment; hours and earnings from 2008), Police registers on victimisation and criminal convictions, Hospital admissions data (National Patient Register) for assault injuries, Central population register (BEF) for demographics, spouse links and divorce
Key variables
  • Treatment: family-reunified woman granted her own refugee status (asylum), staggered timing
  • Outcome: quarterly employment, hours of work and earnings
  • Outcome: victimisation of violence (police records or assault-related hospitalisation)
  • Outcome: divorce
  • Design: staggered difference-in-differences with not-yet-treated women as controls
Period
2001–2019 (hours and earnings from 2008)
Sample
1,349 adult women Women aged 18+ who were family-reunified to a refugee spouse in Denmark between 2001 and 2017 and later applied for and obtained their own refugee status. source

Comparing the Effects of Policies for the Labor Market Integration of Refugees

Mette Foged, Linea Hasager and Giovanni Peri · Journal of Labor Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses quasi-experimental variation from several Danish reforms to compare, in a common framework, the long-run employment and earnings effects of language training, active labour market programmes and benefit cuts for refugees, and ranks the policies by their effects and costs.

Data
Admission Register (OPHG), Migration register (VNDS), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Income register (IND), Population register (BEF), Education register (UDDA), Family register (FAM)
Key variables
  • Outcome: employment (any employment in the year)
  • Outcome: annual gross labour earnings (2015 USD)
  • Policy 1: initial dispersal across municipalities (conditional random assignment)
  • Policy 2: 1999 language training reform (RD at 1 January 1999 admission)
  • Policy 3: Start Aid welfare benefit cut (RD at 1 July 2002 admission)
  • Policy 4: 'Industry Packages' ALMP rolled out across municipalities 2015–2018
Period
1986–2008 refugee arrivals (outcomes followed up to 15 years; ALMP analysis January 2008–April 2019)
Sample
56,192 adult refugees Refugees and family-reunified refugees aged 18–55 on arrival who settled in a Danish municipality 1986–2008, split into policy-specific arrival cohorts. source

Language Training and Refugees' Integration

Mette Foged, Linea Hasager, Giovanni Peri, Jacob Nielsen Arendt and Iben Bolvig · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

A 1999 reform improved language training for newly recognised refugees. A regression discontinuity around the cut-off date shows a permanent, positive effect on earnings that emerges after classes are completed, together with more schooling and a higher probability of working in complex jobs.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Income register (IND), Education enrollment and completion registers, Crime register: charges and convictions, Population register (BEF), Admission/residence permit register (OPHG), Migration register (VNDS) and internal mobility register (FLYT), Register for Labour Market Policy Measures (AMFORA), Danish language training database (Danskuddannelsesdatabasen), O*NET task intensities
Key variables
  • Running variable: date refugee status granted relative to January 1, 1999
  • Treatment: 1999 reform expanding Danish language training
  • Outcome: annual earnings and employment
  • Outcome: education, occupation task complexity, crime
  • Outcome (children): lower secondary completion and crime
Period
Admissions 1997–2000; followed until 2016 (children's outcomes until 2018)
Sample
8,558 individuals; children sample of 9,730 Refugees and reunified family members aged 18–49 granted asylum in Denmark between January 1, 1997 and December 31, 2000. source

Neighbourhood Gangs, Crime Spillovers and Teenage Motherhood

Christian Dustmann, Mikkel Mertz and Anna Okatenko · Economic Journal, 2023 · Individual

Uses the random assignment of refugees to municipalities between 1986 and 1998. Gang crime in the assignment neighbourhood raises boys' probability of offending before 19 and girls' probability of teenage motherhood, with lasting consequences: more inactivity for men and lower earnings and more benefit claims for women at ages 19 to 28.

Data
Immigration register, Annual population registers (BEF), Central Police Register (criminal charges and convictions), Education registers (including survey-based register of immigrants' education), Income registers and register-based labour force statistics (RAS), National Patient Register (abortions)
Key variables
  • Outcome: teenage motherhood (child born while aged 15–18); youth crime convictions for boys
  • Treatment: municipal group (gang) crime conviction rate of males 15–25 in assignment year
  • Identification: quasi-random assignment of refugees to municipalities under the 1986–1998 dispersal policy
  • Long-run outcomes: earnings, employment, education
Period
Refugee assignment 1986–1998; population registers 1980–2019
Sample
8,172 assigned refugee children; 3,792 girls in teenage motherhood estimation sample Refugee children from eight refugee-sending countries assigned to Danish municipalities at age 0–14 between 1986 and 1998, observed every year until age 19. source

Intergenerational Spillover Effects of Language Training for Refugees

Mette Foged, Linea Hasager, Giovanni Peri, Jacob Nielsen Arendt and Iben Bolvig · Journal of Public Economics, 2023 · Individual

Follows the children of refugees affected by the 1999 Danish reform that improved language training. Children of treated parents achieve better educational outcomes, showing that the integration gains from language training carry over to the next generation.

Data
Danish administrative registers from Statistics Denmark on refugees' asylum/admission dates and parent–child links, Lower secondary school completion and final exam records, Criminal conviction records (property, violent, narcotics crime), Earnings, occupation (ISCO), disposable income, marriage and fertility registers for parents
Key variables
  • Running variable: parent's admission (asylum grant) date relative to 1 January 1999 (Integration Act language training reform)
  • Outcome: completing lower secondary school and taking final exams by age 18
  • Outcome: criminal convictions between ages 15 and 18
  • Parental outcomes: annual earnings, complex job, disposable income, convictions
Period
Children born 1990–2001, followed up to 2019; parental outcomes 1999–2016
Sample
6,467 children; 5,197 adult parents Children born 1990–2001 with at least one parent granted asylum in Denmark within a four-year window around 1 January 1999, who arrived before or during primary school age. source

The Impact of Immigration on Firm-Level Offshoring

William W. Olney and Dario Pozzoli · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2021 · Individual + firm

Uses the Danish refugee dispersal policy as a natural experiment and matched data on all firms and workers from 1995 to 2011. An exogenous inflow of immigrants into a municipality reduces firm-level offshoring on both margins, while immigrants' ties to their origin countries raise offshoring to those particular countries.

Data
Firm Statistics Register (FirmStat), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (FIDA), Foreign Trade Statistics Register: Intrastat and Extrastat firm-level imports (UHDI), Central Business Register (foreign ownership)
Key variables
  • Outcome: extensive margin of offshoring (firm imports in same HS4 as own production)
  • Outcome: log volume of narrow offshoring
  • Treatment: non-EU immigrant share of municipality workforce
  • Instrument: refugee placement under the 1986–1998 Spatial Dispersal Policy (shift-share)
Period
1995–2011
Sample
approximately 35,000 firms and 1 million workers Unbalanced panel of Danish private-sector firms with more than one employee in 97 municipalities, 1995–2011. source

The Welfare Magnet Hypothesis: Evidence from an Immigrant Welfare Scheme in Denmark

Ole Agersnap, Amalie Sofie Jensen and Henrik Kleven · AER: Insights, 2020 · Individual

The 2002 cut in benefits for non-EU immigrants, its repeal and its reintroduction provide repeated quasi-experiments on the welfare magnet hypothesis. The cut reduced net immigration by about 5,000 people a year and the repeal reversed it almost exactly, implying a migration elasticity with respect to benefits of 1.3.

Data
Danish administrative registers on the full population 1980–2017 (immigrant status, country of origin, demographics, family links), Residence permit data by permit type (asylum, family, work/study; available from 1997), Central Person Register (CPR) migration records, National statistics bureaus of other Nordic countries and OECD International Migration Database (1991–2017)
Key variables
  • Treatment: maximum monthly welfare benefit for non-EU immigrants (2002 introduction, 2012 repeal, 2015 reintroduction of the immigrant welfare scheme)
  • Outcome: net immigration flow by welfare benefit group, scaled by pre-scheme stock
  • Outcome: gross inflow by residence permit type
  • Comparison: EU vs non-EU immigrants; Denmark vs other Nordic countries
Period
1980–2017
Sample
444 group-year observations (12 welfare benefit groups) Immigrant flows to Denmark aged at least 30 (baseline), collapsed into 12 benefit-eligibility groups by EU/non-EU origin, marital status and number of children. source

Refugee Migration and Electoral Outcomes

Christian Dustmann, Kristine Vasiljeva and Anna Piil Damm · Review of Economic Studies, 2019 · Individual

Using the quasi-random refugee dispersal policy, refugee allocation raised vote shares for anti-immigration parties in rural municipalities and lowered them in the largest urban municipalities. Effects are strongest where refugees are few relative to natives and where crime rises after allocation.

Data
Population register (BEF) to identify refugees and municipality of initial placement, Income, education, labour force and socio-economic registers for 1985 municipality characteristics, Statistics Denmark municipal and parliamentary election results, Danish National Election Survey (DNES) 1990–98
Key variables
  • Outcome: change in vote share of anti-immigration parties (Progress Party, Danish People's Party)
  • Treatment: cumulative share of allocated refugees in municipal population (1986 base)
  • Source of variation: Danish Spatial Dispersal Policy allocation of refugees 1986–98
  • Heterogeneity: urban vs rural municipality
  • Controls: 1985 municipality characteristics
Period
Refugee allocation 1986–1998; parliamentary elections 1990–98 and municipal elections 1989–97
Sample
275 municipalities (550 observations for parliamentary elections); 76,673 refugees allocated 1986–98 All 275 Danish municipalities observed across election cycles during the 1986–98 refugee dispersal policy. source

Immigrants' Effect on Native Workers: New Analysis on Longitudinal Data

Mette Foged and Giovanni Peri · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2016 · Individual

Refugee-country immigration into Danish municipalities from 1991 to 2008 pushed less-educated native workers toward more complex, less manual occupations. Native wages and employment rose rather than fell, and effects were positive even for low-skilled natives who moved across firms.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Occupation codes linked to O*NET task intensities (manual, communication, cognitive), Full population registers for municipality immigrant shares, Refugee spatial dispersal policy 1986–1998 (initial refugee allocation by municipality)
Key variables
  • Treatment: share of refugee-country immigrants in the municipality of work
  • Instrument: immigrant inflow predicted from the 1986–1998 refugee dispersal policy
  • Outcome: occupational complexity index (O*NET-based)
  • Outcome: log hourly wage (incl. mandatory pension contributions)
  • Outcome: fraction of year worked; occupational mobility
  • Controls: age, experience, tenure, education, marital status, region, industry
Period
1991–2008
Sample
1,864,027 observations of employed low-skilled natives, 1995–2008, in the fixed-effects panel (20 percent random sample of natives); balanced-panel difference-in-differences regressions use 71,053 natives employed in 1994 Danish-born individuals aged 18–65, not in school or permanently disabled, focusing on low-skilled (non-college) natives, across 97 municipalities. source

Does Growing Up in a High Crime Neighborhood Affect Youth Criminal Behavior?

Anna Piil Damm and Christian Dustmann · American Economic Review, 2014 · Individual

Refugees arriving between 1986 and 1998 were allocated to municipalities quasi-randomly. Boys placed in neighbourhoods with a higher share of youth criminals were later more likely to be convicted, especially for violent crime, which identifies a causal neighbourhood effect on criminal behaviour.

Data
Central Police Register: crime charges and convictions for the full population, Administrative registers: age, residence, parents' IDs, country of origin, immigrant status and date of immigration, Educational Institution Register and surveys, Municipality-level crime and socio-economic measures built from the registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: youth crime conviction rate (share of 15–25-year-olds convicted) in the municipality of assignment
  • Identification: quasi-random assignment of refugee families under the 1986–1998 spatial dispersal policy
  • Outcome: criminal conviction and charge between ages 15 and 21 (by crime type)
  • Longer-term outcomes: education and employment
  • Controls: family background, country of origin, municipality characteristics at assignment
Period
1980–2006
Sample
4,425 children Refugee children from eight source countries who arrived in Denmark before age 15 with at least one refugee parent between 1986 and 1998 and are observed to age 21. source

Migration and Wage Effects of Taxing Top Earners: Evidence from the Foreigners' Tax Scheme in Denmark

Henrik Jacobsen Kleven, Camille Landais, Emmanuel Saez and Esben Schultz · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2014 · Individual

The 1991 preferential tax scheme for high-earning foreigners roughly doubled the number of highly paid foreigners in Denmark. The implied elasticity of migration with respect to the net-of-tax rate is above one, far larger than domestic labour supply elasticities, and the scheme generated modest wage spillovers.

Data
Administrative tax and payroll records for all residents (income tax register), Civil registration (CPR) citizenship and migration records with dates of entry and exit, Foreigners' tax scheme beneficiary records (contract start and end dates), Employer links at firm and establishment level with industry
Key variables
  • Treatment: eligibility for the preferential foreigners' tax scheme (earnings above threshold)
  • Outcome: number of foreigners above vs below the earnings threshold (migration response)
  • Outcome: earnings of scheme beneficiaries (wage effects / bunching at the threshold)
  • Outcome: spillovers on native top earners' employment and earnings
  • Key variable: citizenship and years of presence in Denmark
Period
1980–2010 (tax data since 1980; scheme introduced 1992)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked All foreigners and natives resident in Denmark, focusing on foreigners with earnings around the scheme eligibility threshold (researchers excluded). source

Ethnic Enclaves and Immigrant Labor Market Outcomes: Quasi-Experimental Evidence

Anna Piil Damm · Journal of Labor Economics, 2009 · Individual

Uses the quasi-random dispersal of refugees across Danish municipalities between 1986 and 1998 to separate the effect of living in an ethnic enclave from self-selection. Larger enclaves raise refugee men's annual earnings without changing their employment rates, and the gains are larger where more co-nationals are employed, pointing to job information networks.

Data
Longitudinal administrative registers on the immigrant population (Statistics Denmark), 1984–2000, Population register (BEF) incl. country of origin, year of immigration and municipality, Survey-based register on immigrants' pre-immigration education and integrated pupil register, Municipality statistics: unemployment register, election statistics, register-based labour force statistics, housing statistics, Danish Refugee Council administrative statistics on temporary housing (to identify assignment municipality)
Key variables
  • Outcome: employment probability
  • Outcome: real annual labour earnings
  • Key regressor: ethnic enclave (co-national group) size in municipality
  • Instrument / identification: initial assignment municipality under the 1986–1998 spatial dispersal policy
  • Controls: gender, age, marital status, children, education, country of origin, year of immigration
Period
Registers 1984–2000; refugee immigration cohorts 1986–1993
Sample
13,927 individuals (balanced panel observed annually for seven years after immigration) Adult refugees from 11 main refugee-sending countries assigned to Danish municipalities under the dispersal policy, observed annually from receipt of residence permit to seven years after. source

Crime

Charges and convictions as outcomes of neighbourhoods, school timing and welfare policy.

Breaking Bad: How Health Shocks Prompt Crime

Steffen Andersen, Elin Colmsjö, Gianpaolo Parise and Kim Peijnenburg · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2026 · Individual

Uses plausibly exogenous timing of cancer diagnoses in Danish registers to show that a health shock raises the probability of committing a crime, both first offences and re-offences, persistently. Patients replace lost legal income with illegal income and face a lower expected cost of punishment because of reduced survival, and welfare programmes that cushion the income loss dampen the effect.

Data
Danish administrative registers on cancer diagnoses (timing of diagnosis), Crime registers (first crimes and re-offenses), Income and welfare program registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: timing of a cancer diagnosis (health shock)
  • Outcome: probability of committing a crime (first crimes and re-offenses)
  • Mechanism: loss of legal income
  • Mechanism: survival probability (expected cost of punishment)
  • Heterogeneity: welfare program coverage
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish individuals diagnosed with cancer, using variation in the timing of diagnosis. source (abstract)

Refugee Benefit Cuts

Christian Dustmann, Rasmus Landersø and Lars Højsgaard Andersen · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

A 2002 reform cut welfare benefits for newly arrived refugees by roughly half. Employment rose modestly in the short run and then faded, while disposable income fell sharply. The cut raised crime among adults and harmed children's school enrolment and outcomes.

Data
Danish Immigration Service residence permit records, Income register from tax authority records (earnings, transfers, taxes), Hourly wage and occupation data, Labour market register (employment status), Population register (BEF), Crime registers: police and court records of charges and convictions, Job openings data from Jobindex.dk
Key variables
  • Treatment: residency granted after the July 2002 Start Aid benefit cut
  • Running variable: date residency was granted relative to the reform
  • Outcome: employment, labour earnings, transfer and disposable income
  • Outcome: crime convictions (property crime)
  • Heterogeneity: local labour demand in assignment municipality (low-skill job openings per unemployed)
Period
Residency granted 2001–2003; outcomes up to 10 years after residency
Sample
4,843 individuals Refugees and family-reunified refugees granted residency in Denmark between January 2001 and December 2003 aged 18–55, excluding Afghans, former Yugoslavs and re-migrants. source

Unintended Consequences of Welfare Cuts on Children and Adolescents

Christian Dustmann, Rasmus Landersø and Lars Højsgaard Andersen · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2024 · Individual

The 2002 benefit cut for newly arrived refugees reduced family disposable income by 30 percent over five years. Children exposed at preschool or school age obtained lower grades, less education and lower adult earnings, and teenagers at exposure saw large increases in convictions for violent and property crime.

Data
Danish Immigration Service residence permit records, Historical Migrations database, Income register from tax authority records, Register on Completed Education, Daycare register, 9th-grade exam grades, 6th-grade national language test scores and school well-being survey (Ministry of Education), Crime registers: police and court records
Key variables
  • Treatment: parents granted residency after the 2002 Start Aid welfare cut
  • Running variable: residency date relative to the reform
  • Outcome: 9th-grade GPA, 6th-grade language test scores, completed education
  • Outcome: adult earnings and employment
  • Outcome: criminal convictions
Period
Residency granted January 2001–December 2003; income data through 2019
Sample
6,888 individuals (3,406 adults aged 18–45 with children and 3,482 children aged 0–18) Refugee families with children granted residency in Denmark within 18 months of the 2002 reform, excluding Afghans, former Yugoslavs and emigrants. source

Neighbourhood Gangs, Crime Spillovers and Teenage Motherhood

Christian Dustmann, Mikkel Mertz and Anna Okatenko · Economic Journal, 2023 · Individual

Uses the random assignment of refugees to municipalities between 1986 and 1998. Gang crime in the assignment neighbourhood raises boys' probability of offending before 19 and girls' probability of teenage motherhood, with lasting consequences: more inactivity for men and lower earnings and more benefit claims for women at ages 19 to 28.

Data
Immigration register, Annual population registers (BEF), Central Police Register (criminal charges and convictions), Education registers (including survey-based register of immigrants' education), Income registers and register-based labour force statistics (RAS), National Patient Register (abortions)
Key variables
  • Outcome: teenage motherhood (child born while aged 15–18); youth crime convictions for boys
  • Treatment: municipal group (gang) crime conviction rate of males 15–25 in assignment year
  • Identification: quasi-random assignment of refugees to municipalities under the 1986–1998 dispersal policy
  • Long-run outcomes: earnings, employment, education
Period
Refugee assignment 1986–1998; population registers 1980–2019
Sample
8,172 assigned refugee children; 3,792 girls in teenage motherhood estimation sample Refugee children from eight refugee-sending countries assigned to Danish municipalities at age 0–14 between 1986 and 1998, observed every year until age 19. source

The Effects of DNA Databases on the Deterrence and Detection of Offenders

Anne Sofie Tegner Anker, Jennifer L. Doleac and Rasmus Landersø · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2021 · Individual

A large expansion of Denmark's DNA database cut recidivism within a year by up to 42 percent and raised the chance that re-offenders are caught, implying that a 1 percent higher detection probability lowers crime by more than 2 percent. Registered offenders also became more likely to work, study and live in stable families.

Data
Central Charge Register (crime date, charge date, crime type), Danish Central DNA Database person-specific section (DNA registration records), Conviction records (crime registers), Full-population registers on demographics, labour market and family links
Key variables
  • Treatment: offender registered in the DNA database
  • Instrument: charged after the May 2005 DNA database expansion (donut RD, excluding June–September 2005)
  • Running variable: months before May / after October 2005
  • Outcome: convictions within one, two and three years after the charge (all crime and by type)
  • Detection vs. deterrence: split by charges filed more vs. less than three weeks after the crime
  • Secondary outcomes: employment, education, marriage
Period
Charges June 2003–September 2007 (excluding June–September 2005), with follow-up outcomes
Sample
38,674 individuals with 66,911 charge observations Danish-resident males aged 18–30 charged with Penal Code or Weapons Act offenses who had at most 10 previous charges. source

Child's Gender, Young Fathers' Crime, and Spillover Effects in Criminal Behavior

Christian Dustmann and Rasmus Landersø · Journal of Political Economy, 2021 · Individual

Uses the gender of a first-born child as a natural experiment for young fathers in Denmark. Having a son rather than a daughter lowers the father's subsequent criminal behaviour, and the reduction spills over to the father's peers and later to the child, which identifies both a direct parenting response and network effects in crime.

Data
Birth register (child's gender, parent links), Population register (BEF) for demographics and family links, Central Police Register crime data (charges and convictions), Victim register (from 2001), Income, education and occupation registers, Residential register with neighbourhood classification
Key variables
  • Outcome: young father's crime convictions and charges
  • Treatment: first child is a son vs a daughter
  • Spillover outcome: crime of male peers in the father's neighbourhood
  • Peer group: males within ±3 years of father's age in the same neighbourhood
  • Outcome: neighbourhood crime victimisation
Period
Children born 1991–2004; crime data from 1990
Sample
2,803 first-time fathers aged 20 or younger; 101,132 male peers in 2,114 neighbourhoods Danish men who fathered their first child at age 20 or younger between 1991 and 2004, plus male peers in their neighbourhoods. source

Birth Order and Delinquency: Evidence from Denmark and Florida

Sanni Breining, Joseph Doyle, David N. Figlio, Krzysztof Karbownik and Jeffrey Roth · Journal of Labor Economics, 2020 · Individual

In both Denmark and Florida, second-born boys are substantially more likely than their first-born brothers to be disciplined in school and to enter the criminal justice system. The evidence points to reduced parental time investment for later-born children, rather than differences in health at birth or neighbourhood, as the mechanism.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR), Population register with parent and sibling links (BEF), Central Crime Register (convictions, sentences, incarceration), School registers (9th grade exit exam grades), Register of Daycare Institutions, National Patient Register (LPR) and Psychiatric Central Register, Parental education, employment, earnings and maternity leave registers, Florida birth records linked to school and juvenile justice records
Key variables
  • Outcome: criminal conviction by age 16 and 21; sentenced to prison; incarcerated
  • Treatment: being second-born vs first-born boy (sibling fixed effects)
  • Outcome: 9th grade exit exam grades in reading and math
  • Mechanisms: maternal leave and employment, daycare enrolment ages 1–3, school starting age, psychiatric diagnoses by age 10
  • Controls: parental age, education, employment, family income, immigrant status
Period
Danish birth cohorts 1981–1990; Florida births 1994–2002
Sample
Denmark: 121,100 children in 60,550 families (first- and second-born boys); 448,979 singleton births 1981–1990 in the full Danish population Danish families with two or more children with the same father, comparing first-born and second-born boys born 1981–1990 (plus Florida comparison samples). source

School Starting Age and the Crime-Age Profile

Rasmus Landersø, Helena Skyt Nielsen and Marianne Simonsen · Economic Journal, 2017 · Individual

Children born just after the school start cut-off begin school almost a year later. Starting school older lowers crime at young ages, especially for boys, by shifting the crime-age profile rather than permanently reducing crime, consistent with an incapacitation and maturity channel.

Data
Population register with exact birth dates and parent links (BEF), Criminal charge register (charges for property, violence and other crimes, with dates), Birth records: birth weight, gestational length, APGAR score (likely Medical Birth Register, MFR; not named in paper), Education registers (age in 8th grade as proxy for school starting age), Parental education and labour market registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: criminal charge at a given age / at or before a given age (from age 15)
  • Treatment: school starting age (7.6 vs 6.6)
  • Instrument/running variable: birth date relative to January 1 cut-off (±30 days)
  • Heterogeneity: gender; crime type (property, violence, other)
Period
Children born mid-1981 to mid-1993
Sample
48,546 and 50,383 children (the two gender groups) at age 15, born in December or January (Table 1) Danish children born in December or January between December 1981 and January 1993 (main bandwidth ±30 days around January 1). source

Does Growing Up in a High Crime Neighborhood Affect Youth Criminal Behavior?

Anna Piil Damm and Christian Dustmann · American Economic Review, 2014 · Individual

Refugees arriving between 1986 and 1998 were allocated to municipalities quasi-randomly. Boys placed in neighbourhoods with a higher share of youth criminals were later more likely to be convicted, especially for violent crime, which identifies a causal neighbourhood effect on criminal behaviour.

Data
Central Police Register: crime charges and convictions for the full population, Administrative registers: age, residence, parents' IDs, country of origin, immigrant status and date of immigration, Educational Institution Register and surveys, Municipality-level crime and socio-economic measures built from the registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: youth crime conviction rate (share of 15–25-year-olds convicted) in the municipality of assignment
  • Identification: quasi-random assignment of refugee families under the 1986–1998 spatial dispersal policy
  • Outcome: criminal conviction and charge between ages 15 and 21 (by crime type)
  • Longer-term outcomes: education and employment
  • Controls: family background, country of origin, municipality characteristics at assignment
Period
1980–2006
Sample
4,425 children Refugee children from eight source countries who arrived in Denmark before age 15 with at least one refugee parent between 1986 and 1998 and are observed to age 21. source

Children, intergenerational mobility and skills

What is transmitted across generations: wealth, earnings, financial trouble, health and skills.

Universal Investments in Toddler Health: Learning from a Large Government Trial

Jennifer L. Baker, Lise G. Bjerregaard, Christian M. Dahl, Torben S. D. Johansen, Emil N. Sørensen and Miriam Wüst · Economic Journal, 2026 · Individual

A 1960s Copenhagen government trial of preventive health care for toddlers, documented from handwritten nurse records and linked to the registers, improved a good adult health index by five percent of a standard deviation. Gains are largest for initially disadvantaged children and girls and fade for later cohorts as the counterfactual policy environment improved.

Data
Transcribed handwritten Copenhagen nurse records (Copenhagen City Archives), 1959–1967 cohorts, Central Person Register (CPR) linkage, Education registers (UDDA), Income registers (IND), Death register, National Patient Register (LPR), Copenhagen Perinatal Cohort (CPC), births 1959–1961, Family links to parents, siblings, children and spouses
Key variables
  • Treatment: born on the first three days of the month (extended toddler home-visiting trial)
  • First stage: nurse record contains a treatment table (take-up)
  • Outcome: adult SES index (years of education, post-secondary education, earnings and employment ages 30–50)
  • Outcome: adult good-health index (hospital contacts and diagnoses), mortality
  • Second-generation outcomes of trial children's own children
Period
Births 1959–1967 (trial covered births January 1959–April 1968); register outcomes 1977–2020
Sample
87,336 individuals Children born in Copenhagen 1959–1967 with transcribed nurse records linked to Statistics Denmark administrative data, excluding twins. source

Germs in the Family: The Short- and Long-Term Consequences of Intrahousehold Disease Spread

N. Meltem Daysal, Hui Ding, Maya Rossin-Slater and Hannes Schwandt · American Economic Review, 2026 · Individual

Shows that infant younger siblings are hospitalised for respiratory conditions two to three times as often as their older siblings were, because preschool-aged children bring disease home. Combining birth order with local variation in respiratory disease prevalence, early-life exposure lowers younger siblings' later earnings and education and worsens chronic respiratory and mental health.

Data
National Patient Register (LPR), Medical Birth Register (MFR), Population register (BEF), Register-Based Labour Force Statistics (RAS), Income Statistics Register (IND), Education Register (UDDA), Academic Achievement Register (9th grade test scores), Psychiatric Central Research Register (PCRR), Health Insurance Register (Sygesikringsregisteret), Childcare enrollment data, National Child Database (breastfeeding)
Key variables
  • Outcome: acute respiratory hospitalizations in the first year of life
  • Treatment: birth order (second-born vs first-born) interacted with municipality respiratory disease exposure index
  • Outcome: earnings, labour force participation and income rank at ages 18–32
  • Outcome: high school and college completion; 9th grade test scores
  • Outcome: chronic respiratory and mental healthcare use
Period
Children born 1981–2017; outcomes 1980–2019
Sample
1,230,180 children First- and second-born singleton siblings born in Denmark 1981–2017, at least 11 months apart, in 91 municipalities. source

Background Matters, but Not Whether Parents Are Immigrants: Outcomes of Children Born in Denmark

Mathias Fjællegaard Jensen and Alan Manning · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2025 · Individual

Children born in Denmark to immigrant parents have lower earnings, more unemployment, less education, more transfers and more convictions than children of Danish-born parents. Conditional on parental socioeconomic characteristics, however, they do as well or slightly better, so the gaps reflect deprived backgrounds rather than immigrant parentage.

Data
