Danish register data linking children to parents (Statistics Denmark, 1980–2017), Income and earnings registers (annual labour income incl. self-employment, transfers), Monthly employment register (hours, hourly wages, from 2008), Education registers (incl. survey-based registration of foreign education of immigrants), Criminal justice registers (convictions, prison sentences, dropped charges / not-guilty verdicts), Occupation codes (ISCO88)
Key variables
  • Group: number of parents born outside Denmark (0, 1, 2; Greenland/Faroe Islands)
  • Outcome: earnings and unemployment at age 30
  • Outcome: public transfers, completed education by age 30
  • Outcome: criminal convictions, prison sentences, dropped charges
  • Controls: parental income, unemployment years, occupation and education during childhood
Period
1980–2017
Sample
396,417 children (Table 1 total); 362,460 with two Danish-born parents, 10,399 with two immigrant parents, 20,908 with one immigrant parent, 2,650 with a parent from Greenland/Faroe Islands All children born in Denmark 1980–1987 with both parents known, followed to age 30. source

Genetic and Socioeconomic Achievement Gaps in Elementary School

Mikkel Aagaard Houmark, Victor Ronda, Esben Agerbo, Preben Bo Mortensen and Michael Rosholm · Economic Journal, 2025 · Individual

Shows that the achievement gap by polygenic score for educational attainment is as large as the gap by socioeconomic status and widens during elementary school, but only among low-SES children. Selection into neighbourhoods and schools explains part of the SES gap but none of the genetic gap, which instead relates to conscientiousness and the learning environment.

Data
iPSYCH2015 case-cohort genotype sample (neonatal blood spots from the Danish National Biobank), Danish national tests (reading and mathematics, grades 2–8), Population registers with parent-child links (BEF), Parental income registers (IND), Parental education registers (UDDA), Psychiatric diagnoses of parents (patient registers), School and neighbourhood identifiers
Key variables
  • Outcome: standardised national reading test scores (grades 2, 4, 6, 8); mathematics tests (grades 3, 6, 8)
  • Key regressor: polygenic score for educational attainment (EA PGS)
  • Key regressor: SES index from parental income and education
  • Sibling fixed effects: within-family variation in the PGS
  • Mediators: neighbourhood and school sorting
Period
Births 1994–2008; national tests from 2010
Sample
40,136 individuals with 87,917 reading test outcomes (24,062 psychiatric cases, 16,074 random controls); sibling sample 2,705 individuals Genotyped iPSYCH children of European descent born 1994–2008 who completed at least one national test between 2nd and 8th grade. source

The Impact of the Level and Timing of Parental Resources on Child Development and Intergenerational Mobility

Sadegh Eshaghnia, James J. Heckman and Rasmus Landersø · Journal of Labor Economics, 2025 · Individual

Uses Danish registers covering parents' full income histories to separate the level of parents' lifetime resources from their timing during childhood. Lifetime resources matter far more than when income arrives, and conventional intergenerational elasticities based on short income windows give a misleading picture of mobility.

Data
Population register with family links (BEF), Education register (UDDA), Income, assets and transfers register (IND), Sentencing register (KRAF), Incarceration register (KRIN), Danish Household Expenditure Survey, Danish Longitudinal Survey of Children (DALSC), 9th-grade national leaving exam scores
Key variables
  • Outcome: child income at ages 30–35 (IGE)
  • Outcome: years of education by age 35
  • Outcome: any criminal conviction by age 35
  • Outcome: math test score at age 16 and language test at age 11
  • Explanatory: level and timing of parental income/resources by child age (lifetime vs snapshot)
Period
1980–2019
Sample
around 100,000 children and their families (105,953 individuals; 98,686 in the IGE log-log sample) Children born in 1981 and 1982 linked to their parents, non-migrants with non-migrant parents; plus the 1995–1997 birth cohorts for exam scores and DALSC children born 1995. source

Attendance Boundary Policies and the Limits to Combating School Segregation

Andreas Bjerre-Nielsen and Mikkel Høst Gandil · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

Uses address-level data on the universe of Danish children and changes in school attendance boundaries over time. Households defy reassignment to schools with lower socioeconomic status, resourceful households most of all, so redrawing boundaries toward highly disadvantaged schools barely changes those schools' composition.

Data
Register data on the universe of Danish children (Statistics Denmark), School attendance boundary data with boundary changes over time
Key variables
  • Treatment: reassignment of a residential area to a different school by an attendance boundary change
  • Outcome: enrolment in (or defiance of) the assigned school
  • Heterogeneity: household socioeconomic resources
  • Socioeconomic composition of the new school's students
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish children (universe) whose residential areas were affected by changes in school attendance boundaries. source (abstract)

Public Health Policy at Scale: Impact of a Government-Sponsored Information Campaign on Infant Mortality in Denmark

Onur Altındağ, Jane Greve and Erdal Tekin · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

A nationwide campaign against sudden infant death syndrome, delivered mainly through universal nurse home visits, cut infant mortality by 17 percent in a regression discontinuity on population birth data. Effects were 11 to 14 times larger for low-birthweight and preterm infants and concentrated among low-SES families.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR): birth weight, gestational age, parity, maternal age, immigrant status, Register of Causes of Death (exact date and cause of death), Education register (parental education)
Key variables
  • Running variable: birth month relative to December 1991
  • Treatment: birth after the 1991 change in infant sleep-position guidelines
  • Outcome: SIDS mortality per 10,000 births
  • Outcome: infant and child mortality per 10,000 births
  • Heterogeneity: low birth weight, preterm, maternal education
Period
Live births 1973–2006
Sample
568,106 births (SIDS outcome, MSE-optimal bandwidth of 52 months); 632,449 births for infant mortality (58 months) All live births in Denmark within the RD bandwidth around the December 1991 guideline change. source

On the Family Origins of Human Capital Formation: Evidence from Donor Children

Petter Lundborg, Erik Plug and Astrid Würtz Rasmussen · Review of Economic Studies, 2024 · Individual

Uses Danish children conceived through sperm and egg donation to separate nurture from genetic transmission, since donors are not selected on parental traits. Only mothers' education matters once genes are removed: fathers' schooling has no effect on children's test scores, while mothers' schooling raises reading scores as much as among non-donor children.

Data
Danish IVF register (Danish Health Data Authority), Danish Education Register (UDDA), National test scores in reading and math (from 2010), Administrative registers on demographics and labour market (birth year, gender, marital status, children, earnings)
Key variables
  • Outcome: child average standardized national test score (reading and math)
  • Explanatory: mother's and father's years of schooling
  • Variation: donor sperm / donor egg conception (genetically unrelated parent)
  • Controls: month of first donor treatment and clinic fixed effects (waiting-list assignment)
Period
IVF treatments 1994–2007; demographics 1991–2016; labour market 1991–2012; tests from 2010
Sample
19,509 non-donor IVF children, 820 sperm donor children and 157 egg donor children (97 matched to egg donors) Children conceived through IVF treatments in Denmark 1994–2007 with at least one national test score, compared with 30% random samples of non-adopted (650,930) and adopted (2,674) children. source

Intergenerational Spillover Effects of Language Training for Refugees

Mette Foged, Linea Hasager, Giovanni Peri, Jacob Nielsen Arendt and Iben Bolvig · Journal of Public Economics, 2023 · Individual

Follows the children of refugees affected by the 1999 Danish reform that improved language training. Children of treated parents achieve better educational outcomes, showing that the integration gains from language training carry over to the next generation.

Data
Danish administrative registers from Statistics Denmark on refugees' asylum/admission dates and parent–child links, Lower secondary school completion and final exam records, Criminal conviction records (property, violent, narcotics crime), Earnings, occupation (ISCO), disposable income, marriage and fertility registers for parents

Spillover Effects of Early-Life Medical Interventions

N. Meltem Daysal, Marianne Simonsen, Mircea Trandafir and Sanni Breining · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2022 · Individual

Extra medical treatment for very-low-birth-weight newborns, identified from a birth weight threshold, improves not only the treated infant but also the outcomes of older siblings, who do better in school. Family resources and parental time respond to the child's health.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR): birth weight, gestational age, parity, Parent and sibling links (BEF), 9th-grade exam test score register (reading and math), Education/enrollment registers, Prescription drug register (antidepressant use, from 1995), Health registers for diagnoses (intellectual disability, ADHD) and mortality, Income and labour market registers for parents
Key variables
  • Running variable: focal child's birth weight relative to 1,500-gram VLBW cutoff
  • Outcome: siblings' standardized 9th-grade reading and math test scores
  • Outcome: sibling enrollment beyond compulsory education by age 19 and high-school track
  • Mechanisms: focal-child mortality and mental health; maternal antidepressant use; parental income, employment, days worked
Period
Focal children born 1982–1993; test scores 2001–2010
Sample
5,827 sibling observations (siblings of 3,677 focal children) Siblings (same mother) of Danish children born 1982–1993 with birth weight between 1,300 and 1,700 grams. source

Education, Birth Order and Family Size

Jesper Bagger, Javier A. Birchenall, Hani Mansour and Sergio Urzúa · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Develops a model of parental investment with birth order preferences and human capital cost differences that delivers a generalised quantity–quality trade-off. Danish administrative data confirm its predictions: at any given parity, children in smaller families have more education, and the average child's education falls with family size even after accounting for birth order.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), 1980–2006, with mother-child links, Precise date of birth from Statistics Denmark (to identify twin/multiple births)
Key variables
  • Outcome: years of education (measured in 2006)
  • Key regressors: birth order and family size
  • Instrument: twin birth at last birth (twins at 2nd, 3rd or 4th birth)
  • Controls: parental education, gender, age
Period
1980–2006 (outcomes measured in 2006)
Sample
1,256,031 children in 581,159 families (excluding single-child families) Individuals aged 25–41 in 2006 whose mother was alive and present in IDA during 1980–2006, in families with completed fertility, siblings defined by common mother. source

Home Prices, Fertility, and Early-Life Health Outcomes

N. Meltem Daysal, Michael Lovenheim, Nikolaj Siersbæk and David N. Wasser · Journal of Public Economics, 2021 · Individual

For Danish homeowners aged 20 to 44, a 100,000 kroner rise in home prices raises the probability of giving birth by about 2.35 percent. Birth outcomes improve too, but mostly because of who gives birth, and there is no effect on children's health in the first five years.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR), National Patient Register (LPR), State's Sales and Valuation Registry (public property valuations and sales prices), Population register (BEF) and other registers for demographics, education, household income and partner status
Key variables
  • Treatment: once-lagged one-year change in estimated home value (public valuation adjusted by local sale-price ratio)
  • Outcome: indicator for giving birth
  • Outcomes: birth weight, low birth weight, very low birth weight, prematurity
  • Outcomes: hospital admissions, ER visits and days hospitalised in first year and ages 1–5
  • Comparison group: renters
Period
1992–2011 (fertility analysis 1995–2006)
Sample
1,105,559 observations on 198,435 women (125,903 births) Women aged 20–44 who became first-time homeowners between 1993 and 2004, purchasing between ages 18 and 42. source

Family, Community and Long-Term Socio-Economic Inequality: Evidence from Siblings and Youth Peers

Paul Bingley, Lorenzo Cappellari and Konstantinos Tatsiramos · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Decomposes sibling correlations in permanent earnings, education and unemployment for the Danish population into family and community components, allowing for life-cycle dynamics. Family is the dominant factor; community background shapes earnings and unemployment correlations early in working life but fades quickly.

Data
Civil registration system / population register with parent links (BEF), Central person register address histories (parish of residence), Education register / national pupil database (school at age 15), Annual earnings from tax-based income registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: pre-tax annual labour earnings (residual log earnings), 1984–2011
  • Grouping: brothers (family), schoolmates at age 15, parish neighbours at age 15
  • Parameters: sibling, school-peer and neighbour correlations over the life cycle
Period
Men born 1960–1983; earnings 1984–2011
Sample
695,960 men; 10,930,859 earnings observations (122,652 brother pairs, 380,948 singletons) Danish men born 1960–1983 (first and second sons sharing both legal parents, plus singletons and same-age peers), linked to schoolmates and parish neighbours at age 15. source

Sibling Spillovers

Sandra E. Black, Sanni Breining, David N. Figlio, Jonathan Guryan, Krzysztof Karbownik, Helena Skyt Nielsen, Jeffrey Roth and Marianne Simonsen · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Identifies spillovers within the family from having a disabled younger sibling using administrative data from both Denmark and Florida. Comparing first- and second-born children in families with three or more children, the second child, who spends more early childhood with the younger sibling, is differentially affected when the third child is disabled.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR), National Patient Register (LPR) for disability diagnoses, 9th grade exit exam grades (school/education registers), Parental education, labour market, earnings and leave registers, Florida birth records linked to Florida public school records (FCAT)
Key variables
  • Outcome: cohort-standardised 9th grade exit exam GPA (Denmark); FCAT test scores (Florida)
  • Treatment: third-born sibling has a disability (diagnosed in medical registers) observed by age five
  • Comparison: second-born vs first-born child (difference-in-differences)
  • Controls: parental age, education, labour market status, earnings, immigrant status
Period
Danish births 1990–2001; Florida births 1994–2002
Sample
Denmark: 134,277 families with first two children observed, 28,581 families with first three children (4,430 with a disabled third child by age five); Florida: 80,879 and 9,987 families Families in Denmark (births 1990–2001) and Florida (births 1994–2002) with at least three children where the first two children have the same father. source

Financial Trouble Across Generations: Evidence from the Universe of Personal Loans in Denmark

Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Søren Leth-Petersen and Louise Charlotte Willerslev-Olsen · Economic Journal, 2020 · Individual

Loan-level records on all personal loans show strong intergenerational persistence in financial trouble: children of parents who default are far more likely to default themselves, and the correlation is present already in early adulthood and is not explained by income or education.

Data
Loan register from the Danish Tax Agency (SKAT): all personal loan accounts with default status (60+ days late), Population register (BEF) for parent-child links, Income-tax register and other administrative registers on labour market history, income and wealth, Credit bureau bad-payer files (Experian, Debitor Registret), 2009 snapshot, Survey of 1,748 individuals on risk willingness, patience and impulsivity (2014)
Key variables
  • Outcome: child default on personal loans (60+ days late)
  • Key regressor: parental default status
  • Preference measures from survey (risk willingness, patience, impulsivity)
  • Controls: income, wealth, education, GPA
Period
2004–2011
Sample
28,027,610 parent-child-year observations on 2,501,088 individuals All Danes aged 18–45 in 2004–2011 with at least one living parent, organized as parent-child-year cells. source

What Is the Added Value of Preschool for Poor Children? Long-Term and Intergenerational Impacts and Interactions with an Infant Health Intervention

Maya Rossin-Slater and Miriam Wüst · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2020 · Individual

Access to preschool targeted at low-income children between 1933 and 1960 raised adult education, earnings and survival past 65, and the children of exposed women obtained more education by 25. Exposure to the nurse home visiting programme in infancy reduces preschool's added value, so the two programmes are partial substitutes.

Data
Archival data on all approved Danish preschools 1933–1960 (establishment and approval year, slots), Nurse Home Visiting program implementation dates by municipality, Statistical Commune Data Archive (elections and censuses) for municipality controls, Municipal live births and infant deaths, DigDag historical administrative geography (parish-municipality mapping), Administrative population register data 1980–2012 (education, earnings, survival, hospital admissions, diagnoses), Mother-child links (Danish Civil Registration System)
Key variables
  • Treatment: approved preschool in municipality of birth at age 3
  • Interaction: exposure to the infant Nurse Home Visiting program
  • Outcome: education and earnings around age 50
  • Outcome: survival past age 65, hospital admissions, diagnoses
  • Intergenerational outcome: oldest child's educational attainment by age 25
  • Controls: municipality and birth-year fixed effects, county-by-year trends, municipality characteristics
Period
Cohorts born 1930–1957; outcomes 1980–2012; children born 1955–1987
Sample
1,640,214 observations (Danish-born with valid parish codes); 880,708 observations in municipalities with an approved preschool by 1960, collapsed to 3,862 municipality × birth-year cells Danish-born individuals born 1930–1957 with a valid parish of birth who are observed after 1980, focusing on the 138 municipalities that ever had an approved preschool. source

Birth Order and Delinquency: Evidence from Denmark and Florida

Sanni Breining, Joseph Doyle, David N. Figlio, Krzysztof Karbownik and Jeffrey Roth · Journal of Labor Economics, 2020 · Individual

In both Denmark and Florida, second-born boys are substantially more likely than their first-born brothers to be disciplined in school and to enter the criminal justice system. The evidence points to reduced parental time investment for later-born children, rather than differences in health at birth or neighbourhood, as the mechanism.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR), Population register with parent and sibling links (BEF), Central Crime Register (convictions, sentences, incarceration), School registers (9th grade exit exam grades), Register of Daycare Institutions, National Patient Register (LPR) and Psychiatric Central Register, Parental education, employment, earnings and maternity leave registers, Florida birth records linked to school and juvenile justice records
Key variables
  • Outcome: criminal conviction by age 16 and 21; sentenced to prison; incarcerated
  • Treatment: being second-born vs first-born boy (sibling fixed effects)
  • Outcome: 9th grade exit exam grades in reading and math
  • Mechanisms: maternal leave and employment, daycare enrolment ages 1–3, school starting age, psychiatric diagnoses by age 10
  • Controls: parental age, education, employment, family income, immigrant status
Period
Danish birth cohorts 1981–1990; Florida births 1994–2002
Sample
Denmark: 121,100 children in 60,550 families (first- and second-born boys); 448,979 singleton births 1981–1990 in the full Danish population Danish families with two or more children with the same father, comparing first-born and second-born boys born 1981–1990 (plus Florida comparison samples). source

Correlation of Brothers' Earnings and Intergenerational Transmission

Paul Bingley and Lorenzo Cappellari · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2019 · Individual

Decomposes the earnings correlation between Danish brothers into the part explained by fathers' earnings and the part due to other shared family and community factors. Intergenerational transmission accounts for a larger share of sibling similarity than simple father-son elasticities suggest, once permanent earnings and life-cycle bias are handled.

Data
Civil registration system / population register with parent-child links (BEF), Income Statistics Register (IND): annual pre-tax labour earnings from tax returns
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual pre-tax labour earnings (sum over all employments), ages 25–60
  • Family structure: father/first-son/second-son triads
  • Life-cycle: 3-year birth cohort groups and age
Period
1980–2014
Sample
303,231 men in 101,077 families; 5,488,445 earnings observations Danish fathers born 1935–1961 and their first and second sons (born 1959–1985) with the same mother, with at least five consecutive positive earnings observations. source

Spillovers in Education Choice

Juanna Schrøter Joensen and Helena Skyt Nielsen · Journal of Public Economics, 2018 · Individual

Exploits the Danish high school pilot that made advanced mathematics cheaper to take for some cohorts. An older sibling's advanced maths choice raises the probability that the younger sibling also chooses advanced maths, with spillovers that depend on the siblings' gender composition.

Data
Danish administrative registers on high school enrolment, course packages and completion (Statistics Denmark education registers), Parent and sibling links from population registers, Parental education and income registers, Earnings registers (younger siblings' earnings at ages 30–35)
Key variables
  • Outcome: younger sibling chooses advanced math-science course package in high school
  • Endogenous regressor: older sibling's advanced math-science choice
  • Instrument: older sibling enrolled in a high school that unexpectedly introduced the pilot scheme (PilotIntro)
  • Outcomes: younger sibling's college/STEM education at age 30 and log earnings at ages 30–35
  • Controls: parental education and income, county and entry-cohort fixed effects
Period
High school entry cohorts 1984–1987 (older siblings) and 1988–1991 (younger siblings, main sample); register data from 1980 onwards
Sample
7,786 sibling pairs (main closely spaced sample); 17,691 sibling pairs in the wider sample with age gap < 10 years Older siblings entering academic high school 1984–1987 who graduated in three years, paired with younger siblings entering high school 1988–1991 with an age gap of at most 4 years. source

Parental Responses to Child Support Obligations: Evidence from Administrative Data

Maya Rossin-Slater and Miriam Wüst · Journal of Public Economics, 2018 · Individual

Uses discontinuities in the Danish child support schedule by fathers' income to estimate how higher obligations affect separated parents. The paper documents responses in fathers' income, subsequent family formation and the parents' living arrangements.

Data
Danish administrative registers on children born 1985–2008 linked to parents and siblings (population/family registers), Income and tax records including tax-deductible child support payments, Labour market registers (employment, earnings, disability insurance, early retirement), Birth records (fertility through 2010), Education registers, Danish Longitudinal Survey of Children (DALSC) and Children and Youth in Denmark (CYD) survey (descriptive, supplementary)
Key variables
  • Treatment: father's average child support obligation (from Danish child support guideline schedule)
  • Identifying variation: income thresholds in the child support guidelines (simulated instrument)
  • Outcome: father's annual child support payment
  • Outcome: father–child co-residence (physical custody)
  • Outcomes: post-separation fertility and labour supply of both parents
Period
1998–2010 (separation years 1999–2008)
Sample
73,303 fathers (71,575 unique mothers) Fathers observed every year 1998–2010 who separated from their oldest child's mother (or had a first child while not cohabiting) in 1999–2008, with one or two children under 18 and separation-year income near the first three guideline thresholds. source

Born with a Silver Spoon? Danish Evidence on Wealth Inequality in Childhood

Simon Halphen Boserup, Wojciech Kopczuk and Claus Thustrup Kreiner · Economic Journal, 2018 · Individual

Third-party reported wealth records of children show that wealth inequality is as high in childhood as among adults, with top shares driven by transfers from parents and grandparents. Wealth at age 18 predicts wealth in adulthood, and intergenerational wealth correlations are present from early ages.

Data
Individual wealth data from tax return records (Danish Tax Agency SKAT, via Statistics Denmark), Population register with parent identifiers (BEF), Education and income registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: child wealth and wealth rank in adulthood (age 45)
  • Key regressor: childhood wealth (age 18) and parental wealth rank
  • Inferred inter vivos transfers and bequests (bunching at gift-tax allowance)
  • Portfolio components (deposits, securities, real estate, debt)
Period
1983–2011
Sample
Pooled cross-section: on average close to 650 thousand observations per one-year age group; panel regressions: 36,102 observations All individuals aged 1–40 in 2000–2011 with living parents (pooled cross-section), plus a panel of the 1965 birth cohort with both parents alive in 2011, followed 1983–2011. source

Universal Investment in Infants and Long-Run Health: Evidence from Denmark's 1937 Home Visiting Program

Jonas Hjort, Mikkel Sølvsten and Miriam Wüst · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2017 · Individual

Uses the staggered municipal roll-out of the 1937 universal infant home visiting programme and follows treated cohorts into middle age in the registers. Treated individuals have higher survival between ages 45 and 64, fewer hospital nights and less cardiovascular disease, consistent with early nutrition and disease environment programming adult health.

Data
Archival records of home visiting program implementation dates by municipality, 1937–1949 (Danish National Archives), Danish Commune Archive / Statistical Commune Data Archive (pre-1937 municipal characteristics from censuses and elections), Medical Reports for the Kingdom of Denmark (1936 infant mortality by medical district), Administrative population data with date and parish of birth (cohorts 1935–1949), Danish Inpatient Register / National Patient Register (LPR), 1980–2012, Danish Death Register (cause of death), 1980–2012, DigDag historical administrative geography
Key variables
  • Treatment: born in a municipality after it implemented the home visiting program
  • Variation: staggered municipal implementation timing 1937–1949
  • Outcome: age-specific survival rates, ages 46–64
  • Outcome: hospital nights between ages 45–64
  • Outcome: diagnoses of heart disease, cardiovascular disease, diabetes, cancer; cause of death
  • Controls: municipality and cohort fixed effects, pre-1937 municipal characteristics
Period
Birth cohorts 1935–1949; outcomes 1980–2012
Sample
876,912 individuals, collapsed to 20,078 municipality × birth cohort × treatment-status cells Danish-born individuals born 1935–1949 outside hospitals who survived and lived in Denmark to age 45, matched to birth municipality. source

School Starting Age and the Crime-Age Profile

Rasmus Landersø, Helena Skyt Nielsen and Marianne Simonsen · Economic Journal, 2017 · Individual

Children born just after the school start cut-off begin school almost a year later. Starting school older lowers crime at young ages, especially for boys, by shifting the crime-age profile rather than permanently reducing crime, consistent with an incapacitation and maturity channel.

Data
Population register with exact birth dates and parent links (BEF), Criminal charge register (charges for property, violence and other crimes, with dates), Birth records: birth weight, gestational length, APGAR score (likely Medical Birth Register, MFR; not named in paper), Education registers (age in 8th grade as proxy for school starting age), Parental education and labour market registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: criminal charge at a given age / at or before a given age (from age 15)
  • Treatment: school starting age (7.6 vs 6.6)
  • Instrument/running variable: birth date relative to January 1 cut-off (±30 days)
  • Heterogeneity: gender; crime type (property, violence, other)
Period
Children born mid-1981 to mid-1993
Sample
48,546 and 50,383 children (the two gender groups) at age 15, born in December or January (Table 1) Danish children born in December or January between December 1981 and January 1993 (main bandwidth ±30 days around January 1). source

Does Growing Up in a High Crime Neighborhood Affect Youth Criminal Behavior?

Anna Piil Damm and Christian Dustmann · American Economic Review, 2014 · Individual

Refugees arriving between 1986 and 1998 were allocated to municipalities quasi-randomly. Boys placed in neighbourhoods with a higher share of youth criminals were later more likely to be convicted, especially for violent crime, which identifies a causal neighbourhood effect on criminal behaviour.

Data
Central Police Register: crime charges and convictions for the full population, Administrative registers: age, residence, parents' IDs, country of origin, immigrant status and date of immigration, Educational Institution Register and surveys, Municipality-level crime and socio-economic measures built from the registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: youth crime conviction rate (share of 15–25-year-olds convicted) in the municipality of assignment
  • Identification: quasi-random assignment of refugee families under the 1986–1998 spatial dispersal policy
  • Outcome: criminal conviction and charge between ages 15 and 21 (by crime type)
  • Longer-term outcomes: education and employment
  • Controls: family background, country of origin, municipality characteristics at assignment
Period
1980–2006
Sample
4,425 children Refugee children from eight source countries who arrived in Denmark before age 15 with at least one refugee parent between 1986 and 1998 and are observed to age 21. source

Estimating Class-size Effects using Within-school Variation in Subject-specific Classes

Eskil Heinesen · Economic Journal, 2010 · Individual

Uses within-school variation over time in the size of French classes, which Danish seventh graders choose between French and German, as quasi-random variation in class size. Larger classes lower French exam marks, especially for weaker students and boys, and marks in subjects taught in the regular class serve as a check on selection.

Data
Administrative registers on 9th-grade marks and exam results, school attended, merged via personal and school ID numbers, Family background registers (parents' education, income, labour market status, family structure)
Key variables
  • Outcome: French examination marks at end of 9th grade
  • Treatment: French class size (school/year cell)
  • Identification: within-school year-to-year variation in subject-specific class size
  • Placebo/control: marks in other subjects taught in basic classes
Period
School years 2001/2002–2003/2004
Sample
6,507 students in 782 school/year cells and 375 schools 9th-grade students in 2002–2004 studying French at schools offering both German and French from 7th grade, with at most 20 French students per grade. source

Non-cognitive Child Outcomes and Universal High Quality Child Care

Nabanita Datta Gupta and Marianne Simonsen · Journal of Public Economics, 2010 · Individual

Links the Danish Longitudinal Survey of Children to the registers to compare non-cognitive outcomes at age seven for children in universal preschool, family day care and home care at age three. Attending high-quality preschool does not harm non-cognitive outcomes relative to home care, while long hours in family day care are associated with worse outcomes for some groups.

Data
Danish Longitudinal Survey of Children (DALSC), waves 1996, 1999, 2003, Administrative registers on parents' education, labour market status, hours, wages and income (1994–2003), Day Care Register (Statistics Denmark), used descriptively
Key variables
  • Outcome: Strengths and Difficulties Questionnaire (SDQ) index at age 7½
  • Treatment: mode of care at age 3½ (preschool, family day care, home care)
  • Instrument/pseudo-experiment: municipality differences in childcare take-up and provision
  • Heterogeneity: child gender and mother's education
  • Hours in non-parental care
Period
1994–2003 (children born 1995; care measured 1999, outcomes 2003)
Sample
4,343 observations (children) in the main OLS estimates About 6,000 Danish children born between 15 September and 31 October 1995 surveyed in DALSC, linked to parental register data. source

Education

Student aid, school starting age and schooling outcomes.

Reskilling and Resilience

Anders Humlum and Pernille Plato · AER: Insights (forthcoming), 2026 · Individual

Studies how retraining shapes workers' resilience to labour market shocks in Danish administrative data, extending the authors' work on human capital investment after injury to the question of which workers reskill and how reskilling protects earnings when jobs disappear.

Data
Work accident reports from Labour Market Insurance (AES), Education Register (UDDA), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), National Patient Registry (hospitalizations), Health Insurance Registry (primary care and specialist visits), Prescription Drug Database, Population register (BEF) for partner linkages
Key variables
  • Shock: work accident causing permanent loss of earning capacity
  • Treatment/moderator: access to reskilling (vocational degree granting direct entry to higher education)
  • Outcome: antidepressant use and other mental health measures
  • Outcome: partner labour market and health outcomes
  • Outcome: earnings and employment of injured workers
Period
1995–2017 (work accidents 1998–2017)
Sample
2,915 injury events (male craft workers); 2,103 stable partners Male craft workers with vocational degrees and full-time employment in the three years before a physical work accident causing permanent loss of earning capacity, and their partners. source

Instrumental Variables with Unordered Treatments: Theory and Evidence from Returns to Fields of Study

Eskil Heinesen, Christian Hvid, Lars Kirkebøen, Edwin Leuven and Magne Mogstad · Journal of Labor Economics, 2026 · Individual

Shows that the assumptions needed to identify returns to unordered treatments, such as fields of study, have testable implications, and characterises the bias when they fail. Using admission cut-offs and applicants' ranked choices in Denmark and Norway, payoffs to fields are strongly correlated across the two countries once fields violating the assumptions are dropped.

Data
Danish administrative data on all applications to post-secondary education 1994–2002 (coordinated admission system), Population register and other registers (background, completed field of study, annual earnings), Norwegian post-secondary application data 1998–2004 linked to registers (comparison country)
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual earnings eight years after application (wages, self-employment and income-replacing transfers)
  • Treatment: completed field of study (nine broad fields)
  • Instrument: application score just above vs below the admission cutoff of the preferred field
  • Preferred and next-best field from applicants' rankings
Period
Applications 1994–2002 (Denmark), outcomes eight years after application
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked First-time applicants to Danish post-secondary education without a prior post-secondary degree who applied to at least two broad fields, with the preferred field having an admission cutoff, and who completed a field within eight years (paralleled with Norwegian applicants). source

Educational Ambition, Marital Sorting, and Inequality

Frederik Almar, Benjamin Friedrich, Ana Reynoso, Bastian Schulz and Rune Vejlin · Journal of Labor Economics, 2026 · Individual

Classifies detailed Danish education programmes into ambition types by their graduates' starting wages and wage growth. Assortative matching by educational ambition has risen sharply even though sorting by conventional education levels looks flat, and the marriage market accounts for more than 40 percent of the rise in household income inequality since 1980.

Data
Population register with partner links (BEF), Education register with four-digit ISCED programme codes (UDDA), Employment register with hourly wages (IDAN), Income register (IND), Danish Labour Force Survey (LFS/AKU), 2000–2018, Employment register for hours, part-time/full-time (RAS)
Key variables
  • Classification: educational 'ambition types' from programme-level starting wages and wage growth (about 1,800 programmes)
  • Outcome: marital sorting by ambition type (assortative matching)
  • Outcome: household labour income inequality
  • Wage measure: residualised log hourly wages by programme
  • LFS job attributes: hours, evening/weekend/home work
Period
1980–2018
Sample
on average 3,031,511 individuals per year aged 19–60; 1,800,866 individuals in couples per year All individuals living in Denmark aged 19–60 in 1980–2018, with couples defined as married or cohabiting opposite-sex partners. source

Changing Tracks: Human Capital Investment after Loss of Ability

Anders Humlum, Jakob R. Munch and Pernille Plato · American Economic Review, 2025 · Individual

Uses quasi-random work accidents in Danish administrative data to show that injured workers enrol in bachelor's programmes that build on their previous training. Exploiting differences in how stackable degrees are, higher education moves injured workers from disability benefits into full-time jobs paying 25 percent more than before injury, and reskilling subsidies pay for themselves four times over.

Data
Industrial Injury Register (Arbejdsskaderegisteret) / workers' compensation claims from AES, National Patient Register (LPR), Health Insurance Register (Sygesikringsregisteret), Prescription Drug Database (LMDB), Education Register (UDDA), Course Participant Register (VEUV), Danish Register for Evaluation of Marginalization (DREAM), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), linked to O*NET, Population register (BEF/POP)
Key variables
  • Treatment: work accident causing loss of earning capacity (vs. matched non-injured workers)
  • Outcome: enrollment in and completion of formal degrees and courses
  • Outcome: earnings, hours and occupation (task content)
  • Outcome: disability and other social transfers
  • Severity: assessed earning capacity loss and personal impairment
Period
1995–2017 (work injury claims 1998–2017)
Sample
14,481 injury events (14,369 distinct individuals); 4,568 events (4,528 individuals) for workers with vocational degrees with access to higher education Full-time employed Danish workers (excluding military) who suffered an accepted physical work accident with positive earning capacity loss, compared with matched non-injured workers. source

Permanent Residency and Refugee Immigrants' Skill Investment

Jacob Nielsen Arendt, Christian Dustmann and Hyejin Ku · Journal of Labor Economics, 2025 · Individual

A Danish reform tightened the employment and language requirements refugees must meet to obtain permanent residency. Rather than encouraging investment, it lowered overall employment and barely moved language proficiency, because refugees with weak pre-reform prospects reduced effort when the requirements looked out of reach.

Data
Population, income, education and immigration registers linked via the Central Person Registry, Danish language test results, Employment in annual full-time equivalents (register data)
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual employment in full-time equivalents
  • Outcome: passing the Danish language test at intermediate or higher level
  • Treatment: temporary residency in Jan–Oct 2004 (exposed to the 2007 permanent-residency reform) vs Jan–Oct 2003
  • Heterogeneity: pre-reform employment or language performance type
  • Time: years since temporary residency (YSR 1–7)
Period
Residency cohorts 2003–2004, followed seven years since temporary residency (to 2011)
Sample
767 unique individuals; 5,122 person-year observations Refugees aged 18–64 who applied for asylum after 28 February 2002 and received temporary residency in Jan–Oct 2003 (control) or Jan–Oct 2004 (treatment). source

Genetic and Socioeconomic Achievement Gaps in Elementary School

Mikkel Aagaard Houmark, Victor Ronda, Esben Agerbo, Preben Bo Mortensen and Michael Rosholm · Economic Journal, 2025 · Individual

Shows that the achievement gap by polygenic score for educational attainment is as large as the gap by socioeconomic status and widens during elementary school, but only among low-SES children. Selection into neighbourhoods and schools explains part of the SES gap but none of the genetic gap, which instead relates to conscientiousness and the learning environment.

Data
iPSYCH2015 case-cohort genotype sample (neonatal blood spots from the Danish National Biobank), Danish national tests (reading and mathematics, grades 2–8), Population registers with parent-child links (BEF), Parental income registers (IND), Parental education registers (UDDA), Psychiatric diagnoses of parents (patient registers), School and neighbourhood identifiers
Key variables
  • Outcome: standardised national reading test scores (grades 2, 4, 6, 8); mathematics tests (grades 3, 6, 8)
  • Key regressor: polygenic score for educational attainment (EA PGS)
  • Key regressor: SES index from parental income and education
  • Sibling fixed effects: within-family variation in the PGS
  • Mediators: neighbourhood and school sorting
Period
Births 1994–2008; national tests from 2010
Sample
40,136 individuals with 87,917 reading test outcomes (24,062 psychiatric cases, 16,074 random controls); sibling sample 2,705 individuals Genotyped iPSYCH children of European descent born 1994–2008 who completed at least one national test between 2nd and 8th grade. source

Attendance Boundary Policies and the Limits to Combating School Segregation

Andreas Bjerre-Nielsen and Mikkel Høst Gandil · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

Uses address-level data on the universe of Danish children and changes in school attendance boundaries over time. Households defy reassignment to schools with lower socioeconomic status, resourceful households most of all, so redrawing boundaries toward highly disadvantaged schools barely changes those schools' composition.

Data
Register data on the universe of Danish children (Statistics Denmark), School attendance boundary data with boundary changes over time
Key variables
  • Treatment: reassignment of a residential area to a different school by an attendance boundary change
  • Outcome: enrolment in (or defiance of) the assigned school
  • Heterogeneity: household socioeconomic resources
  • Socioeconomic composition of the new school's students
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish children (universe) whose residential areas were affected by changes in school attendance boundaries. source (abstract)

Grades and Employer Learning

Anne Toft Hansen, Ulrik Hvidman and Hans Henrik Sievertsen · Journal of Labor Economics, 2024 · Individual

Uses a nationwide rescaling of Danish university grades to identify the return to GPA that is unrelated to ability. A reform-induced higher GPA raises earnings right after graduation, especially for graduates with fewer other signals, but the effect fades within a few years, consistent with employers learning quickly about productivity.

Data
University student records (Aarhus University and University of Copenhagen) with grades before and after the 2007 recoding, Administrative registers from Statistics Denmark (background, parental income and education, earnings)
Key variables
  • Outcome: log total gross earnings in each of the first five calendar years after graduation
  • Treatment: reform-induced change in GPA from the 1 August 2007 recoding to the new grading scale
  • Outcome: indicator for positive earnings
  • Controls: gender, age at graduation, high school GPA, parental income, employment and education
Period
Students enrolled on 1 August 2007 who graduated before 2011; earnings for the first five years after graduation
Sample
3,813 students Master's students at Aarhus University or University of Copenhagen enrolled on 1 August 2007 with at most 40 ECTS remaining who graduated before 2011. source

Unintended Consequences of Welfare Cuts on Children and Adolescents

Christian Dustmann, Rasmus Landersø and Lars Højsgaard Andersen · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2024 · Individual

The 2002 benefit cut for newly arrived refugees reduced family disposable income by 30 percent over five years. Children exposed at preschool or school age obtained lower grades, less education and lower adult earnings, and teenagers at exposure saw large increases in convictions for violent and property crime.

Data
Danish Immigration Service residence permit records, Historical Migrations database, Income register from tax authority records, Register on Completed Education, Daycare register, 9th-grade exam grades, 6th-grade national language test scores and school well-being survey (Ministry of Education), Crime registers: police and court records
Key variables
  • Treatment: parents granted residency after the 2002 Start Aid welfare cut
  • Running variable: residency date relative to the reform
  • Outcome: 9th-grade GPA, 6th-grade language test scores, completed education
  • Outcome: adult earnings and employment
  • Outcome: criminal convictions
Period
Residency granted January 2001–December 2003; income data through 2019
Sample
6,888 individuals (3,406 adults aged 18–45 with children and 3,482 children aged 0–18) Refugee families with children granted residency in Denmark within 18 months of the 2002 reform, excluding Afghans, former Yugoslavs and emigrants. source

Language Training and Refugees' Integration

Mette Foged, Linea Hasager, Giovanni Peri, Jacob Nielsen Arendt and Iben Bolvig · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

A 1999 reform improved language training for newly recognised refugees. A regression discontinuity around the cut-off date shows a permanent, positive effect on earnings that emerges after classes are completed, together with more schooling and a higher probability of working in complex jobs.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Income register (IND), Education enrollment and completion registers, Crime register: charges and convictions, Population register (BEF), Admission/residence permit register (OPHG), Migration register (VNDS) and internal mobility register (FLYT), Register for Labour Market Policy Measures (AMFORA), Danish language training database (Danskuddannelsesdatabasen), O*NET task intensities
Key variables
  • Running variable: date refugee status granted relative to January 1, 1999
  • Treatment: 1999 reform expanding Danish language training
  • Outcome: annual earnings and employment
  • Outcome: education, occupation task complexity, crime
  • Outcome (children): lower secondary completion and crime
Period
Admissions 1997–2000; followed until 2016 (children's outcomes until 2018)
Sample
8,558 individuals; children sample of 9,730 Refugees and reunified family members aged 18–49 granted asylum in Denmark between January 1, 1997 and December 31, 2000. source

On the Family Origins of Human Capital Formation: Evidence from Donor Children

Petter Lundborg, Erik Plug and Astrid Würtz Rasmussen · Review of Economic Studies, 2024 · Individual

Uses Danish children conceived through sperm and egg donation to separate nurture from genetic transmission, since donors are not selected on parental traits. Only mothers' education matters once genes are removed: fathers' schooling has no effect on children's test scores, while mothers' schooling raises reading scores as much as among non-donor children.

Data
Danish IVF register (Danish Health Data Authority), Danish Education Register (UDDA), National test scores in reading and math (from 2010), Administrative registers on demographics and labour market (birth year, gender, marital status, children, earnings)
Key variables
  • Outcome: child average standardized national test score (reading and math)
  • Explanatory: mother's and father's years of schooling
  • Variation: donor sperm / donor egg conception (genetically unrelated parent)
  • Controls: month of first donor treatment and clinic fixed effects (waiting-list assignment)
Period
IVF treatments 1994–2007; demographics 1991–2016; labour market 1991–2012; tests from 2010
Sample
19,509 non-donor IVF children, 820 sperm donor children and 157 egg donor children (97 matched to egg donors) Children conceived through IVF treatments in Denmark 1994–2007 with at least one national test score, compared with 30% random samples of non-adopted (650,930) and adopted (2,674) children. source

Tapping into Talent: Coupling Education and Innovation Policies for Economic Growth

Ufuk Akcigit, Jeremy Pearce and Marta Prato · Review of Economic Studies, 2024 · Individual

Builds an endogenous growth model with heterogeneous talent, financial resources and career choice, and links it to Danish micro data on who obtains a PhD and who becomes an inventor. R&D subsidies work better when combined with higher education subsidies that let talented but poor youth pursue research, and education policy matters most in unequal societies.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-linked IDA (FIDA), PhD enrolment register (PHD), Military conscription IQ test (Boerge Prien Proeve), European Patent Office patents linked to IDA, R&D funding data (Innovation Danmark) and Ministry of Education PhD policy data
Key variables
  • Outcome: PhD enrolment/attainment
  • Outcome: patent filing (inventor status)
  • Talent proxy: military IQ test score
  • Background: parental income
  • Policy: 2002 expansion of PhD slots and R&D subsidies
Period
2001–2013 (background data from 1980)
Sample
approximately 32 million employer-employee observations, 10,000 inventors and 37,000 unique PhDs; approximately 500,000 males with IQ data Danish population in IDA linked to PhD, IQ test and patent data. source

Intergenerational Spillover Effects of Language Training for Refugees

Mette Foged, Linea Hasager, Giovanni Peri, Jacob Nielsen Arendt and Iben Bolvig · Journal of Public Economics, 2023 · Individual

Follows the children of refugees affected by the 1999 Danish reform that improved language training. Children of treated parents achieve better educational outcomes, showing that the integration gains from language training carry over to the next generation.

Data
Danish administrative registers from Statistics Denmark on refugees' asylum/admission dates and parent–child links, Lower secondary school completion and final exam records, Criminal conviction records (property, violent, narcotics crime), Earnings, occupation (ISCO), disposable income, marriage and fertility registers for parents
Key variables
  • Running variable: parent's admission (asylum grant) date relative to 1 January 1999 (Integration Act language training reform)
  • Outcome: completing lower secondary school and taking final exams by age 18
  • Outcome: criminal convictions between ages 15 and 18
  • Parental outcomes: annual earnings, complex job, disposable income, convictions
Period
Children born 1990–2001, followed up to 2019; parental outcomes 1999–2016
Sample
6,467 children; 5,197 adult parents Children born 1990–2001 with at least one parent granted asylum in Denmark within a four-year window around 1 January 1999, who arrived before or during primary school age. source

Spillover Effects of Early-Life Medical Interventions

N. Meltem Daysal, Marianne Simonsen, Mircea Trandafir and Sanni Breining · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2022 · Individual

Extra medical treatment for very-low-birth-weight newborns, identified from a birth weight threshold, improves not only the treated infant but also the outcomes of older siblings, who do better in school. Family resources and parental time respond to the child's health.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR): birth weight, gestational age, parity, Parent and sibling links (BEF), 9th-grade exam test score register (reading and math), Education/enrollment registers, Prescription drug register (antidepressant use, from 1995), Health registers for diagnoses (intellectual disability, ADHD) and mortality, Income and labour market registers for parents
Key variables
  • Running variable: focal child's birth weight relative to 1,500-gram VLBW cutoff
  • Outcome: siblings' standardized 9th-grade reading and math test scores
  • Outcome: sibling enrollment beyond compulsory education by age 19 and high-school track
  • Mechanisms: focal-child mortality and mental health; maternal antidepressant use; parental income, employment, days worked
Period
Focal children born 1982–1993; test scores 2001–2010
Sample
5,827 sibling observations (siblings of 3,677 focal children) Siblings (same mother) of Danish children born 1982–1993 with birth weight between 1,300 and 1,700 grams. source

Education, Birth Order and Family Size

Jesper Bagger, Javier A. Birchenall, Hani Mansour and Sergio Urzúa · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Develops a model of parental investment with birth order preferences and human capital cost differences that delivers a generalised quantity–quality trade-off. Danish administrative data confirm its predictions: at any given parity, children in smaller families have more education, and the average child's education falls with family size even after accounting for birth order.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), 1980–2006, with mother-child links, Precise date of birth from Statistics Denmark (to identify twin/multiple births)
Key variables
  • Outcome: years of education (measured in 2006)
  • Key regressors: birth order and family size
  • Instrument: twin birth at last birth (twins at 2nd, 3rd or 4th birth)
  • Controls: parental education, gender, age
Period
1980–2006 (outcomes measured in 2006)
Sample
1,256,031 children in 581,159 families (excluding single-child families) Individuals aged 25–41 in 2006 whose mother was alive and present in IDA during 1980–2006, in families with completed fertility, siblings defined by common mother. source

Exposure to More Female Peers Widens the Gender Gap in STEM Participation

Anne Ardila Brenøe and Ulf Zölitz · Journal of Labor Economics, 2020 · Individual

Exploits idiosyncratic variation in the share of female peers across cohorts within Danish high schools. A higher share of female peers lowers women's probability of enrolling in and graduating from STEM programmes and raises men's, widening the gender gap in STEM.

Data
Student/education registers with high school, track and cohort identifiers (UDDA), Population register with parental links (BEF), Parental income and education registers (IND, UDDA), Earnings and employment registers, Occupation codes (STEM occupations), Fertility from population/birth registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: STEM college enrolment and completion (ISCED fields 05, 06, 07)
  • Treatment: proportion of female peers in school-cohort of the math track
  • Identification: within-school across-cohort variation (school and cohort fixed effects)
  • Long-run outcomes: STEM occupation, earnings at age 36, gender wage gap, fertility
Period
High school entry 1980–1994; outcomes followed up to 20 years after entry
Sample
182,211 students in 127 schools (1,877 school-cohort observations) All students entering the math track of Danish academic high schools between 1980 and 1994, excluding schools with tiny or atypical cohorts. source

What Is the Added Value of Preschool for Poor Children? Long-Term and Intergenerational Impacts and Interactions with an Infant Health Intervention

Maya Rossin-Slater and Miriam Wüst · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2020 · Individual

Access to preschool targeted at low-income children between 1933 and 1960 raised adult education, earnings and survival past 65, and the children of exposed women obtained more education by 25. Exposure to the nurse home visiting programme in infancy reduces preschool's added value, so the two programmes are partial substitutes.

Data
Archival data on all approved Danish preschools 1933–1960 (establishment and approval year, slots), Nurse Home Visiting program implementation dates by municipality, Statistical Commune Data Archive (elections and censuses) for municipality controls, Municipal live births and infant deaths, DigDag historical administrative geography (parish-municipality mapping), Administrative population register data 1980–2012 (education, earnings, survival, hospital admissions, diagnoses), Mother-child links (Danish Civil Registration System)
Key variables
  • Treatment: approved preschool in municipality of birth at age 3
  • Interaction: exposure to the infant Nurse Home Visiting program
  • Outcome: education and earnings around age 50
  • Outcome: survival past age 65, hospital admissions, diagnoses
  • Intergenerational outcome: oldest child's educational attainment by age 25
  • Controls: municipality and birth-year fixed effects, county-by-year trends, municipality characteristics
Period
Cohorts born 1930–1957; outcomes 1980–2012; children born 1955–1987
Sample
1,640,214 observations (Danish-born with valid parish codes); 880,708 observations in municipalities with an approved preschool by 1960, collapsed to 3,862 municipality × birth-year cells Danish-born individuals born 1930–1957 with a valid parish of birth who are observed after 1980, focusing on the 138 municipalities that ever had an approved preschool. source

The Opportunity Costs of Mandatory Military Service: Evidence from a Draft Lottery

Paul Bingley, Petter Lundborg and Stéphanie Vincent Lyk-Jensen · Journal of Labor Economics, 2020 · Individual

Uses the Danish draft lottery to estimate the effect of conscription on later outcomes. Military service delays education and labour market entry, and the paper quantifies the resulting losses in schooling and earnings for those who serve.

Data
Danish Defence military register (Armed Forces Day draft records: IQ test, eligibility, height, lottery number), Earnings from employer reports to tax authorities (income registers), Education registers (UDDA), Central Crime Register, National hospital discharge register (LPR), Medical Birth Register (MFR) birth weight, Population register (BEF)
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual labour income at ages 25–35
  • Treatment: military service
  • Instrument: draft lottery number below the threshold
  • Heterogeneity: cognitive ability (draft board IQ test)
  • Mechanisms: years of schooling, employment, health care use, crime
Period
Birth cohorts 1976–1983; earnings at ages 25–35
Sample
152,269 men without missing earnings (military register covers 155,570 fit-for-service conscripts) Danish men born 1976–1983 declared fit for military service who drew a draft lottery number at the Armed Forces Day. source

Labor Supply and Optimization Frictions: Evidence from the Danish Student Labor Market

Jakob Egholt Søgaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2019 · Individual

Danish students lose part of their study grant when annual earnings exceed a threshold. Register data show bunching below the threshold that grows as students gain experience with the rule, so optimisation frictions hide a structural elasticity well above the observed response.

Data
Danish administrative registers for the full population (education, earnings, benefits, demographics), Annual income tax returns, Monthly student benefit (SU) take-up records from the Student Benefit Administration, Monthly payroll records of earnings and hours (BFL/eIndkomst), from 2008
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual labour earnings relative to the student benefit income limit (bunching)
  • Incentive: kink/notch from phase-out (payback) of student grants above the individual income limit
  • Simulated individual marginal tax and phase-out rates
  • Monthly earnings and benefit take-up (timing of adjustment)
Period
2006–2011 (monthly earnings from 2008)
Sample
around 75,000 observations per year (core sample 75,500 individuals per year on average) Individuals aged 18–35 enrolled in tertiary education, fully eligible for student benefits and with positive labour earnings (above DKK 8,000 per year). source

Spillovers in Education Choice

Juanna Schrøter Joensen and Helena Skyt Nielsen · Journal of Public Economics, 2018 · Individual

Exploits the Danish high school pilot that made advanced mathematics cheaper to take for some cohorts. An older sibling's advanced maths choice raises the probability that the younger sibling also chooses advanced maths, with spillovers that depend on the siblings' gender composition.

Data
Danish administrative registers on high school enrolment, course packages and completion (Statistics Denmark education registers), Parent and sibling links from population registers, Parental education and income registers, Earnings registers (younger siblings' earnings at ages 30–35)
Key variables
  • Outcome: younger sibling chooses advanced math-science course package in high school
  • Endogenous regressor: older sibling's advanced math-science choice
  • Instrument: older sibling enrolled in a high school that unexpectedly introduced the pilot scheme (PilotIntro)
  • Outcomes: younger sibling's college/STEM education at age 30 and log earnings at ages 30–35
  • Controls: parental education and income, county and entry-cohort fixed effects
Period
High school entry cohorts 1984–1987 (older siblings) and 1988–1991 (younger siblings, main sample); register data from 1980 onwards
Sample
7,786 sibling pairs (main closely spaced sample); 17,691 sibling pairs in the wider sample with age gap < 10 years Older siblings entering academic high school 1984–1987 who graduated in three years, paired with younger siblings entering high school 1988–1991 with an age gap of at most 4 years. source

Measurement Error in Income and Schooling and the Bias of Linear Estimators

Paul Bingley and Alessandro Martinello · Journal of Labor Economics, 2017 · Individual

Links survey reports of income and schooling for Danish respondents to the same variables in the registers. Measurement errors in survey income and schooling are correlated and non-classical, so standard corrections fail, and the paper quantifies the resulting bias in linear estimators such as returns to schooling.

Data
Survey of Health, Ageing and Retirement in Europe (SHARE), Danish respondents, Danish administrative income registers, Danish administrative education registers
Key variables
  • Variable validated: survey-reported annual income vs register income
  • Variable validated: survey-reported schooling vs register schooling
  • Estimand: measurement error bias in OLS and IV estimators of returns to schooling
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish SHARE respondents linked to administrative registers to validate survey income and schooling. source (abstract)

School Starting Age and the Crime-Age Profile

Rasmus Landersø, Helena Skyt Nielsen and Marianne Simonsen · Economic Journal, 2017 · Individual

Children born just after the school start cut-off begin school almost a year later. Starting school older lowers crime at young ages, especially for boys, by shifting the crime-age profile rather than permanently reducing crime, consistent with an incapacitation and maturity channel.

Data
Population register with exact birth dates and parent links (BEF), Criminal charge register (charges for property, violence and other crimes, with dates), Birth records: birth weight, gestational length, APGAR score (likely Medical Birth Register, MFR; not named in paper), Education registers (age in 8th grade as proxy for school starting age), Parental education and labour market registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: criminal charge at a given age / at or before a given age (from age 15)
  • Treatment: school starting age (7.6 vs 6.6)
  • Instrument/running variable: birth date relative to January 1 cut-off (±30 days)
  • Heterogeneity: gender; crime type (property, violence, other)
Period
Children born mid-1981 to mid-1993
Sample
48,546 and 50,383 children (the two gender groups) at age 15, born in December or January (Table 1) Danish children born in December or January between December 1981 and January 1993 (main bandwidth ±30 days around January 1). source

Mathematics and Gender: Heterogeneity in Causes and Consequences

Juanna Schrøter Joensen and Helena Skyt Nielsen · Economic Journal, 2016 · Individual

A pilot scheme in Danish high schools that let students combine advanced mathematics with chemistry increased advanced maths take-up, especially among girls. The induced maths qualification raised later earnings, with the gains concentrated among students who would otherwise have taken less maths and operating through higher education choices.

Data
Education registers: detailed educational histories, high school branch choice and GPA (Statistics Denmark), Labour market registers: earnings, occupation, industry, self-employment, part-time work
Key variables
  • Outcome: log earnings (measured 18 years after high school graduation)
  • Outcomes: college graduation, field of major, occupation/top corporate jobs, sector
  • Treatment: choice of advanced high school math
  • Instrument: unexpected introduction of high school pilot scheme allowing math–chemistry combination
Period
High school entrants 1984–1986, followed into the labour market
Sample
about 25,658 females and 19,406 males from about 140 high schools Danish high school entrants of 1984–86 who graduated in three years and have at least one year of employment. source

The Dynamics of Marriage and Divorce

Gustaf Bruze, Michael Svarer and Yoram Weiss · Journal of Labor Economics, 2015 · Individual

Estimates a dynamic model of marriage, divorce and remarriage with learning about match quality on long register histories of Danish cohorts. Spousal education is complementary in generating marital surplus, education stabilises marriages, education raises men's share of the surplus, and divorce costs are high both early and late in a marriage.

Data
Population register with marital and cohabitation histories (BEF), Education registers (UDDA), completed education at age 46
Key variables
  • Outcome: transitions into and out of marriage, divorce and remarriage
  • Key variable: completed education group at 46 (high school or less, vocational, college)
  • Assortative matching by education of spouses
  • Structural model parameters: match quality, gains from marriage, divorce costs
Period
1980–2006 (ages 20 to 46)
Sample
61,780 individuals (31,835 men and 29,945 women) All men and women born in Denmark in 1960 with complete residence records 1980–2006 and not married at age 20. source

Estimating Class-size Effects using Within-school Variation in Subject-specific Classes

Eskil Heinesen · Economic Journal, 2010 · Individual

Uses within-school variation over time in the size of French classes, which Danish seventh graders choose between French and German, as quasi-random variation in class size. Larger classes lower French exam marks, especially for weaker students and boys, and marks in subjects taught in the regular class serve as a check on selection.

Data
Administrative registers on 9th-grade marks and exam results, school attended, merged via personal and school ID numbers, Family background registers (parents' education, income, labour market status, family structure)
Key variables
  • Outcome: French examination marks at end of 9th grade
  • Treatment: French class size (school/year cell)
  • Identification: within-school year-to-year variation in subject-specific class size
  • Placebo/control: marks in other subjects taught in basic classes
Period
School years 2001/2002–2003/2004
Sample
6,507 students in 782 school/year cells and 375 schools 9th-grade students in 2002–2004 studying French at schools offering both German and French from 7th grade, with at most 20 French students per grade. source

Estimating the Effect of Student Aid on College Enrollment: Evidence from a Government Grant Policy Reform

Helena Skyt Nielsen, Torben Sørensen and Christopher Taber · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2010 · Individual

A 1988 reform raised Danish student grants for students from higher-income families. Roughly 1,000 dollars of extra aid raised enrolment by about 1.35 percentage points, a response that is consistent with modest credit constraints rather than binding ones.

Data
Register-based dataset covering the entire Danish population, 1983–2005 (Statistics Denmark), Educational event histories (education registers), 1973–2005, High school GPA records, Parental income used for student-aid means-testing (tax records), Parental liquid assets from wealth/tax records
Key variables
  • Outcome: college enrollment within one year of high school graduation
  • Treatment: stipend (fraction of full student grant) the individual is eligible for
  • Variation: 1988 Danish student grant reform and kinked means-tested aid schedule
  • Running/ranking variable: parental (social) income percentile
  • Borrowing-constraint proxy: low parental liquid assets
  • Controls: high school GPA, sex, age, region, parents' education
Period
1983–2005 (population register data); high school graduation cohorts 1983–1990, estimation on cohorts 1985 and 1988–1990
Sample
108,933 high school graduates in the final selected sample; 54,843 observations in the main probit estimates (cohorts 1985, 1988–1990) Danish graduates of the ordinary academic high school track who graduated at ages 19–20 in 1983–1990, with observed GPA and parental information. source

Non-cognitive Child Outcomes and Universal High Quality Child Care

Nabanita Datta Gupta and Marianne Simonsen · Journal of Public Economics, 2010 · Individual

Links the Danish Longitudinal Survey of Children to the registers to compare non-cognitive outcomes at age seven for children in universal preschool, family day care and home care at age three. Attending high-quality preschool does not harm non-cognitive outcomes relative to home care, while long hours in family day care are associated with worse outcomes for some groups.

Data
Danish Longitudinal Survey of Children (DALSC), waves 1996, 1999, 2003, Administrative registers on parents' education, labour market status, hours, wages and income (1994–2003), Day Care Register (Statistics Denmark), used descriptively
Key variables
  • Outcome: Strengths and Difficulties Questionnaire (SDQ) index at age 7½
  • Treatment: mode of care at age 3½ (preschool, family day care, home care)
  • Instrument/pseudo-experiment: municipality differences in childcare take-up and provision
  • Heterogeneity: child gender and mother's education
  • Hours in non-parental care
Period
1994–2003 (children born 1995; care measured 1999, outcomes 2003)
Sample
4,343 observations (children) in the main OLS estimates About 6,000 Danish children born between 15 September and 31 October 1995 surveyed in DALSC, linked to parental register data. source

Inequality and fairness

Top wealth, tax evasion, sibling correlations and what people believe about their own position.

Educational Ambition, Marital Sorting, and Inequality

Frederik Almar, Benjamin Friedrich, Ana Reynoso, Bastian Schulz and Rune Vejlin · Journal of Labor Economics, 2026 · Individual

Classifies detailed Danish education programmes into ambition types by their graduates' starting wages and wage growth. Assortative matching by educational ambition has risen sharply even though sorting by conventional education levels looks flat, and the marriage market accounts for more than 40 percent of the rise in household income inequality since 1980.

Data
Population register with partner links (BEF), Education register with four-digit ISCED programme codes (UDDA), Employment register with hourly wages (IDAN), Income register (IND), Danish Labour Force Survey (LFS/AKU), 2000–2018, Employment register for hours, part-time/full-time (RAS)
Key variables
  • Classification: educational 'ambition types' from programme-level starting wages and wage growth (about 1,800 programmes)
  • Outcome: marital sorting by ambition type (assortative matching)
  • Outcome: household labour income inequality
  • Wage measure: residualised log hourly wages by programme
  • LFS job attributes: hours, evening/weekend/home work
Period
1980–2018
Sample
on average 3,031,511 individuals per year aged 19–60; 1,800,866 individuals in couples per year All individuals living in Denmark aged 19–60 in 1980–2018, with couples defined as married or cohabiting opposite-sex partners. source

Sick of Your Poor Neighborhood? Quasi-Experimental Evidence on Neighborhood Effects on Health

Linea Hasager and Mia Jørgensen · Journal of Public Economics, 2026 · Individual

Uses the quasi-random dispersal of refugees across Danish neighbourhoods from 1986 to 1998. Refugees placed in low-income neighbourhoods had a 12 percent higher risk of developing a lifestyle-related disease 8 to 15 years later, driven by neighbours and the local environment rather than health care access, ethnic networks or labour market outcomes.

Data
National Patient Register (LPR), Health Insurance Register (SYSI, SSSY), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Income register (IND), Population register (BEF) and address/mobility registers (BEFBOP, BEFADR), Migration register (VNDS), Criminal convictions register (KRAF), O*NET occupational data merged via occupation codes
Key variables
  • Treatment: assignment to a bottom- vs top-income-third parish (neighbourhood median household disposable income) under the 1986–1998 Refugee Spatial Dispersal Policy
  • Outcome: lifestyle-related disease diagnosis (circulatory, nutritional/metabolic, COPD, hip arthrosis, alcohol-related) 2–15 and 8–15 years after immigration
  • Outcome: mental disorder diagnoses
  • Mechanisms: health care access, employment and earnings, neighbour characteristics
Period
1985–2017 (refugees arriving 1986–1998)
Sample
21,965 refugees Refugees aged 18–64 at arrival from nine refugee-sending countries who arrived in Denmark in 1986–1998 and were assigned a municipality under the dispersal policy (excluding family-reunified spouses). source

Managing Inequality: Manager-Specific Wage Premiums and Selection in the Managerial Labor Market

Alex Xi He and Daniel le Maire · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual + firm

Builds a manager-firm-worker matched dataset for the whole Danish population to estimate manager effects on wages. Manager-specific premiums are almost as important as firm premiums for between-firm wage inequality, are unrelated to productivity, and high-paying managers are more likely to be replaced after acquisitions.

Data
Matched employer-employee data covering all Danish workers and firms (IDA), Occupation codes used to identify managers, Firm balance sheet data for private-sector firms (FIRE), Mergers and acquisitions identified from firm and establishment identifiers
Key variables
  • Outcome: worker log wages
  • Key estimate: manager-specific wage premiums (manager fixed effects identified from worker and manager mobility)
  • Comparison: firm-specific wage premiums
  • Event: firm acquisition and manager replacement
  • Heterogeneity: industry concentration (HHI)
Period
1995–2011 (per 2019 working-paper slides)
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Manager-firm-worker matched panel covering the entire Danish population; acquisitions sample of about 3,700 private-sector acquisitions in the 2019 working-paper version. source (abstract)

The Physician-Patient Match and Health Inequality

Ida Lykke Kristiansen and Sophie Yanying Sheng · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2025 · Individual

Measures general practitioners' childhood socioeconomic status from their parents' education in the registers. Low-SES patients matched to a GP of similar background have substantially lower mortality, while high-SES patients are unaffected, cutting the SES mortality gradient by a quarter through more intensive care, better detection of chronic conditions and better adherence.

Data
National Health Service Register: patient-to-GP clinic assignment, Service Provider Registry (physician IDs), Cause of Death Register, National Health Insurance Service Registry: visits, services, reimbursements, Hospital/patient data and first-line treatments for chronic conditions, Education registers and parent links (BEF/UDDA) for patient SES and physicians' parental education
Key variables
  • Treatment: assignment to a low-SES (parental education) physician after clinic closure
  • Outcome: patient mortality and cause of death
  • Outcome: detection/treatment of chronic conditions (CVC, cancer, diabetes, COPD)
  • Outcome: health care utilization (visits, services per visit, reimbursements)
  • Patient SES: highest completed education (primary school = low SES)
Period
1995–2019 (clinic closures 1999–2016)
Sample
over 352,000 adult patients affected by 894 clinic closures; 1,019,005 observations in the main mortality regression Danish adults aged 40–70 whose primary care clinic closed between 1999 and 2016, observed at least four years before the closure. source

Background Matters, but Not Whether Parents Are Immigrants: Outcomes of Children Born in Denmark

Mathias Fjællegaard Jensen and Alan Manning · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2025 · Individual

Children born in Denmark to immigrant parents have lower earnings, more unemployment, less education, more transfers and more convictions than children of Danish-born parents. Conditional on parental socioeconomic characteristics, however, they do as well or slightly better, so the gaps reflect deprived backgrounds rather than immigrant parentage.

Data
