Danish register data linking children to parents (Statistics Denmark, 1980–2017), Income and earnings registers (annual labour income incl. self-employment, transfers), Monthly employment register (hours, hourly wages, from 2008), Education registers (incl. survey-based registration of foreign education of immigrants), Criminal justice registers (convictions, prison sentences, dropped charges / not-guilty verdicts), Occupation codes (ISCO88)
Key variables
  • Group: number of parents born outside Denmark (0, 1, 2; Greenland/Faroe Islands)
  • Outcome: earnings and unemployment at age 30
  • Outcome: public transfers, completed education by age 30
  • Outcome: criminal convictions, prison sentences, dropped charges
  • Controls: parental income, unemployment years, occupation and education during childhood
Period
1980–2017
Sample
396,417 children (Table 1 total); 362,460 with two Danish-born parents, 10,399 with two immigrant parents, 20,908 with one immigrant parent, 2,650 with a parent from Greenland/Faroe Islands All children born in Denmark 1980–1987 with both parents known, followed to age 30. source

Genetic and Socioeconomic Achievement Gaps in Elementary School

Mikkel Aagaard Houmark, Victor Ronda, Esben Agerbo, Preben Bo Mortensen and Michael Rosholm · Economic Journal, 2025 · Individual

Shows that the achievement gap by polygenic score for educational attainment is as large as the gap by socioeconomic status and widens during elementary school, but only among low-SES children. Selection into neighbourhoods and schools explains part of the SES gap but none of the genetic gap, which instead relates to conscientiousness and the learning environment.

Data
iPSYCH2015 case-cohort genotype sample (neonatal blood spots from the Danish National Biobank), Danish national tests (reading and mathematics, grades 2–8), Population registers with parent-child links (BEF), Parental income registers (IND), Parental education registers (UDDA), Psychiatric diagnoses of parents (patient registers), School and neighbourhood identifiers
Key variables
  • Outcome: standardised national reading test scores (grades 2, 4, 6, 8); mathematics tests (grades 3, 6, 8)
  • Key regressor: polygenic score for educational attainment (EA PGS)
  • Key regressor: SES index from parental income and education
  • Sibling fixed effects: within-family variation in the PGS
  • Mediators: neighbourhood and school sorting
Period
Births 1994–2008; national tests from 2010
Sample
40,136 individuals with 87,917 reading test outcomes (24,062 psychiatric cases, 16,074 random controls); sibling sample 2,705 individuals Genotyped iPSYCH children of European descent born 1994–2008 who completed at least one national test between 2nd and 8th grade. source

The Impact of the Level and Timing of Parental Resources on Child Development and Intergenerational Mobility

Sadegh Eshaghnia, James J. Heckman and Rasmus Landersø · Journal of Labor Economics, 2025 · Individual

Uses Danish registers covering parents' full income histories to separate the level of parents' lifetime resources from their timing during childhood. Lifetime resources matter far more than when income arrives, and conventional intergenerational elasticities based on short income windows give a misleading picture of mobility.

Data
Population register with family links (BEF), Education register (UDDA), Income, assets and transfers register (IND), Sentencing register (KRAF), Incarceration register (KRIN), Danish Household Expenditure Survey, Danish Longitudinal Survey of Children (DALSC), 9th-grade national leaving exam scores
Key variables
  • Outcome: child income at ages 30–35 (IGE)
  • Outcome: years of education by age 35
  • Outcome: any criminal conviction by age 35
  • Outcome: math test score at age 16 and language test at age 11
  • Explanatory: level and timing of parental income/resources by child age (lifetime vs snapshot)
Period
1980–2019
Sample
around 100,000 children and their families (105,953 individuals; 98,686 in the IGE log-log sample) Children born in 1981 and 1982 linked to their parents, non-migrants with non-migrant parents; plus the 1995–1997 birth cohorts for exam scores and DALSC children born 1995. source

Attendance Boundary Policies and the Limits to Combating School Segregation

Andreas Bjerre-Nielsen and Mikkel Høst Gandil · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2024 · Individual

Uses address-level data on the universe of Danish children and changes in school attendance boundaries over time. Households defy reassignment to schools with lower socioeconomic status, resourceful households most of all, so redrawing boundaries toward highly disadvantaged schools barely changes those schools' composition.

Data
Register data on the universe of Danish children (Statistics Denmark), School attendance boundary data with boundary changes over time
Key variables
  • Treatment: reassignment of a residential area to a different school by an attendance boundary change
  • Outcome: enrolment in (or defiance of) the assigned school
  • Heterogeneity: household socioeconomic resources
  • Socioeconomic composition of the new school's students
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish children (universe) whose residential areas were affected by changes in school attendance boundaries. source (abstract)

Understanding the Rise in Life Expectancy Inequality

Gordon B. Dahl, Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Torben Heien Nielsen and Benjamin Ly Serena · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

Decomposes widening life expectancy gaps between rich and poor into changes in age-specific mortality and differences in survivability to each age. Lower survivability of the poor explains the entire rise in Danish life expectancy inequality; cardiovascular gains helped everyone, but lifestyle-related survivability diverged.

Data
Population registers (BEF, FAIN): sex and age, Income register (IND): household income net of universal transfers, Death registers (DODAARS, DODAASG) with cause of death, US mortality by income from Chetty et al. (2016), IRS tax data
Key variables
  • Outcome: period life expectancy by income group
  • Income rank: household income measured two years before mortality
  • Decomposition: mortality changes vs. survivability
  • Cause of death: cardiovascular, cancer, lifestyle-related, other
Period
2001–2014
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Universe of Danish residents 2001–2014, compared with US tax-return-based mortality data. source

Tapping into Talent: Coupling Education and Innovation Policies for Economic Growth

Ufuk Akcigit, Jeremy Pearce and Marta Prato · Review of Economic Studies, 2024 · Individual

Builds an endogenous growth model with heterogeneous talent, financial resources and career choice, and links it to Danish micro data on who obtains a PhD and who becomes an inventor. R&D subsidies work better when combined with higher education subsidies that let talented but poor youth pursue research, and education policy matters most in unequal societies.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-linked IDA (FIDA), PhD enrolment register (PHD), Military conscription IQ test (Boerge Prien Proeve), European Patent Office patents linked to IDA, R&D funding data (Innovation Danmark) and Ministry of Education PhD policy data
Key variables
  • Outcome: PhD enrolment/attainment
  • Outcome: patent filing (inventor status)
  • Talent proxy: military IQ test score
  • Background: parental income
  • Policy: 2002 expansion of PhD slots and R&D subsidies
Period
2001–2013 (background data from 1980)
Sample
approximately 32 million employer-employee observations, 10,000 inventors and 37,000 unique PhDs; approximately 500,000 males with IQ data Danish population in IDA linked to PhD, IQ test and patent data. source

Social Positions and Fairness Views on Inequality

Kristoffer B. Hvidberg, Claus T. Kreiner and Stefanie Stantcheva · Review of Economic Studies, 2023 · Individual

Links a large survey to income registers to compare perceived and actual positions in the income distribution and within reference groups (co-workers, neighbours, schoolmates). People misperceive their rank, fairness views depend on the perceived gap to others, and showing people their true position changes their views.

Data
Custom online survey with randomised information treatment (February–March 2019), sent via Digital Post and linked on CPR, Income registers / tax returns (IND) for respondents and all reference group members, Population register (BEF) for cohort, municipality, neighbours and siblings, Education register (UDDA) incl. school attended at age 15 for schoolmates, Workplace links from employer-employee data (IDA) for co-workers and sector, Parents' income positions via family links
Key variables
  • Outcome: perceived own income position (rank) in each reference group
  • Outcome: perceived P50 and P95 incomes in reference groups
  • Outcome: fairness views on inequality within reference groups
  • Actual income rank computed from register data
  • Treatment: randomised information on actual income position
  • Events: unemployment, promotion and income shocks from income histories
Period
Survey 2019 (income for 2017); register income histories
Sample
9,415 respondents (from 50,100 invited) Danish-born people born 1969–1973 (aged 45–50), randomly drawn by Statistics Denmark, who completed the survey. source

Family, Community and Long-Term Socio-Economic Inequality: Evidence from Siblings and Youth Peers

Paul Bingley, Lorenzo Cappellari and Konstantinos Tatsiramos · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Decomposes sibling correlations in permanent earnings, education and unemployment for the Danish population into family and community components, allowing for life-cycle dynamics. Family is the dominant factor; community background shapes earnings and unemployment correlations early in working life but fades quickly.

Data
Civil registration system / population register with parent links (BEF), Central person register address histories (parish of residence), Education register / national pupil database (school at age 15), Annual earnings from tax-based income registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: pre-tax annual labour earnings (residual log earnings), 1984–2011
  • Grouping: brothers (family), schoolmates at age 15, parish neighbours at age 15
  • Parameters: sibling, school-peer and neighbour correlations over the life cycle
Period
Men born 1960–1983; earnings 1984–2011
Sample
695,960 men; 10,930,859 earnings observations (122,652 brother pairs, 380,948 singletons) Danish men born 1960–1983 (first and second sons sharing both legal parents, plus singletons and same-age peers), linked to schoolmates and parish neighbours at age 15. source

Financial Trouble Across Generations: Evidence from the Universe of Personal Loans in Denmark

Claus Thustrup Kreiner, Søren Leth-Petersen and Louise Charlotte Willerslev-Olsen · Economic Journal, 2020 · Individual

Loan-level records on all personal loans show strong intergenerational persistence in financial trouble: children of parents who default are far more likely to default themselves, and the correlation is present already in early adulthood and is not explained by income or education.

Data
Loan register from the Danish Tax Agency (SKAT): all personal loan accounts with default status (60+ days late), Population register (BEF) for parent-child links, Income-tax register and other administrative registers on labour market history, income and wealth, Credit bureau bad-payer files (Experian, Debitor Registret), 2009 snapshot, Survey of 1,748 individuals on risk willingness, patience and impulsivity (2014)
Key variables
  • Outcome: child default on personal loans (60+ days late)
  • Key regressor: parental default status
  • Preference measures from survey (risk willingness, patience, impulsivity)
  • Controls: income, wealth, education, GPA
Period
2004–2011
Sample
28,027,610 parent-child-year observations on 2,501,088 individuals All Danes aged 18–45 in 2004–2011 with at least one living parent, organized as parent-child-year cells. source

Wealth Taxation and Wealth Accumulation: Theory and Evidence from Denmark

Katrine Jakobsen, Kristian Jakobsen, Henrik Kleven and Gabriel Zucman · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2020 · Individual

Exploits the 1989 Danish wealth tax reform, which cut rates sharply for the wealthy, to estimate long-run effects of wealth taxes on wealth accumulation. Responses are sizable for the very wealthy (top one percent) and small for the moderately wealthy, with effects that keep growing over a decade.

Data
Administrative wealth registry (third-party reported assets and debts, since 1980), Income registers and socio-economic registers (occupation, family composition)
Key variables
  • Outcome: log taxable wealth and third-party reported wealth
  • Treatment: 1989 wealth tax cut (unbound vs bound by tax ceiling: ceiling DD)
  • Treatment: doubling of couples' exemption threshold (couples DD)
  • Running variable for bunching: taxable wealth around exemption threshold
  • Controls: pre-reform portfolio shares and income deciles
Period
Wealth data since 1980; quasi-experimental panel 1985–1996
Sample
138,384 observations (ceiling DD) and 520,452 observations (couples DD, couples vs couples) Balanced panels of Danish households in the top 1% (ceiling DD) or married couples in the top 5% (couples DD) of the wealth distribution before the 1989 reform. source

Time Discounting and Wealth Inequality

Thomas Epper, Ernst Fehr, Helga Fehr-Duda, Claus Thustrup Kreiner, David Dreyer Lassen, Søren Leth-Petersen and Gregers Nytoft Rasmussen · American Economic Review, 2020 · Individual

Links incentivised time-discounting experiments to the wealth positions of a large sample of middle-aged Danes in the registers. Patient individuals sit markedly higher in the wealth distribution, and the association survives controls for education, income profiles, school grades, initial and parental wealth, credit constraints and risk preferences, which points to saving behaviour as the channel.

Data
Incentivised online convex time budget experiment (February 2015) linked on CPR, Income and wealth registers based on third-party reports (1980–2015), Population register (BEF) for sampling and demographics, Education and family registers, Survey of time discounting for the 1952–1955 cohorts at ages 18–21
Key variables
  • Outcome: individual's percentile rank in the net wealth distribution
  • Main regressor: experimentally measured patience (time discounting)
  • Controls: education, income, parental wealth, risk attitudes
  • Heterogeneity: credit-constrained vs unconstrained individuals
Period
Experiment in 2015; register data 1980–2015 (wealth ranks tracked 2001–2015)
Sample
3,620 experiment participants (of 27,613 invited); 2,548 survey subjects in the 1952–1955 cohorts Danes born 1973–1983 who lived in Copenhagen municipality at age seven and completed the online experiment. source

Tax Evasion and Inequality

Annette Alstadsæter, Niels Johannesen and Gabriel Zucman · American Economic Review, 2019 · Individual

Matches leaked offshore account data (HSBC Switzerland, Panama Papers) and tax amnesty records to Scandinavian wealth registers. Tax evasion rises steeply with wealth: the top 0.01 percent evade about a quarter of their taxes, which materially raises measured top wealth shares.

Data
Leaked HSBC Switzerland client list (Swiss Leaks, 2007) matched to tax records in Denmark, Norway and Sweden, Panama Papers / Mossack Fonseca leak matched to Norwegian and Swedish wealth data (not Denmark), Tax amnesty voluntary disclosures in Norway and Sweden, Population-wide administrative wealth and income tax records for Norway, Sweden and Denmark, Danish stratified random tax audits by SKAT (tax years 2008, 2010, 2012), National accounts household wealth totals
Key variables
  • Outcome: taxes evaded (share of taxes owed) by wealth group
  • Ranking variable: household net wealth including imputed pension wealth
  • Indicator: owning an undeclared HSBC account / Panama Papers shell / amnesty participation
  • Outcome (event study): reported wealth and income after amnesty
  • Random-audit detected evasion (domestic evasion)
Period
HSBC leak 2006–2007; amnesties since 2006; Danish random audits for tax years 2008, 2010, 2012
Sample
520 HSBC households; 165 Panama Papers households; 1,422 amnesty taxpayers in Norway and 6,811 in Sweden; 18,985 randomly audited Danish taxpayers Scandinavian households appearing in offshore leaks or tax amnesties, ranked in the full population wealth distribution, combined with Danish random audits. source

Correlation of Brothers' Earnings and Intergenerational Transmission

Paul Bingley and Lorenzo Cappellari · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2019 · Individual

Decomposes the earnings correlation between Danish brothers into the part explained by fathers' earnings and the part due to other shared family and community factors. Intergenerational transmission accounts for a larger share of sibling similarity than simple father-son elasticities suggest, once permanent earnings and life-cycle bias are handled.

Data
Civil registration system / population register with parent-child links (BEF), Income Statistics Register (IND): annual pre-tax labour earnings from tax returns
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual pre-tax labour earnings (sum over all employments), ages 25–60
  • Family structure: father/first-son/second-son triads
  • Life-cycle: 3-year birth cohort groups and age
Period
1980–2014
Sample
303,231 men in 101,077 families; 5,488,445 earnings observations Danish fathers born 1935–1961 and their first and second sons (born 1959–1985) with the same mother, with at least five consecutive positive earnings observations. source

Born with a Silver Spoon? Danish Evidence on Wealth Inequality in Childhood

Simon Halphen Boserup, Wojciech Kopczuk and Claus Thustrup Kreiner · Economic Journal, 2018 · Individual

Third-party reported wealth records of children show that wealth inequality is as high in childhood as among adults, with top shares driven by transfers from parents and grandparents. Wealth at age 18 predicts wealth in adulthood, and intergenerational wealth correlations are present from early ages.

Data
Individual wealth data from tax return records (Danish Tax Agency SKAT, via Statistics Denmark), Population register with parent identifiers (BEF), Education and income registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: child wealth and wealth rank in adulthood (age 45)
  • Key regressor: childhood wealth (age 18) and parental wealth rank
  • Inferred inter vivos transfers and bequests (bunching at gift-tax allowance)
  • Portfolio components (deposits, securities, real estate, debt)
Period
1983–2011
Sample
Pooled cross-section: on average close to 650 thousand observations per one-year age group; panel regressions: 36,102 observations All individuals aged 1–40 in 2000–2011 with living parents (pooled cross-section), plus a panel of the 1965 birth cohort with both parents alive in 2011, followed 1983–2011. source

Wage Dispersion and Decentralization of Wage Bargaining

Christian M. Dahl, Daniel le Maire and Jakob R. Munch · Journal of Labor Economics, 2013 · Individual + firm

Exploits the shift in Denmark during the 1990s from centralised to firm-level wage bargaining. Workers covered by firm-level bargaining earn a wage premium, returns to skills are higher, and wage dispersion is greater than under sector-level bargaining.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), matched employer-employee data, Income register (tax files), Supplementary Pension Fund (ATP) contributions for hours worked, DA/LO bargaining-segment classification by NACE industry and DISCO occupation (Confederation of Danish Employers)
Key variables
  • Outcome: log hourly wage (labour income plus mandatory pension payments over hours)
  • Treatment: wage-setting system (standard-rate, minimum-wage, minimum-pay, firm-level bargaining)
  • Outcome: wage dispersion (quantile regressions) and returns to skills
  • Controls: gender, children, marriage, immigrant status, education, experience, plant size, industry-by-year dummies
  • Worker fixed effects; changes in wage-setting system over time
Period
1992–2001
Sample
about 1.14 million worker-year observations (1,137,367) 30% random sample of full-time private-sector workers aged 25–65 in 36 DA/LO bargaining segments, in plants with at least five employees. source

Unemployment Insurance, Wage Dynamics and Inequality Over the Life Cycle

Paul Bingley, Lorenzo Cappellari and Niels Westergård-Nielsen · Economic Journal, 2013 · Individual

Models permanent and transitory wage dynamics over the life cycle for Danish men, allowing dynamics to differ by unemployment insurance membership. Insurance is associated with lower wage inequality and less persistence of shocks, and the paper quantifies how much of life-cycle wage inequality UI members avoid relative to non-members.

Data
Administrative register data on the Danish labour force (Statistics Denmark): annual labour earnings and hours-derived hourly wages, UI fund membership contributions reported to tax authorities, Mandatory pension contributions (ATP) for year of labour market entry
Key variables
  • Outcome: log gross hourly wage (permanent and transitory components)
  • Key regressor: UI fund membership
  • Potential labour market experience
  • Birth cohort (1943–1973, 31 cohorts)
Period
1980–2003
Sample
roughly 810,000 individuals, about 12.5 million person-year observations Danish men aged 21–55, full-time private-sector employees, born 1943–1973, with at least five consecutive valid wage observations. source

Political economy

Electoral responses to immigration.

Refugee Migration and Electoral Outcomes

Christian Dustmann, Kristine Vasiljeva and Anna Piil Damm · Review of Economic Studies, 2019 · Individual

Using the quasi-random refugee dispersal policy, refugee allocation raised vote shares for anti-immigration parties in rural municipalities and lowered them in the largest urban municipalities. Effects are strongest where refugees are few relative to natives and where crime rises after allocation.

Data
Population register (BEF) to identify refugees and municipality of initial placement, Income, education, labour force and socio-economic registers for 1985 municipality characteristics, Statistics Denmark municipal and parliamentary election results, Danish National Election Survey (DNES) 1990–98
Key variables
  • Outcome: change in vote share of anti-immigration parties (Progress Party, Danish People's Party)
  • Treatment: cumulative share of allocated refugees in municipal population (1986 base)
  • Source of variation: Danish Spatial Dispersal Policy allocation of refugees 1986–98
  • Heterogeneity: urban vs rural municipality
  • Controls: 1985 municipality characteristics
Period
Refugee allocation 1986–1998; parliamentary elections 1990–98 and municipal elections 1989–97
Sample
275 municipalities (550 observations for parliamentary elections); 76,673 refugees allocated 1986–98 All 275 Danish municipalities observed across election cycles during the 1986–98 refugee dispersal policy. source

Macroeconomics and expectations

Disaggregated accounts, expectations and firm pricing built from Danish micro data.

Disaggregated Economic Accounts

Asger Lau Andersen, Kilian Huber, Niels Johannesen, Ludwig Straub and Emil Toft Vestergaard · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2026 · Individual + firm

Builds a system of national accounts broken down into bilateral money flows between region-by-industry consumer cells, producer cells, the government and the rest of the world, and implements it for Denmark using bank transaction data and registers. Spending is locally and urban biased, and fiscal transfers raise GDP more when targeted at cells whose spending reaches cells with high unemployment.

Data
Danske Bank geo-coded transaction-level data (about 20% of Danish adult consumers), 2018–2019, Government tax and income registers (labour compensation, mixed income, interest, dividends, transfers), Employment registers linking adults to industry of main employment and region, Danish national accounts (SNA positions, input-output tables), 2018, CrediWire producer-to-producer transactions (about 5 million), Firm-level export and import data for manufacturing firms
Key variables
  • Outcome: bilateral cell-to-cell flows (consumer spending, labour and profit income, taxes, transfers, intermediates trade)
  • Unit: consumer cells (industry of main employment x region of residence)
  • Unit: producer cells (industry x region of establishments)
  • Derived: domestic spending share, gravity, urban bias and spending intensity measures
Period
2018 (consumer spending 2018–2019)
Sample
approx. 2,700 consumer cells and approx. 2,600 producer cells; around 43 million entries All Danish adults and production establishments grouped into region-by-industry cells, with consumer spending measured from Danske Bank customers. source

Beliefs About the Economy Are Excessively Sensitive to Household-Level Shocks: Evidence from Linked Survey and Administrative Data

Dmitry Taubinsky, Luigi Butera, Matteo Saccarola and Chen Lian · Quarterly Journal of Economics, 2026 · Individual

Links Statistics Denmark's consumer expectations survey to the registers to show that people's inflation forecasts and backcasts move strongly, and negatively, with their own recent and expected income changes, far more than actual inflation does. The pattern departs from Bayesian rational expectations and is consistent with affect-cued recall, where bad personal events cue pessimistic recollections.

Data
Danish Consumer Expectations Survey (Statistics Denmark Omnibus), linked on CPR, Tax registers from SKAT: income, liquid assets, net wealth, taxes (IND, formue), Civil Registration System / Population register (CPR/BEF), Education register (UDDA), National Patient Register (LPR), emergency room visits 1994–2018
Key variables
  • Outcome: inflation forecasts and backcasts (qualitative and quantitative)
  • Explanatory: recent change in household log nominal income (t-1 vs t-2)
  • Explanatory: future household income change and expected household financial situation
  • Shock: family emergency room visit in the survey month
  • Benchmark: realized inflation
Period
2012–2019 (robustness from 2008; population registers from 1991)
Sample
35050 usable responses (from 55171 survey respondents aged 25–60) Danish Consumer Expectations Survey respondents aged 25–60 in 2012–2019 without substantial self-employment income and with complete answers, linked to administrative data. source

Credit Supply Shocks and Prices: Evidence from Danish Firms

Tobias Renkin and Gabriel Züllig · AEJ: Macroeconomics, 2024 · Firm

Combines loan-level data on Danish firm–bank relationships with survey producer prices and export unit values. Firms whose banks were exposed to the global financial crisis saw loans fall and rates rise, and they raised output prices by 3 to 5 percent to generate cash, with smaller increases where demand is more elastic.

Data
Bank account-level loan and deposit reports to the Danish Tax Authority (firm part), Bank balance sheets from the Danish FSA (Finanstilsynet) and Danmarks Nationalbank monetary and financial statistics, Producer Price Index survey microdata, Customs export data for export unit values, Firm register, Accounting statistics (regnskabsstatistik) and VAT sales data
Key variables
  • Treatment: firm exposure to banks with high pre-crisis loan-to-deposit ratios
  • Outcome: producer prices (PPI) and export unit values
  • Intermediate outcome: firm bank loans and average interest rate
  • Heterogeneity: product demand elasticity
Period
2005–2010
Sample
1,753 firms (213 linked to the PPI survey, 1,176 to export data) Danish manufacturing firms active 2005–2010 with at least ten employees and an active bank lending relationship in 2007. source

Consumption Heterogeneity: Micro Drivers and Macro Implications

Edmund Crawley and Andreas Kuchler · AEJ: Macroeconomics, 2023 · Individual

Estimates marginal propensities to consume across Danish households with a method that corrects biases in earlier register-based estimates. Liquid wealth is the dominant predictor of MPCs, and the estimated heterogeneity is used to quantify monetary policy transmission in Denmark and the United States.

Data
Tax-authority income records (household after-tax and transfer income), Tax-reported wealth data: bank deposits, loans, bond and stock holdings, mortgages (year-end balances), Private pension contribution records, Civil Registration System (CPR) family/household links, Mortgage reset data (amount resetting over the following year)
Key variables
  • Outcome: imputed household expenditure (income minus pension contributions minus change in non-housing net worth)
  • Shock: unexpected (residual) change in after-tax household income of the head
  • Heterogeneity: liquid wealth (bank deposits) quintiles
  • Heterogeneity: Unhedged Interest Rate Exposure (URE) and Net Nominal Position (NNP)
Period
2003–2015
Sample
7.7 million observations from 2004 to 2015 Danish households headed by an individual aged 30–55 in 2008, excluding business owners, households with housing transactions, and top/bottom 1% of income and income changes. source

Housing

Home ownership and labour market behaviour.

Spending Response to a Predictable Increase in Mortgage Repayments: Evidence from Expiring Interest-Only Loans

Henrik Yde Andersen, Stine Ludvig Bech and Alessia De Stefani · Review of Economics and Statistics, 2024 · Individual

Matches the universe of Danish mortgages to borrower registers and studies the fully anticipated start of amortisation on interest-only loans. When instalments rise by 9 percent of income, consumption falls by 3 percent of income and stays down, with the failure to smooth concentrated among highly leveraged borrowers likely to be refused a new interest-only loan.

Data
Mortgage registry: loan-level data on the universe of Danish mortgages reported by mortgage institutions to Danmarks Nationalbank (from 2009), Income and wealth registers (IND/FORMUE): household income, bank deposits, stocks, bonds, pensions, debts, Population/household registers (BEF): family composition, residence, Imputed household consumption from income and wealth registers
Key variables
  • Treatment: expiry of the interest-only period (start of amortisation)
  • Outcome: imputed household consumption relative to income
  • Installment increase relative to income
  • Heterogeneity: loan-to-value ratio and liquid savings
Period
Mortgage registry 2009–2018; IO loans originated 2003–2007 expiring 2013–2017
Sample
46,025 household-year observations (baseline regressions); 8,813 households beginning amortization Danish households with an interest-only mortgage originated 2003–2007 whose IO period expires in 2013–2017 and who keep the original loan, excluding self-employed and house buyers/sellers. source

Reference Dependence in the Housing Market

Steffen Andersen, Cristian Badarinza, Lu Liu, Julie Marx and Tarun Ramadorai · American Economic Review, 2022 · Individual

Combines Danish housing listings and transactions with register data on owners to estimate a structural model of listing and sale decisions with loss aversion and financial constraints. Households derive utility from gains over the original purchase price and weigh losses about 2.5 times more than gains, which helps explain the co-movement of house prices and turnover.

Data
Property ownership and transaction register from SKAT and the Building and Housing Register (BBR), 1992–2016, Tax-assessed property values (SKAT), Property listings data from RealView, 2008–2016, Loan-level mortgage data from Danmarks Nationalbank, 2009–2016, Civil Registration System (CPR/BEF) for demographics and household links, Income and wealth records from SKAT (IND/formue)
Key variables
  • Outcome: listing decision and listing premium relative to hedonic value
  • Main regressor: potential gain or loss relative to original purchase price (reference point)
  • Potential home equity relative to down-payment constraint
  • Expected house value from hedonic pricing model
  • Structural parameter: loss aversion
Period
2009–2016 (transactions from 1992)
Sample
214,103 listings (191,507 unique households, 178,933 unique properties); housing stock of 5,538,052 observations of 807,345 properties Listings of Danish owner-occupied housing between 2009 and 2016, both sold and retracted, matched to mortgages and household finances. source

Do People Respond to the Mortgage Interest Deduction? Quasi-Experimental Evidence from Denmark

Jonathan Gruber, Amalie Sofie Jensen and Henrik Kleven · AEJ: Economic Policy, 2021 · Individual

A major reform that scaled back the mortgage interest deduction for middle- and high-income households had a precisely estimated zero effect on home ownership. It did reduce the size and price of houses bought, lowered household indebtedness and pushed down equilibrium house prices.

Data
Income tax records (incomes, taxes, deductible interest expenses), Housing register (homeownership, property characteristics, home size), Tax authority property value appraisals (for imputed rent and wealth tax), Population registers with spouse/cohabitant and child links
Key variables
  • Policy: 1987 tax reform reducing the value of the mortgage interest deduction for top-rate taxpayers
  • Treatment: top-bracket taxpayers vs. below-top/middle/bottom groups (difference-in-differences)
  • Outcome: homeownership
  • Outcome: home size (square metres) and home value
  • Outcome: total deductible interest expenses (debt)
  • Elasticity with respect to the net-of-tax rate (1−τ)
Period
1980–2011 (main panel 1980–1996)
Sample
about 40 million individual-year observations; 34,440,869 observations in the main homeownership regression (below-top controls) Semi-balanced panel of Danish individuals aged 20–80 observed in every year 1980–1996, excluding children living with parents. source

Home Prices, Fertility, and Early-Life Health Outcomes

N. Meltem Daysal, Michael Lovenheim, Nikolaj Siersbæk and David N. Wasser · Journal of Public Economics, 2021 · Individual

For Danish homeowners aged 20 to 44, a 100,000 kroner rise in home prices raises the probability of giving birth by about 2.35 percent. Birth outcomes improve too, but mostly because of who gives birth, and there is no effect on children's health in the first five years.

Data
Medical Birth Register (MFR), National Patient Register (LPR), State's Sales and Valuation Registry (public property valuations and sales prices), Population register (BEF) and other registers for demographics, education, household income and partner status
Key variables
  • Treatment: once-lagged one-year change in estimated home value (public valuation adjusted by local sale-price ratio)
  • Outcome: indicator for giving birth
  • Outcomes: birth weight, low birth weight, very low birth weight, prematurity
  • Outcomes: hospital admissions, ER visits and days hospitalised in first year and ages 1–5
  • Comparison group: renters
Period
1992–2011 (fertility analysis 1995–2006)
Sample
1,105,559 observations on 198,435 women (125,903 births) Women aged 20–44 who became first-time homeowners between 1993 and 2004, purchasing between ages 18 and 42. source

Gender Differences in Negotiation: Evidence from Real Estate Transactions

Steffen Andersen, Julie Marx, Kasper Meisner Nielsen and Lise Vesterlund · Economic Journal, 2021 · Individual

Men appear to secure better prices than women in Danish real estate transactions, but the gap shrinks with better controls for property value and disappears in repeat-sales and inherited-property samples. The price differences reflect men and women demanding different properties, not differences in negotiation.

Data
Property transactions from the Danish Tax and Customs Administration (SKAT), Housing register (BBR), Civil Registration System (CPR), Income and tax-assessed property values from SKAT, Education registers (Ministry of Education), Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA) employment status, Cause-of-Death Register (Danish National Board of Health)
Key variables
  • Outcome: realised transaction price of house or apartment
  • Key regressor: gender of single buyer/seller
  • Controls: tax-assessed property value, BBR property characteristics, property fixed effects (repeated sales)
  • Death sales: properties inherited by an only child from a deceased parent
Period
1994–2013
Sample
337,685 real estate transactions (97,216 in repeated-sales sample; 13,953 inherited houses and apartments in death-sales sample) Arm's-length transactions of Danish houses and apartments involving single female or single male buyers and sellers, 1994–2013. source

Housing Wealth and Consumption: A Micro Panel Study

Martin Browning, Mette Gørtz and Søren Leth-Petersen · Economic Journal, 2013 · Individual

Uses register-based imputed consumption and house price changes over the 1987 to 1996 boom and bust to test whether housing wealth drives spending. For most households consumption does not respond to unanticipated house price changes; the response is confined to young households, consistent with a collateral rather than a wealth effect.

Data
Administrative income and wealth (tax) registers for a 10% random sample of the Danish population aged 16+, House price / housing wealth information from registers
Key variables
  • Outcome: imputed household total expenditure (disposable income minus change in net wealth)
  • Key regressor: unexpected changes in house prices / housing wealth
  • Heterogeneity: age, owners vs renters, credit constraints (1992–93 mortgage reform allowing home-equity borrowing)
  • Disposable household income
Period
1987–1996
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Married or cohabiting stable couples from a 10% random sample of the Danish population aged 16+, who were either homeowners or renters throughout the period. source

Are Homeowners Really More Unemployed?

Jakob Roland Munch, Michael Rosholm and Michael Svarer · Economic Journal, 2006 · Individual

Tests the Oswald hypothesis that home ownership raises unemployment. Danish homeowners are less geographically mobile, but they find local jobs faster, so their unemployment spells are shorter overall. Ownership does not raise aggregate unemployment.

Data
Administrative register data on unemployment spells for a 2.5% random sample of the Danish population, Regional unemployment measure from the Institute for Local Government Studies (1987–2000)
Key variables
  • Outcome: unemployment duration in weeks
  • Outcome: exit destination (new job locally, job in another commuting area, other states)
  • Key regressor: home ownership indicator
  • Controls: age, gender, children, household type, immigrant status, education, UI fund membership and replacement rate
Period
1997–2000
Sample
18,992 persons with 52,738 unemployment spells Flow sample of all unemployment spells in 1997–2000 for individuals aged 19–66 in a 2.5% random sample of the Danish population. source

Rent Control and Unemployment Duration

Michael Svarer, Michael Rosholm and Jakob Roland Munch · Journal of Public Economics, 2005 · Individual

Uses Danish register data on unemployment spells and housing to study whether living in rent-controlled housing affects job finding. Unemployed workers in rent-controlled dwellings have lower exit rates from unemployment, consistent with rent control reducing geographic mobility.

Data
10% random sample of the Danish adult population from administrative registers (Statistics Denmark), Housing unit characteristics for dwellings occupied by the sample, Weekly labour market event histories, Danish Tax Authorities 1999 hedonic model for owner-occupied housing (to predict uncontrolled rent)
Key variables
  • Outcome: unemployment duration in weeks (exit to job in local vs national labour market)
  • Explanatory: rent control benefit decile (predicted uncontrolled rent minus actual rent), time-varying
  • Mobility: job finding with move to another of 51 commuting areas
  • Tenure type: private rental housing vs other
Period
1997–2000
Sample
14,645 persons contributing 31,966 unemployment spells Inflow sample of unemployment spells 1997–2000 from a 10% random sample of the Danish adult population (IZA DP 842 working-paper version). source

Measurement and methods

How to build variables, such as consumption, from register data.

Subjective Earnings Risk

Andrew Caplin, Victoria Gregory, Eungik Lee, Søren Leth-Petersen and Johan Sæverud · Review of Economic Studies, 2026 · Individual

Introduces a survey instrument for earnings risk that allows for quitting and being fired, fielded to Danish workers and linked to their register earnings histories. Job transitions drive subjective risk, which is many times smaller than traditional register-based estimates even after conditioning on demographics and job history, and helps explain why many hold few liquid assets.

Data
Copenhagen Life Panel (CLP) survey, January 2021 wave, linked on CPR number, Income-tax register earnings (IND), Monthly employer-employee match and earnings data (BFL/eIndkomst), Demographic, education, household composition and household wealth registers
Key variables
  • Survey: subjective probabilities of staying, quitting and being laid off
  • Survey: subjective earnings distributions conditional on each job transition
  • Benchmark: realized job transitions, time out of work and earnings growth in 2021 (administrative)
  • Weights: population weights from a survey-participation model on 2020 administrative data
Period
Survey January 2021; administrative data through 2022
Sample
10,942 employed respondents (gross sample 14,683 respondents aged 20–65) Copenhagen Life Panel respondents aged 20–65 who were employed at the time of the January 2021 survey. source

Instrumental Variables with Unordered Treatments: Theory and Evidence from Returns to Fields of Study

Eskil Heinesen, Christian Hvid, Lars Kirkebøen, Edwin Leuven and Magne Mogstad · Journal of Labor Economics, 2026 · Individual

Shows that the assumptions needed to identify returns to unordered treatments, such as fields of study, have testable implications, and characterises the bias when they fail. Using admission cut-offs and applicants' ranked choices in Denmark and Norway, payoffs to fields are strongly correlated across the two countries once fields violating the assumptions are dropped.

Data
Danish administrative data on all applications to post-secondary education 1994–2002 (coordinated admission system), Population register and other registers (background, completed field of study, annual earnings), Norwegian post-secondary application data 1998–2004 linked to registers (comparison country)
Key variables
  • Outcome: annual earnings eight years after application (wages, self-employment and income-replacing transfers)
  • Treatment: completed field of study (nine broad fields)
  • Instrument: application score just above vs below the admission cutoff of the preferred field
  • Preferred and next-best field from applicants' rankings
Period
Applications 1994–2002 (Denmark), outcomes eight years after application
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked First-time applicants to Danish post-secondary education without a prior post-secondary degree who applied to at least two broad fields, with the preferred field having an admission cutoff, and who completed a field within eight years (paralleled with Norwegian applicants). source

Identifying Behavioral Responses to Tax Reforms: New Insights and a New Approach

Katrine Jakobsen and Jakob Egholt Søgaard · Journal of Public Economics, 2022 · Individual

Shows that standard estimates of taxable income elasticities from tax reforms are biased by mean reversion and diverging income trends across the distribution, and proposes an approach that exploits reform timing to separate behavioural responses from these trends, applied to Danish tax reforms.

Data
Danish administrative registers for the full population since 1980 (labour market history, education, earnings, demographics), Third-party reported income tax records, Simulated marginal tax rates from an extended Danish tax simulator (Kleven and Schultz 2014 model)
Key variables
  • Outcome: changes in taxable income (broad, personal, labour income) over four-year periods
  • Treatment: predicted change in log net-of-tax rate from the 2004 and 2009–10 Danish tax reforms
  • Income-bin dummies capturing trend differentials across the income distribution
  • Validation: income trends in parts of the distribution unaffected by the reform
Period
Data since 1980; main analyses 1999–2012 (2004 reform: 1999–2003 pre-period, 2003–2007 reform period; 2009–10 reform: 2004–2008 pre-period, 2008–2012 reform period); 1987 and 1994 reforms in appendix
Sample
over 2,000,000 individuals per period (2,240,332 and 2,295,922 for the 2004 reform pre-reform and reform periods; 2,097,862 and 2,240,833 for the 2009–10 reform) Individuals present in the tax data with positive income over each four-year period and initial personal income above DKK 200,000 (2004 reform) or DKK 250,000 (2009–10 reform), about half the Danish adult population. source

Equilibrium Trade in Automobiles

Kenneth Gillingham, Fedor Iskhakov, Anders Munk-Nielsen, John Rust and Bertel Schjerning · Journal of Political Economy, 2022 · Individual

Develops and estimates a dynamic equilibrium model of the market for new and used cars with heterogeneous households on the universe of Danish car ownership records, which record every vehicle's owner, age and scrappage. The model is used to evaluate Denmark's very high registration taxes and fuel taxes, showing how tax changes propagate through used-car prices and the age distribution of the fleet.

Data
Car ownership register covering all privately owned cars (Statistics Denmark extract), Odometer readings from mandatory vehicle inspections (driving), Demographic and income registers for all Danish households, Tax deduction for commuting distance (work distance), Fuel prices from eof.dk
Key variables
  • Outcome: car ownership transitions (keep, trade, buy new, scrap)
  • Car types: four classes by weight and fuel efficiency, 25 age groups
  • Household types: couple/single, high/low income, high/low work distance
  • Outcome: kilometres driven
  • Policy variable: new car registration tax and fuel tax
Period
1996–2008 (estimation 1997–2008)
Sample
nearly 39 million observations on car ownership transitions All Danish households and all cars owned by private individuals, 1996–2008. source

Measurement Error in Income and Schooling and the Bias of Linear Estimators

Paul Bingley and Alessandro Martinello · Journal of Labor Economics, 2017 · Individual

Links survey reports of income and schooling for Danish respondents to the same variables in the registers. Measurement errors in survey income and schooling are correlated and non-classical, so standard corrections fail, and the paper quantifies the resulting bias in linear estimators such as returns to schooling.

Data
Survey of Health, Ageing and Retirement in Europe (SHARE), Danish respondents, Danish administrative income registers, Danish administrative education registers
Key variables
  • Variable validated: survey-reported annual income vs register income
  • Variable validated: survey-reported schooling vs register schooling
  • Estimand: measurement error bias in OLS and IV estimators of returns to schooling
Period
Not stated in the sources checked
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Danish SHARE respondents linked to administrative registers to validate survey income and schooling. source (abstract)

Imputing Consumption from Income and Wealth Information

Martin Browning and Søren Leth-Petersen · Economic Journal, 2003 · Individual

Tests whether consumption can be imputed from register data as income minus the change in wealth, by comparing the imputation with expenditure survey responses for the same households. The imputation works well for most households but is noisy for those with housing transactions or large stock holdings.

Data
Danish Expenditure Survey 1994–96, Administrative income and wealth (tax) records: disposable income and year-end asset holdings
Key variables
  • Outcome: survey-reported total household expenditure
  • Imputed consumption: disposable income minus change in wealth
  • Year-end holdings of different assets
Period
1994–1996 survey years, linked to register years around the survey year
Sample
Not stated in the sources checked Households in the 1994–96 Danish Expenditure Survey linked to their administrative income and wealth records. source (abstract)

Innovation

Product innovation and growth at the firm level.

Tapping into Talent: Coupling Education and Innovation Policies for Economic Growth

Ufuk Akcigit, Jeremy Pearce and Marta Prato · Review of Economic Studies, 2024 · Individual

Builds an endogenous growth model with heterogeneous talent, financial resources and career choice, and links it to Danish micro data on who obtains a PhD and who becomes an inventor. R&D subsidies work better when combined with higher education subsidies that let talented but poor youth pursue research, and education policy matters most in unequal societies.

Data
Integrated Database for Labour Market Research (IDA), Firm-linked IDA (FIDA), PhD enrolment register (PHD), Military conscription IQ test (Boerge Prien Proeve), European Patent Office patents linked to IDA, R&D funding data (Innovation Danmark) and Ministry of Education PhD policy data
Key variables
  • Outcome: PhD enrolment/attainment
  • Outcome: patent filing (inventor status)
  • Talent proxy: military IQ test score
  • Background: parental income
  • Policy: 2002 expansion of PhD slots and R&D subsidies
Period
2001–2013 (background data from 1980)
Sample
approximately 32 million employer-employee observations, 10,000 inventors and 37,000 unique PhDs; approximately 500,000 males with IQ data Danish population in IDA linked to PhD, IQ test and patent data. source

Productivity Spillovers Across Firms through Worker Mobility

Andrey Stoyanov and Nikolay Zubanov · AEJ: Applied Economics, 2012 · Individual + firm

Tracks firm-to-firm worker moves in Danish manufacturing. Firms that hire workers from more productive firms see productivity gains of about 0.35 percent a year for an average firm, rising with the education, tenure and skill of the new hires and persisting for several years, consistent with knowledge spillovers carried by workers.

Data
Matched employer-employee data from Statistics Denmark (worker records from 1983, ages 15–65), Manufacturing firm-level accounts data with sales, value added, materials, capital and investment, 1995–2007
Key variables
  • Outcome: receiving firm's log value added per worker normalised by 4-digit industry-year mean
  • Treatment: productivity gap between sending and receiving firms of newly hired workers, weighted by hires' employment share
  • Positive and negative productivity gaps (hires from more vs. less productive firms)
  • Worker characteristics: education, occupation/skill level, experience, wage
  • Firm characteristics: size, industry
Period
1995–2007
Sample
173,929 firm-year observations (about 5.8 million worker-year observations, 668,034 job changers); benchmark regressions use 74,507 and 61,112 firm-year observations All Danish manufacturing firms and their employees with positive annual salary, 1995–2007. source

An Empirical Model of Growth Through Product Innovation

Rasmus Lentz and Dale T. Mortensen · Econometrica, 2008 · Firm

Estimates a Schumpeterian model of firm growth through product innovation on a Danish firm panel. Reallocation of labour from less to more innovative firms accounts for roughly half of aggregate productivity growth, with the remainder coming from innovation within firms.

Data
Danish Business Statistics Register: annual panel of privately owned firms (value added, total wage bill, full-time equivalent employment)
Key variables
  • Firm value added (Y)
  • Total wage bill (W) used to build quality-adjusted employment
  • Full-time equivalent employment (N)
  • Labor productivity: value added per worker and per quality-adjusted worker
  • Moments: firm growth rates including exit, and productivity growth decomposition (within, between, cross, exit)
Period
1992–1997
Sample
unbalanced panel of 4,872 firms All privately owned Danish firms existing in 1992 with more than 20 workers, followed through 1997, excluding later entrants. source
